     Chapter 2 (FINAL DRAFT—NOT FOR IMPLEMENTATION)

138       “Hand Shake Con” in Provide Comfort

139

140       [Regarding relationships with other multinational contingents, t]he Chairman of the

141       Joint Chiefs of Staff asked me…“The lines in your command chart, the command re-

142       lationships, what are they? OPCON? TACON? Command?” “Sir, we don’t ask, be-

143       cause no one can sign up to any of that stuff.” “Well, how do you do business?”

144       “Hand Shake Con. That’s it.” No memoranda of agreement. No memoranda of un-

145       derstanding…the relationships are worked out on the scene, and they aren’t pretty.

146       And you don’t really want to try to capture them…distill them, and say as you go off in

147       the future, you’re going to have this sort of command relationship…it is Hand Shake

148       Con and that’s the way it works. It is consultative. It is behind-the-scene.

149                                                                            General Anthony Zinni, 1994

150 KEY COUNTERINSURGENCY PARTICIPANTS AND THEIR LIKELY
151 ROLES

152       2-14. Likely participants in COIN operations include the following:

153       z U.S. military forces.

154       z Multinational forces.

155       z U.S. government agencies.

156       z Nongovernmental organizations.

157       z International organizations.

158       z Multinational corporations and contractors.

159       z HN civil authorities.

160 U.S. MILITARY FORCES

161       2-15. The role of military forces in COIN operations is extensive. As one of the most demanding and com-

162       plex forms of warfare, COIN draws heavily on the broad range of capabilities of the joint force. Military

163       forces must be prepared to conduct a different mix of offensive, defensive and stability operations from

164       that expected in major combat operations. Air, land, and maritime elements all contribute to successful op-

165       erations and to the vital effort to cut off and isolate insurgents from the populations they seek to control.

166       Nonetheless, the Army and Marine Corps usually furnish the principal U.S. military contributions to

167       COIN. Within the land forces, special operations forces (SOF) are particularly valuable to COIN due to

168       their specialized capabilities: civil affairs, psychological operations, intelligence, language skills, and re-

169       gional-specific knowledge. SOF can also provide very light, agile, high-capability teams that can operate

170       discreetly in local communities.

171       2-16. U.S. military forces are vastly capable. Designed predominantly for conventional warfare, they none-

172       theless have the essential components to successfully prosecute COIN. The most important assets in COIN

173       are disciplined Soldiers and Marines with adaptive, self-aware, and intelligent leaders. There are also ele-

174       ments of the military forces that are particularly relevant to common COIN challenges. For example, COIN

175       often requires dismounted infantry, human intelligence, language specialists, military police, civil affairs,

176       engineers, medical units, logistical support, legal affairs, and contracting elements. All of these elements

177       are found in the Army; most are be found in the Marine Corps as well. To a limited degree, they are also

178       found in the Air Force and Navy.

179       2-17. U.S. forces help HN military, paramilitary, and police forces conduct COIN operations, including

180       area security and local security operations. U.S. forces advise and assist in finding, dispersing, capturing,

181       and defeating the insurgent force, while emphasizing the training of HN forces to perform essential defense

182       functions. These are the central tasks of foreign internal defense, which until recently was primarily the re-

     2-4                                    FM 3-24/FMFM 3-24                  16 June 2006 (FINAL DRAFT)


---

     Unity of Effort: Integrating Civilian and Military Activities (FINAL DRAFT—NOT FOR IMPLEMENTATION)

183  sponsibility of SOF. The current and more extensive national security demands for such efforts require that

184  all Services be prepared to contribute to the establishment and training of local security forces.

185  2-18. Land combat forces conduct full spectrum operations to disrupt or destroy insurgent military capa-

186  bilities. Land forces use offensive combat operations to disrupt insurgent efforts to establish base areas and

187  consolidate their forces. They conduct defensive operations to provide area and local security. They con-

188  duct stability operations to thwart insurgent efforts to control or disrupt people’s lives and routine activi-

189  ties. In all applications of combat power, U.S. forces first ensure that likely costs do not outweigh or un-

190  dermine other more important COIN efforts.

191  2-19. Most valuable to long-term success in winning the support of the population are the contributions

192  land forces can make by conducting stability operations. A stability operation is an operation to establish,

193  preserve, and exploit security and control over areas, populations, and resources (FM 3-0). Forces engaged

194  in stability operations establish, safeguard, or restore basic civil services. They act directly and in support

195  of governmental agencies. Success in stability operations enables the local population and government

196  agencies of the host nation to resume or develop the capabilities needed to conduct COIN operations and

197  create the conditions that will permit U.S. military forces to disengage.

198  2-20. Military forces can also use their capabilities to enable the efforts of nonmilitary participants. Logis-

199  tics, transportation, equipment, personnel, and other assets can support interagency partners and other civil-

200  ian organizations.

201 MULTINATIONAL MILITARY FORCES

202  2-21. U.S. military forces rarely operate alone. They normally function as part of a multinational force. In

203  a COIN operation, U.S. forces usually operate with the security forces of the local population or host na-

204  tion. Few multinational military forces have the comprehensive capabilities possessed by the United States,

205  but many possess significant similar capabilities that can contribute to COIN. Many other countries’ mili-

206  tary forces bring cultural backgrounds, historical experiences, and other capabilities that can be particularly

207  valuable to COIN efforts.

208  2-22. However, nations join coalitions for varied policy aims. Although missions may be ostensibly simi-

209  lar, rules of engagement, home-country policies, and sensitivities will differ among multinational partners.

210  U.S. military leaders require a strong cultural and political awareness of HN and other multinational mili-

211  tary partners.

212 NONMILITARY COUNTERINSURGENCY PARTICIPANTS

213  2-23. Many nonmilitary organizations may be engaged in supporting a host nation as it confronts an insur-

214  gency. Some of these organizations are discussed below. JP 3-08 contains a detailed description of all or-

215  ganizations listed here as well as many others.

216 U.S. Government Organizations

217  2-24. Commanders must be familiar with other U.S. government organizations and aware of the capabili-

218  ties they can provide for COIN. During planning, all forces should determine which organizations are sup-

219  porting their function or operating in their AO. Commanders and civilian leaders of U.S. government or-

220  ganizations should collaboratively plan and coordinate actions to avoid conflict or duplication of effort.

221  Within the U.S. government, key organizations include—

222  z Department of State.

223  z U.S. Agency for International Development (USAID).

224  z Central Intelligence Agency.

225  z Department of Justice.

226  z Department of the Treasury.

227  z Department of Homeland Security.

228  z Department of Agriculture.

     16 June 2006 (FINAL DRAFT)      FM3-24/FMFM 3-24                                                    2-5


---

     Chapter 2 (FINAL DRAFT—NOT FOR IMPLEMENTATION)

229       z Department of Commerce.

230       z Department of Transportation.

231       z The U.S. Coast Guard (under Department of Homeland Security).

232       z The Federal Bureau of Investigation (under Department of Justice).

233       z Immigration Customs Enforcement (under Department of Homeland Security).

234 Nongovernmental Organizations

235       2-25. Joint doctrine defines a nongovernmental organization as a private, self-governing, not-for-profit or-

236       ganization dedicated to alleviating human suffering; and/or promoting education, health care, economic

237       development, environmental protection, human rights and conflict resolution; and/or encouraging the es-

238       tablishment of democratic institutions and civil society (JP 1-02). There are several thousand NGOs of

239       many different types. Their activities are governed by their organizing charters and the motivations of their

240       members. Some NGOs receive at least part of their funding from national governments or international

241       governmental organizations and may become implementing partners in accordance with specific grants or

242       contracts. (For example, USAID provides some NGO funding.) In these cases, the funding organization of-

243       ten gains oversight and authority over how the funds are used.

244       2-26. Some NGOs maintain strict independence from governments and other belligerents in a conflict and

245       do not want to be seen directly associating with military forces. Gaining their support and coordinating op-

246       erations with them can be difficult. While establishing basic awareness of these groups and their activities

247       may be the most commanders can achieve, it is important to note the role of NGOs in resolving an insur-

248       gency. Many of these NGOs arrive before military forces and remain long afterwards. They can support

249       lasting stability. To the greatest extent possible, the military should complement and not override their ca-

250       pabilities. Building a complementary and trust-based relationship is vital. Examples of NGOs include—

251       z International Committee of the Red Cross.

252       z World Vision.

253       z Médecins sans Frontières (Doctors Without Borders).

254       z Cooperative for Assistance and Relief Everywhere (CARE).

255       z Oxford Committee for Famine Relief (OXFAM).

256       z Save the Children.

257       z Mercy Corps.

258       z Academy for Educational Development.

259 International Organizations

260       2-27. The most notable international organization is the United Nations (UN). Regional organizations like

261       the Organization of American States and the European Union may be involved in some COIN operations.

262       The UN in particular has many subordinate or affiliated agencies that are active around the world. Depend-

263       ing on the nature of the situation and the needs of the country involved, any of several UN organizations

264       may be present, such as the following:

265       z Office of the Chief of Humanitarian Affairs.

266       z Department of Peacekeeping Operations.

267       z World Food Program.

268       z UN Refugee Agency (known by the acronym for its director, the UN High Commissioner for

269       Refugees—UNHCR).

270       z UN High Commissioner for Human Rights.

271       z UN Development Program.

272 Multinational Corporations and Contractors

273       2-28. Multinational corporations often engage in reconstruction, economic development, and governance

274       activities. At a minimum, commanders should know which companies are present in their AO and where

     2-6                                          FM 3-24/FMFM 3-24        16 June 2006 (FINAL DRAFT)


---

     Unity of Effort: Integrating Civilian and Military Activities (FINAL DRAFT—NOT FOR IMPLEMENTATION)

275  those companies are conducting business. Such information can prevent fratricide or destruction of private

276  property.

277  2-29. Recently, private contractors from firms providing military-related services have become more

278  prominent in theaters of operations. This category includes armed contractors providing an array of secu-

279  rity services to the U.S. government, NGOs, and private businesses. There are also many businesses that

280  market expertise in areas related to supporting governance, economics, education, and other aspects of civil

281  society. Providing capabilities similar to some NGOs, these firms often attain contracts through govern-

282  ment agencies.

283  2-30. When contractors or other businesses are being paid to support U.S. military or other government

284  agencies, the principle of unity of command should apply. Commanders should be able to influence con-

285  tractors’ performance through U.S. government contract supervisors. When under contract to the United

286  States, many such contractors should behave as an extension of the organizations or agencies for which

287  they work. Commanders should identify contractors operating in their AO and determine the nature of their

288  contract, existing accountability mechanisms, and appropriate coordination relationships.

289 Host-nation Civil Authorities

290  2-31. Sovereignty issues are among the most difficult for commanders conducting COIN operations, both

291  in regard to forces contributed by other nations and by the host nation. Often, commanders are required to

292  lead through coordination, communication, and consensus, in addition to traditional command practices.

293  Political sensitivities must be acknowledged. Commanders and subordinates often act as diplomats as well

294  as warriors. Within military units, legal officers and their staffs are particularly valuable for clarifying legal

295  arrangements with the host nation. To avoid adverse effects on operations, commanders should address all

296  sovereignty issues through the chain of command to the U.S. Ambassador. As much as possible, sover-

297  eignty issues should be addressed before executing operations. Examples of key sovereignty issues include

298  the following:

299  z Collecting and sharing information.

300  z Basing.

301  z Overflight rights.

302  z Aerial ports of debarkation.

303  z Seaports of debarkation.

304  z Location and access.

305  z Railheads.

306  z Border crossings.

307  z Force protection.

308  z Jurisdiction over members of the U.S. and multinational forces.

309  z Operations in the territorial sea and internal waters.

310  2-32. Commanders should create coordinating mechanisms, such as committees or liaison elements, to fa-

311  cilitate cooperation and build trust with HN authorities. Military or nonmilitary representatives of the host

312  nation should have leading roles in such mechanisms. These organizations facilitate operations by reducing

313  sensitivities and misunderstandings while removing impediments. Sovereignty issues can be formally re-

314  solved with the host nation through the development of appropriate technical agreements to augment exist-

315  ing or recently developed status of forces agreements. In many cases, security assistance organizations,

316  NGOs, and international organizations have detailed local knowledge and reservoirs of good will that can

317  facilitate the establishment of a positive and constructive relationship with the host nation.

318  2-33. Coordination and support should exist down to local levels (such as villages and neighborhoods).

319  Soldiers and Marines should be aware of the political and societal structures in their AOs. Political struc-

320  tures usually have designated leaders responsible to the government and people. However, the societal

321  structure may include informal leaders who operate outside the political structure. These leaders may be

322  economic (such as businessmen), theological (such as clerics and lay leaders), informational (such as news-

323  paper publishers or journalists), or family-based (such as elders or patriarchs). Some societal leaders may

     16 June 2006 (FINAL DRAFT)      FM3-24/FMFM 3-24                                                2-7


---

     Chapter 2 (FINAL DRAFT—NOT FOR IMPLEMENTATION)

324       emerge due to charisma or other intangible influences. Commanders should identify the key leaders and the

325       manner in which they are likely to influence COIN efforts.

326 KEY RESPONSIBILITIES IN COUNTERINSURGENCY

327       2-34. David Galula wisely notes, “To confine soldiers to purely military functions while urgent and vital

328       tasks have to be done, and nobody else is available to undertake them, would be senseless. The soldier

329       must then be prepared to become…a social worker, a civil engineer, a schoolteacher, a nurse, a boy scout.

330       But only for as long as he cannot be replaced, for it is better to entrust civilian tasks to civilians.”

331 PREFERRED DIVISION OF LABOR

332       2-35. The preference in COIN is always to have civilians carry out civilian tasks. Civilian agencies or in-

333       dividuals with the greatest expertise for a given task should perform it—with special preference for legiti-

334       mate local civil authorities. Although there are many U.S. and other international civilian agencies that

335       possess greater expertise than military forces for meeting the fundamental needs of a population under as-

336       sault, the ability of such agencies to deploy to foreign countries in sustainable numbers and with ready ac-

337       cess to necessary resources is usually limited. The degree of violence in the AO also affects the ability of

338       civilian agencies to operate. The more violent the environment, the more difficult it is for civilians to oper-

339       ate effectively. Hence, the preferred or ideal division of labor is frequently unattainable. The more violent

340       the insurgency, the more unrealistic is this preferred division of labor.

341 REALISTIC DIVISION OF LABOR

342       2-36. By default, U.S. and other military forces often possess the only readily available capability to meet

343       many of the fundamental needs of local populations. Human decency and the law of war require land

344       forces to assist populations in their AOs. Leaders at all levels prepare to address civilian needs. Leaders

345       identify people in their units with regional expertise, interagency know-how, civil-military competence,

346       and other critical skills that can usefully support a local population and HN government. Useful skill sets

347       may include the following:

348       z Knowledge, cultural understanding, and appreciation of the host nation and its region.

349       z Functional skills for interagency and HN coordination (for example, liaison, negotiation, and

350       appropriate social or political relationships).

351       z Language skills enabling more effective coordination with the host nation, NGOs, and multina-

352       tional partners.

353       z Knowledge of the civil foundations for infrastructure, economy, governance, or other lines of

354       operation being pursued as part of the COIN effort.

355       2-37. U.S. government and international agencies rarely have the resources and capabilities needed to ad-

356       dress all tasks required in a COIN environment. Successfully accomplishing a COIN mission requires lead-

357       ers who are adaptive in their thinking and understand that they must be prepared to address the required

358       tasks with available resources. They must understand that the overall purpose is always guided by the fact

359       that long-term security does not come solely or even primarily from a gun; it comes from an integrated and

360       balanced application of efforts along all lines of operations, with the goal of supporting the local popula-

361       tion and achieving legitimacy for the HN government.

362 TRANSITIONS

363       2-38. Regardless of the division of labor, an important recurring feature of COIN is the transition of re-

364       sponsibility and participation in key lines of operations. As consistently and conscientiously as possible,

365       military leaders ensure continuity in meeting the needs of the HN government and the local population.

366       The same general guidelines that govern battle handovers apply to transitions between participants in

367       COIN. Whether the transition is from one military unit to another or from a military unit to civilian agen-

368       cies (external or local), everyone involved requires a clear understanding of tasks and responsibilities to be

369       passed from one element to the next. Maintaining unity of effort is particularly important during transi-

370       tions, especially between elements of different capabilities and capacity. Relationships tend to break down

     2-8                              FM 3-24/FMFM 3-24                              16 June 2006 (FINAL DRAFT)


---

     Unity of Effort: Integrating Civilian and Military Activities (FINAL DRAFT—NOT FOR IMPLEMENTATION)

371  during transitions. Transition is not a single event, where all activity happens at once. It is a rolling process

372  of little handoffs between different actors along several streams of activities, and there are usually multiple

373  transitions for any one stream of activity over time. Using the coordination mechanisms in the next section

374  can help to create and sustain the linkages that support effective transitions without compromising unity of

375  effort.

376 CIVILIAN AND MILITARY INTEGRATION MECHANISMS

377  2-39. Applying the principle of unity of effort is possible in many organizational forms. The first choice

378  should be to identify existing coordination mechanisms and incorporate them into comprehensive COIN ef-

379  forts. This includes existing U.S. government, multinational, and HN mechanisms. Context is extremely

380  important. Although many of these structures exist and are often employed in other types of missions (such

381  as peacekeeping or humanitarian relief), there is an acute and fundamental difference in an insurgency en-

382  vironment. The nature of the conflict and its focus on the population makes civilian and military unity a

383  decisive point for the insurgents. The following discussion highlights some of the well established, general

384  mechanisms for civilian and military integration. Many civil-military organizations and mechanisms have

385  been created for specific missions. Although the names and acronyms differ, in their general outlines they

386  usually reflect the concepts portrayed in the following sections.

387  2-40. The U.S. government influences events worldwide through the effective employment of the instru-

388  ments of national power. These instruments are diplomatic, informational, military and economic and are

389  coordinated by the appropriate executive branch officials, often with National Security Council assistance.

390  2-41. The National Security Council is the President’s principal forum for considering national security

391  and foreign policy matters. It serves as the President’s principal means for coordinating policy among vari-

392  ous interagency organizations. At the strategic level, the National Security Council creates the interagency

393  political-military plan for COIN. (See JP 1.)

394 JOINT INTERAGENCY COORDINATION GROUP

395  2-42. Joint interagency coordination groups (JIACGs) help combatant commanders in the conduct COIN

396  operations by providing interagency support of plans, operations, contingencies, and initiatives. The goal

397  of a JIACG is to provide timely, usable information and advice from an interagency perspective to the

398  combatant commander by information sharing, integration, synchronization, training, and exercising.

399  JIACGs may include representatives from other federal departments and agencies, state and local authori-

400  ties, and liaison officers from other commands and DOD components. The interagency representatives and

401  liaison officers are the subject matter experts for their respective agencies and commands, and provide the

402  critical bridge between the combatant commander and interagency organizations. (See JP 3-08, Vol I.)

403 COUNTRY TEAM

404  2-43. At the HN level, the U.S. country team is the primary interagency coordinating structure for COIN.

405  (See figure 2-1.) The country team is the senior in-country coordinating and supervising body, headed by

406  the U.S. chief of mission—usually the Ambassador—and composed of the senior member of each repre-

407  sented department or agency. In a foreign country, the chief of mission is the highest U.S. civil authority.

408  The Foreign Service Act states, “The Chief of Mission to a foreign country has responsibility for the direc-

409  tion, coordination, and supervision of all Government executive branch employees in that country except

410  for service-members and employees under the command of a United States area military commander. The

411  chief of mission is the senior United States Government official permanently assigned in the host country,

412  and as such is responsible to the president for policy oversight of all United States government programs.”

413  The chief of mission leads the country team and is responsible for integrating U.S. efforts in support of the

414  host nation. As permanently established interagency organizations, country teams represent a priceless

415  COIN resource, with deep reservoirs of local knowledge and interaction with the HN government and

416  population.

     16 June 2006 (FINAL DRAFT)  FM3-24/FMFM 3-24                                                          2-9


---

     Chapter 2 (FINAL DRAFT—NOT FOR IMPLEMENTATION)

417                                 Figure 2-1. Sample country team

418        2-44. The more extensive the U.S. participation is in a COIN and the more dispersed U.S. forces are

419        throughout a country, the greater the need for additional mechanisms to extend civilian oversight and assis-

420        tance to match the scope of the challenge. However, given the limited resources of the Department of State

421        and the other U.S. government agencies, military forces often represent the country team in decentralized

422        and diffuse conflict environments. Operating with a clear understanding of the guiding political aims,

423        members of the military at all levels must be prepared to exercise judgment and act without the benefit of

424        immediate civilian oversight and control. At each subordinate political level of the HN government, mili-

425        tary and civilian leaders should establish a coordinating structure, such as a civil-military operations center

426        (CMOC), that includes representatives of the HN government and security forces, as well as U.S. and mul-

427        tinational forces and agencies. CMOCs facilitate the integration of military and political actions. Below the

428        national level, additional structures may be established where military commanders and civilian leaders can

429        meet directly with local leaders to discuss issues. Where possible, international organizations and NGOs

430        should be encouraged to participate in coordination meetings to ensure their actions are integrated with

431        military and HN plans.

432        2-45. In practice, the makeup of country team varies widely, depending on the U.S. departments and agen-

433        cies represented in country, the desires of the Ambassador and the HN situation. During COIN, members

434        of the country team meet regularly to coordinate U.S. government diplomatic, informational, military, and

435        economic activities in the host nation to ensure unity of effort. The interagency representatives usually in-

436        clude at least the Departments of State, Defense, Justice and Treasury; USAID; Central Intelligence

437        Agency; and Drug Enforcement Administration. Participation of other U.S. government organizations de-

438        pends on the situation.

439        2-46. In almost all bilateral missions, DOD is represented on the country team by the U.S. defense atta-

440        ché’s office or the security assistance organization. They are key military sources of information for inter-

441        agency coordination in foreign countries. (Security assistance organizations are called by various names,

442        such as the Office of Defense Cooperation, Security Assistance Office, or Military Group. The choice is

443        largely governed by the preference of the host nation.)

     2-10                           FM 3-24/FMFM 3-24                16 June 2006 (FINAL DRAFT)


---

     Unity of Effort: Integrating Civilian and Military Activities (FINAL DRAFT—NOT FOR IMPLEMENTATION)

444  Provincial Reconstruction Teams in Afghanistan

445

446  A model for civil-military cooperation is the provincial reconstruction teams (PRTs)

447  first fielded in 2003 in Afghanistan. PRTs were conceived as a means to extend the

448  reach and enhance the legitimacy of the central government into the provinces of Af-

449  ghanistan at a time when most assistance was limited to the nation’s capital. Though

450  PRTs were staffed by a number of coalition and NATO allied countries, they gener-

451  ally consisted of 50 to 300 troops as well as representatives from multinational de-

452  velopment and diplomatic agencies. Within U.S. PRTs, USAID and State Department

453  leaders and the PRT commander formed a senior team that coordinated the policies,

454  strategies, and activities of each agency towards a common goal. In secure areas,

455  PRTs maintained a low profile. In areas where coalition combat operations were un-

456  derway, PRTs worked closely with maneuver units and local government entities to

457  ensure that shaping operations achieved their desired effects. Each PRT leadership

458  team received tremendous latitude to determine its own strategy. However, each

459  PRT used its significant funding and diverse expertise to pursue activities that fell into

460  one of three general lines of operations: pursue security sector reform, build local

461  governance, or execute reconstruction and development.

462  2-47. The country team determines how the United States can effectively apply interagency capabilities to

463  assist a HN government in creating a complementary institutional capacity to deal with an insurgency. Ef-

464  forts to support local officials and build HN capacity must be integrated with information operations so HN

465  citizens are aware of their government’s efforts. In addition, interagency capabilities must be applied down

466  to the tactical level so commanders have access to the options that such capabilities make available. The

467  CORDS approach developed during the Vietnam War provides a positive example of integrated civilian

468  and military structures that reached every level of the COIN effort.

469  Civil Operations and Rural Development Support and Accelerated

470                              Pacification in Vietnam (1967-72)

471

472  During the Vietnam War, one of the most valuable and successful elements of COIN

473  was the Civil Operations and Revolutionary—later Rural—Development Support

474  (CORDS) program. CORDS was created in 1967 to integrate U.S. civilian and mili-

475  tary support of the South Vietnamese government and people. CORDS achieved

476  great success in supporting and protecting the South Vietnamese population and un-

477  dermining the communist insurgents’ influence and appeal, particularly after the im-

478  plementation of accelerated pacification in 1968.

479  Pacification was the process by which the government asserted its influence and

480  control into an area beset by insurgents. This included local security efforts, pro-

481  grams to distribute food and medical supplies, and lasting reforms (like land redistri-

482  bution). In 1965, U.S. civilian contributions to pacification consisted of several civilian

483  agencies (among others, the Central Intelligence Agency, Agency for International

484  Development, U.S. Information Service, and State Department). Each developed its

485  own programs. Coordination was uneven. The U.S. military contribution to pacifica-

486  tion consisted of thousands of advisors. By early 1966, there were military advisory

487  teams in all of South Vietnam’s 44 provinces and most of its 243 districts. But there

488  were two separate chains of command for military and civilian pacification efforts,

489  making it particularly difficult for the civilian-run pacification program to function.

490  To establish closer integration of civilian and military efforts, in 1967 President Lyn-

491  don B. Johnson established CORDS within General William Westmoreland’s Military

492  Assistance Command, Vietnam (MACV). Robert Komer was appointed to run the

     16 June 2006 (FINAL DRAFT)  FM3-24/FMFM 3-24                                                 2-11


---

     Chapter 2 (FINAL DRAFT—NOT FOR IMPLEMENTATION)

493        program, with a three-star-equivalent rank. Civilians, including an assistant chief of

494        staff for CORDS, were integrated into military staffs at all levels. This placed civilians

495        in charge of military personnel and resources. Komer was energetic, strong-willed,

496        and persistent in getting the program started. Nicknamed “Blowtorch” for his aggres-

497        sive style, Komer was modestly successful in leading improvements in pacification

498        before the 1968 Tet offensive.

499        In mid-1968 the new MACV Commander, General Creighton Abrams, and his new

500        civilian deputy, William Colby, used CORDS as the implementing mechanism for an

501        accelerated pacification program that became the priority effort for the United States.

502        Significant allocations of personnel helped make CORDS effective. In this, the mili-

503        tary’s involvement was key. In September 1969—the highpoint of the pacification ef-

504        fort in terms of total manpower—there were 7,601 advisors assigned to province and

505        district pacification teams. Of these 6,464 were military.

506        The effectiveness of CORDS was a function of integrated civilian and military teams

507        at every level of society in Vietnam. From district to province to national level, U.S.

508        advisors and U.S. interagency partners worked closely with their Vietnamese coun-

509        terparts. The entire effort was well established under the direction of the country

510        team, led by Ambassador Ellsworth Bunker. General Abrams and his civilian deputy

511        were clear in their focus on pacification as the priority and ensured the military and

512        civilian agencies worked closely together. Keen attention was given to the ultimate

513        objective of serving the needs of the local population. Success in meeting basic

514        needs of the population led, in turn, to improved intelligence that facilitated an assault

515        on the Viet Cong political infrastructure. By early 1970, statistics indicated that 93

516        percent of South Vietnamese lived in “relatively secure” villages, an increase of al-

517        most 20 percent from the middle of 1968. By 1972, pacification had largely uprooted

518        the insurgency from among the South Vietnamese population and forced the com-

519        munists to rely more heavily on infiltrating conventional forces from North Vietnam

520        and employing them in irregular and conventional operations.

521        In 1972, South Vietnamese forces operating with significant support from U.S. air-

522        power, defeated large-scale North Vietnamese conventional attacks. Unfortunately, a

523        North Vietnamese conventional assault succeeded in 1975 after the withdrawal of

524        U.S. forces, ending of U.S. air support, and curtailment of U.S. funding to South Viet-

525        nam.

526        Pacification, once it was integrated under CORDS, was generally led, planned, and

527        executed well. CORDS was a successful synthesis of military and civilian efforts. It is

528        a useful model to consider for other COIN operations.

529 CIVIL-MILITARY OPERATIONS CENTER

530        2-48. Another mechanism for bringing elements together for coordination is the CMOC. CMOCs can be

531        established at all levels of command. CMOCs coordinate the interaction of U.S. and multinational military

532        forces with a wide variety of civilian agencies. A CMOC is not designed, nor should it be used as, a com-

533        mand and control element. However, it does provide a useful venue to transmit the commander’s guidance

534        to other agencies and exchange information, and to facilitate complementary and unified efforts.

535        2-49. Overall management of a CMOC may be assigned to a multinational force commander, shared by a

536        U.S. and a multinational commander, or shared by a U.S. commander and a civilian agency head. A

537        CMOC is an operations center that can be used to build on-site, interagency coordination to achieve unity

538        of effort. There is no established structure for a CMOC; its size and composition depend on the situation.

539        Senior civil affairs officers normally serve as the CMOC director and deputy director. Other military par-

540        ticipants usually include civil affairs, legal, operations, logistics, engineering, and medical sections of the

541        supported headquarters. Civilian members of a CMOC may include U.S. government organizations, multi-

     2-12                                  FM 3-24/FMFM 3-24             16 June 2006 (FINAL DRAFT)


---

     Unity of Effort: Integrating Civilian and Military Activities (FINAL DRAFT—NOT FOR IMPLEMENTATION)

542  national partners, international organizations, HN or other local organizations, and NGOs. (For more in-

543  formation on CMOCs, see FM 41-10.)

544 TACTICAL-LEVEL INTERAGENCY CONSIDERATIONS

545  2-50. Tactical units may find interagency expertise pushed to their level when they are responsible for

546  large AOs in a COIN environment. Tactical units down to company level must be prepared to integrate

547  their efforts with civilian organizations.

548  2-51. To ensure integration of interagency capabilities, units should conduct coordination with all inter-

549  agency representatives and organizations that enter their AO. Despite the best efforts to coordinate, often

550  the fog and friction inherent in COIN will lead to civilian organizations entering an AO without prior coor-

551  dination. Below is a suggested list for conducting coordination with interagency and other nonmilitary or-

552  ganizations who may operate in a unit’s AO:

553  z Identify organizational structures and leadership.

554  z Identify key objectives, responsibilities, capabilities, and programs.

555  z Develop common courses of action or options for inclusion in planning, movement coordina-

556  tion, and security briefings.

557  z Develop relationships that enable the greatest possible integration.

558  z Assign liaison officers to the most important civilian organizations.

559  z Define problems in clear and unambiguous terms.

560  2-52. Other useful information about the organizations includes the following:

561  z Intended duration of operations.

562  z Location of bases of operations.

563  z Number of personnel (names, descriptions).

564  z Type, color, number and license numbers of civilian vehicles.

565  z Other resources in the AO.

566  z Local groups and agencies with whom they are working.

567  z Terms of reference or operating procedures.

568 SUMMARY

569  2-53. As President John F. Kennedy eloquently noted, “You [military professionals] must know something

570  about strategy and tactics and logistics, but also economics and politics and diplomacy and history. You

571  must know everything you can know about military power, and you must also understand the limits of

572  military power. You must understand that few of the important problems of our time have…been finally

573  solved by military power alone.” Nowhere is this insight more relevant than in COIN. Successful COIN

574  requires unity of effort in bringing all instruments of national power to bear. Civilian agencies can contrib-

575  ute directly to military operations, particularly by providing intelligence. That theme will be developed in

576  more detail in the next chapter.

                                FINAL DRAFT—NOT FOR IMPLEMENTATION

1                                                  Chapter 3

2   Intelligence in Counterinsurgency

3   Everything good that happens seems to come from good intelligence.

4                                                                                General Creighton Abrams

5   Effective, accurate, and timely intelligence is essential to the conduct of any form of

6   warfare. This maxim applies especially to counterinsurgency operations, as the ulti-

7   mate success or failure of the mission depends on the effectiveness of the intelligence

8   effort. This chapter builds upon previous concepts to further describe insurgencies,

9   requirements for intelligence preparation of the battlefield and predeployment plan-

10  ning, collection and analysis of intelligence in counterinsurgency, intelligence fusion,

11  and general methodology for integrating intelligence to support operations. This

12  chapter does not supersede processes in U.S. military doctrine (see FM 2-0, FM 34-

13  130/FMFRP 3-23-2, and FMI 2-91.4) but instead provides specific guidance for

14  counterinsurgency.

15 SECTION I – INTELLIGENCE CHARACTERISTICS IN COUNTERINSURGENCY

16  3-1. Counterinsurgency (COIN) is an intelligence war. The function of intelligence in COIN is to facili-

17  tate understanding of the operational environment, with emphasis on the populace, host nation, and insur-

18  gents, so commanders can best address the issues driving the insurgency. Both insurgents and counterin-

19  surgents require an effective intelligence capability to be successful. They attempt to create and maintain

20  intelligence networks while trying to neutralize their opponent’s intelligence capabilities.

21  3-2. Intelligence in COIN is about people. U.S. forces must understand the people of the host nation, the

22  insurgents, and the host-nation (HN) government. Commanders and planners must have insight into cul-

23  tures, perceptions, values, beliefs, interests and decision-making processes of individuals and groups.

24  These requirements are the basis for collection and analytical efforts.

25  3-3. Intelligence and operations feed back on one another. Effective intelligence drives effective opera-

26  tions, which produce more intelligence. Similarly, ineffective or inaccurate intelligence produces ineffec-

27  tive operations, which reduce the availability of intelligence.

28  3-4. All operations have an intelligence component to them. This is because all Soldiers and Marines are

29  potential intelligence collectors whenever they interact with the people. Operations should therefore always

30  include intelligence collection requirements.

31  3-5. Insurgencies are local. They vary greatly in time and space. The insurgency one battalion faces will

32  often be different from that of an adjacent battalion. This shifting mosaic nature of insurgencies coupled

33  with the fact that all Soldiers and Marines are intelligence collectors means all echelons are both intelli-

34  gence producers and consumers, resulting in a bottom-up flow of intelligence. This pattern also means that

35  tactical units at brigade and below will require a great deal of support for both intelligence collection and

36  analysis, as their organic intelligence structure will often be inadequate.

37  3-6. Counterinsurgency occurs in a joint, interagency, and multinational environment at all echelons. Sol-

38  diers and Marines must coordinate intelligence collection and analysis with foreign militaries, foreign intel-

39  ligence services, and many different U.S. intelligence organizations.

    16 June 2006 (FINAL DRAFT)  FM 3-24/FMFM 3-24                                                          3-1


---

    Chapter 3 (FINAL DRAFT—NOT FOR IMPLEMENTATION)

40 SECTION II – PREDEPLOYMENT PLANNING AND INTELLIGENCE
41 PREPARATION OF THE BATTLEFIELD

42       3-7. The purpose of predeployment planning and intelligence preparation of the battlefield (IPB) is to de-

43       velop an understanding of the operating environment to drive planning and predeployment training. Prede-

44       ployment intelligence must be as detailed as possible and focus on the host nation, its people, and insur-

45       gents in the AO. Commanders and staffs use predeployment intelligence to establish a plan for addressing

46       the underlying causes of the insurgency and to prepare their units to interact with the populace in an appro-

47       priate manner. The desired end state is that commanders and their subordinates are not surprised by what

48       they encounter in theater.

49       3-8. Preparation for deployment begins with IPB. This is the systematic, continuous process of analyzing

50       the threat and environment in a specific geographic area. It is accomplished via the four steps described in

51       FM 34-130/FMFRP 3-23-2:

52       z Define the operational environment.

53       z Describe the effects of the environment.

54       z Evaluate the threat.

55       z Determine threat courses of action.

56       3-9. IPB in COIN operations follows the methodology described in FM 34-130/FMFRP 3-23-2, but it

57       varies in that it places greater emphasis on people and leaders in an area of operations (AO). (IPB is cov-

58       ered in depth in appendix B.) Though IPB is a continuous process and products resulting from it will be re-

59       vised throughout the mission, the initial products created prior to deployment are of particular importance

60       for the reasons explained above. Whenever possible, personnel must conduct a very thorough and detailed

61       IPB in preparation for deployment. IPB in COIN requires intelligence personnel to work in areas such as

62       economics, anthropology, and governance that may be outside their expertise. Therefore, integrating staffs

63       and drawing upon the knowledge of nonintelligence personnel and external subject matter experts with lo-

64       cal and regional knowledge are critical to effective unit preparation.

65       3-10. If there are already units deployed in theater, these are the best potential sources of intelligence

66       available. Deploying units should make an effort to reach forward to units in theater. Governmental agen-

67       cies such as the Department of State, U.S. Agency for International Development, and intelligence agen-

68       cies are often able to provide country studies and other background information on a theater. Open-source

69       intelligence is also very important to predeployment IPB. In many cases, background information on the

70       populace, cultures, languages, history, and government of an AO may be found in open sources. These in-

71       clude books, magazines, encyclopedias, Web sites, tourist maps, and atlases. Academic sources, such as

72       journal articles and university professors, can also be of great benefit.

73 DEFINE THE OPERATIONAL ENVIRONMENT

74       3-11. The operational environment consists of the air, land, sea, space, and associated adversary, friendly,

75       and neutral systems (political, military, economic, social, informational, infrastructure, legal, and others)

76       that are relevant to a specific joint operation (JP 1-02). At the tactical and operational levels, defining the

77       operational environment involves defining a unit’s AO and then establishing an area of interest. AOs may

78       be relatively static, but people and information flow through the AO continuously. Therefore, when defin-

79       ing an area of interest, both physical and human geography are taken into account. This is because an AO

80       will often cut across not just physical lines of communication, such as roads, but also areas that are tribally,

81       economically, or culturally interrelated. For instance, tribal and family groups in both Iraq and Afghanistan

82       cross national borders into neighboring countries. The cross-border ties allow insurgents safe haven outside

83       of their country and aid them in cross-border smuggling. The area of interest can be large relative to the

84       AO, as it must often account for the various influences that affect the AO such as—

85       z Cultural geography: family, tribal, ethnic, religious, or other links that go beyond the AO.

86       z Communications links to other regions.

87       z Economic links to other regions.

88       z Media influence on the local populace, U.S. populace, and multinational partners.

    3-2                              FM 3-24/FMFM 3-24                              16 June 2006 (FINAL DRAFT)


---

                   Intelligence in Counterinsurgency (FINAL DRAFT—NOT FOR IMPLEMENTATION)

89   z External financial, moral, and logistic support of the enemy.

90   3-12. At the theater level, this can mean the area of interest is global if enemy forces have an international

91   financial network or are able to affect popular support within the U.S. or multinational partners. At the tac-

92   tical level, this means commands must be aware of activities in neighboring regions and population centers

93   that affect the population in their AO.

94   3-13. As explained in chapter 2, another consideration for defining the operational environment is to learn

95   the lower, adjacent, and higher units of the COIN force. In addition, determine the non–Department of De-

96   fense (DOD) agencies, multinational forces, nongovernmental organizations, and HN organizations in the

97   AO. Knowledge of these units and organizations allows for establishment of working relationships and

98   procedures for sharing intelligence. These relationships and procedures are critical to developing a com-

99   prehensive common operational picture (COP) and enabling unity of effort.

100 DESCRIBE THE EFFECTS OF THE OPERATIONAL ENVIRONMENT

101  3-14. This step involves developing an understanding of the operational environment and is critical to the

102  success of operations. It includes—

103  z Civil considerations, with emphasis on the people, history, and HN government in the AO.

104  z Terrain analysis (physical geography): with emphasis on complex terrain, suburban and urban

105  terrain, key infrastructure, and lines of communication.

106  z Weather analysis, with attention given to the weather’s effects on activities of the population,

107  such as agriculture, smuggling, or insurgent actions.

108  3-15. All staff members are involved in this step. This improves the knowledge base used to develop an

109  understanding of the AO. For instance, civil affairs personnel receive training in analysis of populations,

110  cultures, and economic development, and can contribute greatly to understanding the civil considerations

111  of an AO. As another example, foreign area officers have linguistic, historical, and cultural knowledge

112  about particular regions and have often lived there for extended periods.

113  3-16. The products that result from describing the effects of the operational environment influence opera-

114  tions from the theater level down to the fire team. The description informs political activities and economic

115  policies of theater-level commanders, and drives information operations and civil-military operations plan-

116  ning. The knowledge gained affects the way Soldiers and Marines interact with the populace.

117 CIVIL CONSIDERATIONS: SOCIO-CULTURAL FACTORS ANALYSIS

118  3-17. Civil considerations are how the manmade infrastructure, civilian institutions, and attitudes and ac-

119  tivities of the civilian leaders, populations, and organizations within an area of operations influence the

120  conduct of military operations (FM 6-0). The memory aid ASCOPE expresses the six characteristics of

121  civil considerations: areas, structures, capabilities, organizations, people, and events.

122  3-18. While all characteristics of civil considerations are important, understanding the people characteristic

123  (cultural awareness) is particularly key in COIN. Cultural awareness involves understanding of socio-

124  cultural factors and their impact on military operations. Socio-cultural factors analysis is used to develop

125  cultural awareness. There are six socio-cultural considerations:

126  z Society.

127  z Social structure.

128  z Culture.

129  z Power and authority.

130  z Interests.

131  z Economics.

     16 June 2006 (FINAL DRAFT)               FM3-24/FMFM 3-24                                    3-3


---

     Chapter 3 (FINAL DRAFT—NOT FOR IMPLEMENTATION)

132 Society

133       3-19. A society can be defined as a population whose members are subject to the same political authority,

134       occupy a common territory, have a common culture, and share a sense of identity. A society is not easily

135       created or destroyed, but it is possible to do so through genocide or war.

136       3-20. No society is homogeneous. A society usually has a dominant culture, but can also have a vast num-

137       ber of secondary cultures. Different societies may share similar cultures, such as Canada and the United

138       States do. Societies are not static, but change over time.

139       3-21. Understanding the societies in the AO allows COIN forces to achieve objectives and gain support.

140       Commanders also consider societies outside the AO whose actions, opinions, or political influence can af-

141       fect the mission.

142 Social Structure

143       3-22. Each society is composed of both social structure and culture. Social structure refers to the relations

144       among groups of persons within a system of groups. Social structure is persistent over time, meaning that it

145       is regular and continuous despite disturbances, and the relation between the parts holds steady even as

146       groups expand or contract. In an army, for example, the structure consists of the arrangement into groups

147       like divisions, battalions, and companies, and the arrangement into ranks. In a society, the social structure

148       includes groups, institutions, organizations, and networks. Social structure involves the arrangement of the

149       parts that constitute society, the organization of social positions, and the distribution of people within those

150       positions.

151 Groups

152       3-23. A group is two or more people regularly interacting on the basis of shared expectations of others’

153       behavior and who have interrelated statuses and roles. In an AO, the social structure will include a variety

154       of groups, including racial groups, ethnic groups, religious groups and/or tribal groups and other kinship-

155       based groups.

156       3-24. A race is a human group that defines itself or is defined by other groups as different by virtue of in-

157       nate physical characteristics. Biologically, there is no such thing as race among human beings; race is a so-

158       cial category.

159       3-25. An ethnic group is a human community whose learned cultural practices, language, history, ancestry,

160       or religion, distinguish them from others. Members of ethnic groups see themselves as different from other

161       groups in a society, and are recognized as such by others. Religious groups may be subsets of larger ethnic

162       groups, such as Sri Lankan Buddhists, or religious groups may have members from many different ethnici-

163       ties, such as the Roman Catholic faith.

164       3-26. Tribes are generally defined as autonomous, genealogically structured groups in which the rights of

165       individuals are largely determined by their ancestry and belonging to a particular lineage. Tribes are essen-

166       tially adaptive social networks, organized by extended kinship and descent, with common needs for physi-

167       cal and economic security.

168       3-27. Understanding the composition of groups in the AO is of vital importance for COIN operations, es-

169       pecially because insurgents may organize around racial, ethnic, religious, or tribal identities. Furthermore,

170       tensions or hostilities between groups in an AO may destabilize a society and provide opportunities for in-

171       surgents. Commanders should thus identify powerful groups both inside and outside of their AO, and iden-

172       tify the formal relationships (such as treaties or alliances) and the informal relationships (such as tolerance

173       or friction) between groups. Commanders should identify cleavages between groups and cross-cutting ties

174       (for example, religious alignments that cut across ethnic differences). Insurgent leadership and their rank

175       and file may belong to separate groups, or the bulk of the population may differ from the insurgents. These

176       characteristics may suggest courses of action aimed at reinforcing or widening seams among insurgents or

177       between insurgents and the population.

     3-4                                           FM 3-24/FMFM 3-24                  16 June 2006 (FINAL DRAFT)


---

                        Intelligence in Counterinsurgency (FINAL DRAFT—NOT FOR IMPLEMENTATION)

178 Networks

179  3-28. Networks, which are a series of direct and indirect ties from one actor to a collection of others, may

180  be an important element within a social structure. Common types of networks include elite networks,

181  prison networks, transnational diasporas, and neighborhood networks. Networks can have many purposes:

182  economic, criminal, and emotional. Network analysis should consider both the structure of the network and

183  the nature of the interactions between network actors.

184 Institutions

185  3-29. Groups engaged in patterned activity to complete a common task are called institutions. Educational

186  institutions bring together groups and individuals who statuses and roles concern teaching and learning.

187  Military institutions bring together groups and individuals whose statuses and roles concern defense and

188  security. Institutions, the basic building blocks of societies, are continuous through many generations and

189  continue to exist even when the individuals that compose them are replaced.

190 Organizations

191  3-30. Organizations, both formal and informal, are institutions that have bounded membership, defined

192  goals, established operations, fixed facilities or meeting places, and a means of financial or logistic support.

193  In an AO, organizations may control, direct, restrain, or regulate the local populace. Thus, commanders

194  should identify influential organizations both inside and outside of their AO, which members of what

195  groups belong to the organization, and how their activities may affect military operations. The next step is

196  to determine how these organizations affect the local populace, whose interests they fulfill, and what role

197  they play in influencing local perceptions. Planners can generally group these organizations into communi-

198  cating, religious, economic, governance, and social.

199  3-31. Communicating organizations have the power to influence a population’s perceptions.

200  3-32. Religious organizations regulate norms, restrain or empower activities, reaffirm worldviews, and

201  provide social support. A religious organization differs from a religious group: whereas a religious group is

202  a general category, such as Christian, a religious organization is a specific community, such as the Episco-

203  pal Church.

204  3-33. Economic organizations provide employment, help regulate and stabilize monetary flow, assist in

205  development, and create social networks.

206  3-34. Social institutions, such as schools, civil society groups, and sports teams, provide support to the

207  population, create social networks, and can influence ideologies. Organizations may belong to more than

208  one category. For instance, an influential religious organization may also be a communicating organization.

209 Roles and Statuses

210  3-35. To facilitate COIN operations, the most common roles, statuses, and institutions within the society

211  must be identified. Individuals in any given society interact as members with social positions, referred to as

212  a status. Most societies associate particular statuses with particular social groups, such as family, lineage,

213  ethnicity, or religion. Statuses may be achieved by meeting certain criteria, or may be ascribed by birth.

214  Statuses are often reciprocal, such as that of husband and wife or teacher and student. Every status carries a

215  cluster of expected behaviors, such as how a person in that status is expected to think, feel, and act, as well

216  as expectations about how they should be treated by others. Thus, in American society parents (status) have

217  the obligation to care for their children (role) and the right to discipline them (role).

218 Social Norms

219  3-36. Violation of a role prescribed by a given status, such as failing to feed one’s children, results in so-

220  cial disapproval. The standard of conduct for social roles is known as a ‘social norm.’ A social norm is

221  what people are expected to do or should do, rather than what people actually do. Norms may be either

222  moral (incest prohibition, homicide prohibition) or customary (prayer before a meal, removing shoes be-

223  fore entering a house). When a person’s behavior does not conform to social norms, the person may be

     16 June 2006 (FINAL DRAFT)                FM3-24/FMFM 3-24                                 3-5


---

     Chapter 3 (FINAL DRAFT—NOT FOR IMPLEMENTATION)

224       sanctioned. Understanding roles, statuses, and social norms within an AO can clarify and provide guidance

225       to COIN forces about expected behavior.

226 Culture

227       3-37. Once the social structure has been thoroughly mapped out, COIN forces should identify and analyze

228       the culture of the society as a whole, and of each major group within the society. Whereas social structure

229       (groups, institutions, organizations) comprises the relationships among groups, institutions, and individuals

230       within a society, culture (ideas, norms, rituals, codes of behavior) provides the meaning to individuals

231       within the society. For example, families are a core institutional building block of social structure, found

232       universally. However, marital monogamy, expectations of a certain number of children, and willingness to

233       live with in-laws are all matters of culture, and are highly variable in different societies. Social structure

234       can be thought of as a skeleton, and culture is like the muscle on the bones. The two are mutually depend-

235       ent and reinforcing, and a change in one results in a change in the other.

236       3-38. Culture is “web of meaning” shared by members of a particular society or group within a society.

237       (See FM 3-05.301/MCRP 3-40.6A.) Culture has the following characteristics:

238           z Culture is a system of shared beliefs, values, customs, behaviors, and artifacts that the members

239           of society use to cope with their world and with one another.

240           z Culture is learned, though a process called enculturation.

241           z Culture is shared by members of a society; there is no “culture of one.”

242           z Culture is patterned, meaning that people in a society live and think in ways that form definite,

243           repeating patterns.

244           z Culture is changeable, meaning it is constantly changing through social interactions between

245           people and groups.

246           z Culture is arbitrary, meaning that Soldiers and Marines should make no assumptions regarding

247           what a society considers right and wrong, good and bad.

248           z Culture is internalized, in the sense that is habitual, taken-for-granted, and perceived as “natu-

249           ral” by people within the society.

250       3-39. Culture might also be described as an operational code that is valid for an entire group of people.

251       Culture conditions the individual’s range of action and ideas, including what to do (and what not to do),

252       how to do it (or not do it), whom to do it with (or whom not to do it with), and under what circumstances

253       the “rules” shift and change. Culture influences how people make judgments about what is right and

254       wrong, assess what is important and unimportant, categorize things, and deal with things that do not fit into

255       existing categories. Cultural rules are flexible in practice. For example, the kinship system of the

256       Yanomamo, an Amazonian Indian tribe, requires that individuals marry their cross cousin. However, the

257       definition of cross cousin is often changed to make people eligible for marriage.

258 Identity

259       3-40. Each individual belongs to multiple groups, through birth, assimilation, or achievement. Each group

260       to which the individual belongs influences his or her beliefs, values, attitudes, and perceptions. Individuals

261       rank their identities consciously or unconsciously into primary (national, racial, religious) identities and

262       secondary identities (hunter, blogger, coffee drinker). Frequently, individuals’ identities may be in conflict,

263       and COIN forces can use these conflicts to influence key leaders’ decision-making processes.

264 Beliefs

265       3-41. Beliefs are concepts and ideas accepted as true. Beliefs can be core, intermediate, or peripheral.

266       3-42. Core beliefs are those views that are part of a person’s deep identity, and are not easily changed.

267       Examples include belief in the existence of God, the value of democratic government, and the role of the

268       family. Core beliefs are unstated, taken for granted, resistant to change, and not consciously considered.

269       Attempts to change the central beliefs of a culture may result in significant unintended second- and third-

270       order consequences. Decisions to do so are made at the national-strategic level.

     3-6                                           FM 3-24/FMFM 3-24                  16 June 2006 (FINAL DRAFT)


---

                    Intelligence in Counterinsurgency (FINAL DRAFT—NOT FOR IMPLEMENTATION)

271  3-43. Beliefs in the intermediate region are predicated on reference to authority figures or authoritative

272  texts. Thus, intermediate beliefs can be influenced through co-optation of the opinion leaders.

273  3-44. From intermediate beliefs flow peripheral beliefs, which are open to debate, consciously considered,

274  and easiest to change. For example, peripheral beliefs about birth control, the New Deal, or the theory of

275  sexual repression derive from beliefs about the Roman Catholic Church, President Franklin Roosevelt, or

276  Sigmund Freud respectively.

277 Values

278  3-45. A value is an enduring belief that a specific mode of conduct or end state of existence is preferable to

279  an opposite or converse mode of conduct or end state of existence. Each group to which an individual be-

280  longs inculcates that individual with its values and their ranking of importance. Individuals do not unques-

281  tioningly absorb all the values of the groups to which they belong, but accept some and reject others. The

282  values of each culture to which individuals simultaneously belong are often in conflict: religious values

283  may conflict with generational values, gender values with organizational practices. Commanders should

284  evaluate the values of each group, such as toleration, stability, prosperity, social change, and self-

285  determination. Commanders should also consider whether the values promoted by the insurgency corre-

286  spond to the values of other social groups in the AO or to those of the government, and whether these dif-

287  ferences in values can be exploited by U.S. forces.

288 Attitudes and Perceptions

289  3-46. Attitudes are affinities for and aversions to groups, persons, and objects. Commanders should con-

290  sider groups’ attitudes toward other groups, outsiders, their government, the United States, the U.S. mili-

291  tary, globalization, and so forth. Attitudes affect perception, which is the process by which an individual

292  selects, evaluates, and organizes information from the external environment.

293 Belief Systems

294  3-47. The totality of the identities, beliefs, values, attitudes, and perceptions that an individual holds—and

295  the ranking of their importance—is that person’s belief system. Religions, ideologies, and all types of “-

296  isms” fall in this category. As a belief system, a religion may include such things as a concept of God, a

297  view of the afterlife, ideas about the sacred and the profane, funeral practices, rules of conduct, and modes

298  of worship.

299  3-48. The belief system acts as a filter for new information: it is the lens through which people perceive

300  the world. Understanding the belief systems of various groups in an AO allows COIN forces to more effec-

301  tively influence the population.

302  3-49. The belief systems of insurgents and other groups within the AO should be given careful attention.

303  The insurgency may frame its objectives in terms of a belief system or may use a belief system to mobilize

304  and recruit followers from among the population. Where there are differences between the belief system of

305  the insurgents and civilian groups, an opportunity exists for counterinsurgents to engineer a split between

306  the insurgency and the larger population.

307 Cultural Forms

308  3-50. Cultural forms are the concrete expression of the belief systems shared by members of a particular

309  culture. Cultural forms, such as rituals, symbols and narratives, are the medium for communicating ideolo-

310  gies, values, and norms that influence thought and behavior. Each culture constructs or invents its own cul-

311  tural forms, through which cultural meanings are transmitted and reproduced. COIN forces can identify the

312  belief systems of a culture by observing and analyzing its cultural forms.

313  3-51. The most important cultural form for COIN forces to understand is the narrative. A cultural narra-

314  tive is a story recounted in the form of a causally linked set of events that explains an event in group’s his-

315  tory and expresses the values, character, or self-identity of the group. Narratives are the means through

316  which ideologies are expressed and absorbed by individuals in a society. For example, at the Boston Tea

317  Party in 1773, Sam Adams and the Sons of Liberty dumped five tons of tea in the Boston Harbor as a pro-

     16 June 2006 (FINAL DRAFT)                 FM3-24/FMFM 3-24                                      3-7


---

     Chapter 3 (FINAL DRAFT—NOT FOR IMPLEMENTATION)

318       test against what they considered unfair British taxation. This narrative explains in part why the Revolu-

319       tionary War began but also tells Americans something about themselves each time they hear the story: that

320       fairness, independence, and justice are worth fighting for. As this example indicates, narratives may not

321       conform to historical facts, or they may drastically simplify facts to more clearly express basic cultural val-

322       ues. (For example, Americans in 1773 were taxed less than their British counterparts, and most British at-

323       tempts to raise revenues from the colonies were designed to help reduce the crushing national debt incurred

324       in their defense.) By listening to narratives, COIN forces can identify the basic core values of the society.

325       3-52. Other cultural forms include ritual and symbols. Ritual is a stereotyped sequence of activities involv-

326       ing gestures, words, and objects, performed to influence supernatural entities or forces on behalf of the ac-

327       tors’ goals and interests. Rituals can be either sacred or secular. A symbol is the smallest unit of cultural

328       meaning and is filled with a vast amount of information that can be decoded by a knowledgeable observer.

329       Symbols can be objects, activities, words, relationships, events, or gestures. Institutions and organizations

330       often use cultural symbols to amass political power or to generate resistance against external groups. Care-

331       ful attention should be paid to the meaning of the common symbols and how various groups in the AO use

332       them.

333 Power and Authority

334       3-53. Once the social structure has been mapped and the culture is understood, COIN forces must under-

335       stand how power is apportioned and used within a society. Understanding power is the key to manipulating

336       the interests of groups within the society.

337       3-54. There may be many power holders in a society, both formal and informal. Understanding the formal

338       political system (including central governments, local governments, political interest groups, political par-

339       ties, unions, government agencies, and regional and international political bodies) is necessary but not suf-

340       ficient for COIN operations. Informal power holders are often more important for COIN operations, and

341       may include ethno-religious groups, social elites, or religious figures.

342       3-55. For each group within the AO, COIN forces should identify what type of power the group has, what

343       they use their power for (such as amassing resources, protecting followers, and so forth) and how they ac-

344       quire and maintain power. Commanders should also identify which leaders have power within particular

345       groups, what type of power they have, what they use their power for, and how their power is acquired and

346       maintained. There are four major forms of power in a society: coercive force, social capital, economic re-

347       sources, and authority.

348 Coercive Force

349       3-56. Coercion is the ability to compel a person to act through threat of harm or by use of physical force.

350       Coercive force can be positive, in the sense that a group may provide security to its members (such as po-

351       licing and defense of territory), or it may be negative, in the sense that a group may intimidate or threaten

352       group members or outsiders. One essential role of government is to monopolize coercive force within its

353       territory in order to provide physical security to its citizens. Where the state fails to provide security to its

354       citizens or becomes a threat to them, citizens may seek alternative security guarantees from an ethnic, po-

355       litical, religious, or tribal group in the AO. Insurgents, and other nongovernmental groups, may possess

356       considerable coercive force, in the form of paramilitary units, tribal militias, gangs, or organizational secu-

357       rity personnel that they use to gain power over a civilian population. These groups may be using their coer-

358       cive force for a variety of purposes unrelated to the insurgency, such as protecting their own community

359       members, carrying out vendettas, or engaging in criminal activities. For example, what may appear to be

360       insurgent violence against “innocent civilians” could in fact be a tribal blood feud unrelated to the insur-

361       gency.

362 Social Capital

363       3-57. Social capital refers to the power of individuals and groups to utilize social networks of reciprocity

364       and exchange to accomplish their goals. In many non-Western societies, patron-client relationships are an

365       important form of social capital. In a system based on patron-client relationships, an individual in a power-

366       ful position provides goods, services, security, or other resources to followers in exchange for political sup-

     3-8                                               FM 3-24/FMFM 3-24            16 June 2006 (FINAL DRAFT)


---

                       Intelligence in Counterinsurgency (FINAL DRAFT—NOT FOR IMPLEMENTATION)

367  port or loyalty, thereby amassing power. COIN forces must identify, where possible, which groups and in-

368  dividuals have social capital and how they attract and maintain followers.

369 Economic Power

370  3-58. Economic power is the power of groups or individuals to use economic incentives and disincentives

371  to change people’s behavior. Economic systems can be formal or informal, or a mixture of both. In weak or

372  failed states, where the formal economy may be functioning in a diminished capacity, the informal econ-

373  omy plays a central role in people’s daily lives. For example, in many societies, monies and other eco-

374  nomic goods are distributed though the tribal or clan networks and are connected to indigenous patronage

375  systems. Those groups able to provide their members with economic resources through an informal econ-

376  omy (whether smuggling, black market activities, barter, or exchange) gain followers and may amass con-

377  siderable political power. Counterinsurgents must therefore be aware of the local informal economy and

378  evaluate the role played by various groups and individuals within it. Insurgent organizations may also at-

379  tract followers through criminal activities that provide income.

380 Authority

381  3-59. Authority is power that is attached to positions and is justified by the beliefs of the obedient. There

382  are three primary types of authority:

383            z Rational-legal authority, which is grounded in law and contract, codified in impersonal rules,

384            and commonly found in developed, capitalist societies.

385            z Charismatic authority, which is exercised by leaders who develop allegiance among their fol-
