386            lowers because of their unique, individual charismatic appeal, whether ethical, religious, politi-

387            cal, or social.

388            z Traditional authority, which is usually invested in a hereditary line or in a particular office by a

389            higher power.

390  3-60. Traditional authority, which relies on the precedent of history, is a common type of authority in non-

391  Western societies. In particular, tribal and religious forms of organization rely heavily on traditional au-

392  thority. Traditional authority figures often wield enough power, especially in rural areas, to single-

393  handedly drive an insurgency. Understanding the types of authority at work in the formal and informal po-

394  litical systems of the AO helps COIN forces identify the agents of influence who can help or hinder the ac-

395  complishment of objectives.

396 Interests and Economics

397  3-61. After mapping the social structure, evaluating the culture of the society as a whole and that of each

398  group within the society, and identifying who holds formal and informal power and why, COIN forces

399  should consider how to use the capabilities at their disposal to reduce support for insurgents and gain sup-

400  port for the legitimate government.

401  3-62. To accomplish this task, requires COIN forces to understand the population’s interests, meaning the

402  core motivations that drive behavior, such as physical security, basic necessities, economic well-being, po-

403  litical participation, and social identity. During times of instability when the government cannot function,

404  the groups and organizations to which an individual belongs provide physical security, basic necessities,

405  economic resources, and political identity. Understanding a group’s interests illuminates the various oppor-

406  tunities available to a commander to meet or frustrate those interests with the capabilities at his or her dis-

407  posal. A group’s interests may become grievances if the government does not fulfill the group’s needs.

408 Physical Security

409  3-63. During an insurgency or any period of political instability, the primary interest of the civilian popula-

410  tion is physical security for themselves and their families. When government forces fail to provide security

411  or threaten the security of civilians, the civilian population is much more likely to seek alternative security

412  guarantees from insurgents, militias, or other armed groups. This process can drive an insurgency. How-

413  ever, when government forces provide physical security, civilians are more likely to support the govern-

     16 June 2006 (FINAL DRAFT)             FM3-24/FMFM 3-24                                                 3-9


---

     Chapter 3 (FINAL DRAFT—NOT FOR IMPLEMENTATION)

414        ment against the insurgents. Commanders should therefore identify who is providing security to each group

415        within the civilian population. The provision of security must occur in conjunction with political and eco-

416        nomic reform.

417 Essential Services

418        3-64. Other interests of a population include that which is necessary to sustain life, such as food, water,

419        clothing, shelter, and medical treatment. Stabilizing a populace requires meeting all these essential needs.

420        People pursue these needs until they are met, at any cost and from any source. If an insurgent source pro-

421        vides for the populace’s needs, the populace will support that source. This may impede mission accom-

422        plishment. If the government provides that which is necessary to sustain life, the population is more likely

423        to support the government. Commanders should therefore identify who is providing basic life necessities to

424        each group within the civilian population.

425 Economy

426        3-65. Individuals and groups within a social system satisfy their economic interests through the produc-

427        tion, distribution, and consumption of goods and services. How individuals within a society satisfy their

428        economic needs depends on the level and type of economic development. For instance, in a rural-based so-

429        ciety, land ownership may be a chief component of any economic development plan. For a more urban so-

430        ciety, jobs in both the public and private sector may be the biggest issue of contention.

431        3-66. Economic disparities between groups may drive political instability, and economic disenfranchise-

432        ment may be a core grievance of insurgent groups. Insurgent leadership or traditional authority figures of-

433        ten use real or perceived societal injustices to drive an insurgency, such as economic disenfranchisement,

434        exploitative economic arrangements, or a significant income disparity that creates (or allows for) intracta-

435        ble class distinctions. The economic system within an AO may be adversely affected by military operations

436        and/or insurgent actions. This can cause the population to resent the government. On the other hand, resto-

437        ration of production and distribution systems can energize the economy, create jobs and growth, and posi-

438        tively influence local perceptions. Commanders should evaluate whether the society has a functioning

439        economy, whether civilians have fair access to land and property, and how to minimize the economic

440        grievances of the civilian population in order to reduce support for insurgency and increase support for the

441        government.

442 Political Participation

443        3-67. Another interest of the civilian population is political participation. Many insurgencies begin because

444        certain groups within the society perceive that they have been denied political rights. In order to satisfy

445        their political interests, groups may use preexisting cultural narratives and symbols to mobilize for political

446        action. Very often, they will coalesce around traditional or charismatic authority figures. Commanders

447        should investigate whether all members of the civilian population have a guarantee of political participa-

448        tion; whether there is ethnic, religious, or other discrimination; and whether there are legal, social, or other

449        policies that contribute to the insurgency. Commanders should also identify what narratives mobilize po-

450        litical action within the group and who their traditional or charismatic authority figures are.

451 LANGUAGES

452        3-68. The languages used in an AO have a major impact on operations. Languages must be identified so

453        language training, communication aids such as phrase cards, and requisitioning of translators can be ac-

454        complished. Translators are critical for intelligence collection, interaction with local citizens and commu-

455        nity leaders, and development of products for information operations. The mission cannot proceed without

456        them. (See appendix C.)

457        3-69. Another aspect of language involves transliteration of names not normally written using the English

458        alphabet. This affects all intelligence operations, to include collection, analysis, fusion, and targeting. In

459        countries that do not use the English alphabet, a theaterwide standard should be set for spelling names.

460        Without a spelling standard, it can be difficult to conduct effective analysis. (This problem is compounded

461        in cultures where people use different names depending on the context in which the name is asked.) Unfa-

     3-10                           FM 3-24/FMFM 3-24  16 June 2006 (FINAL DRAFT)


---

     Intelligence in Counterinsurgency (FINAL DRAFT—NOT FOR IMPLEMENTATION)

462  miliar and similar place names can confuse map reading and cause targeting errors. In addition, insurgents

463  may be released from custody if their name is misidentified. To overcome these problems, there must be

464  one spelling standard for a theater. Because of the interagency nature of COIN operations, the standard

465  must also be agreed upon by non-DOD agencies.

466 EVALUATE THE THREAT

467  3-70. The purpose of evaluating the insurgency and other related threats to the mission is to understand the

468  enemy, enemy capabilities, enemy vulnerabilities, and opportunities commanders may be able to exploit.

469  Evaluating an insurgency is difficult. Neatly arrayed enemy orders of battle are not available and are not

470  the critical information commanders require. Commanders require knowledge of difficult-to-measure char-

471  acteristics of the threat, such as insurgent goals, grievances exploited by the insurgents, means used by the

472  insurgents to generate support, the organization of insurgent forces, and accurate locations of key insurgent

473  leaders. However, insurgents usually look no different from the general populace and do their best to blend

474  with noncombatants. The structure of insurgent groups is often adaptive and flexible, and they may pub-

475  licly claim different motivations and goals than what is truly driving their actions. Further complicating

476  matters, insurgent organizations are often rooted in ethnic or tribal groups and they often take part in

477  criminal activities or link themselves to political parties, charities, or religious organizations. This blurs the

478  lines as to what and who the threat is. The following characteristics of an insurgency provide a basis for

479  evaluating the threat and are discussed in depth in appendix B:

480  z Insurgent objectives.

481  z Insurgent motivations.

482  z Popular support/tolerance.

483  z Support activities, capabilities, and vulnerabilities.

484  z Information activities, capabilities, and vulnerabilities.

485  z Political activities, capabilities, and vulnerabilities.

486  z Violent activities, capabilities, and vulnerabilities.

487  z Organization.

488  z Key leaders and personalities.

489  3-71. Of the preceding characteristics, knowledge of objectives, motivations, and means of generating

490  popular support/tolerance will often be the most important intelligence requirements and the most difficult

491  to ascertain. In particular, generating popular support/tolerance often has the greatest impact on the insur-

492  gency’s long-term effectiveness. This is usually the center of gravity of an insurgency. Support/tolerance,

493  provided either willingly or unwillingly by the populace, results in safe havens, freedom of movement, lo-

494  gistic support, financial support, intelligence, and new personnel for the insurgency. It should be noted that

495  support/tolerance is often generated using violent coercion and intimidation of the people. In these cases,

496  even if the people do not favor the insurgent cause, they are forced to tolerate the insurgents or provide

497  them material support.

498  3-72. Generating popular support has a positive effect on an insurgent organization. As the insurgent

499  group gains support, its capabilities grow, which in turn enables it to gain more support. Insurgents gener-

500  ally view popular support as a zero-sum game in which a gain in support for their movement is a loss for

501  the government and a loss of support for the government is a gain for the insurgents.

502  3-73. Although violence is generally the most visible aspect of the insurgency, it can represent only a small

503  portion of the insurgent effort. Support activities, such as recruitment, securing finances, and securing lo-

504  gistics, are more difficult to detect, but often more widespread and important to the insurgency than vio-

505  lence. It is important to track the violent actions of insurgents, but this should not be done at the expense of

506  understanding the other characteristics of the insurgency.

507  3-74. As explained in chapter 1, insurgents are by nature an asymmetric threat. They do not use terrorist

508  and guerrilla tactics because they are cowards afraid of a “fair fight.” They use these tactics because they

509  are the best means available for achieving their goals. Terrorist and guerrilla attacks are usually planned to

510  achieve the greatest political and informational impact with the lowest amount of risk to the insurgents.

     16 June 2006 (FINAL DRAFT)        FM3-24/FMFM 3-24                                     3-11


---

     Chapter 3 (FINAL DRAFT—NOT FOR IMPLEMENTATION)

511        This means that commanders must not simply understand insurgent tactics and targeting; they must also

512        understand how violence is used to achieve the goals of the insurgent organization and how violent actions

513        are linked to political and informational actions.

514        3-75. Attitudes and perceptions play a very important role in understanding the threat. It is important to

515        know how the population perceives the insurgents, the host nation, and U.S. forces. In addition HN and in-

516        surgent perceptions of one another and of U.S. forces are also very important. Attitudes and perceptions of

517        different groups and organizations inform decision-making processes and shape popular thinking on the le-

518        gitimacy of the actors in the war.

519 Insurgency-related Threats

520        3-76. The presence of an insurgency with widespread support in a state usually means the government of

521        the state is weak and losing control. In such situations, criminal organizations and nongovernmental militia

522        forces can become powerful.

523 Criminal Networks

524        3-77. Though not necessarily a part of the insurgency, criminal networks can further undermine the author-

525        ity of the state through banditry, hijackings, kidnappings, smuggling, and other disruptive activities. Insur-

526        gent organizations often link themselves to criminal networks in order to secure additional sources of fund-

527        ing and logistics. In some cases, insurgent networks and criminal networks become indistinguishable. As

528        commanders work to reassert government control of an area, they need to know what criminal networks

529        are present, what their activities are, and how they interact with the insurgency.

530 Nongovernmental Militias

531        3-78. As the state weakens and violence increases, people look for ways to protect themselves. If the state

532        cannot provide protection, people may organize into armed militias to protect themselves. Examples of this

533        are the Protestant militias in Northern Ireland, the right-wing paramilitary organizations that formed in Co-

534        lombia to counter the FARC, and the militias of various ethnic and political groups in Iraq during Opera-

535        tion Iraqi Freedom. If militias are outside of the control of the government, they are often disruptive to end-

536        ing the insurgency. They may become more powerful than the government, particularly at a local level.

537        They may also fuel the insurgency and a downward spiral into full-scale civil war if they conduct their own

538        armed attacks on insurgents.

539        3-79. Although militias may not be an immediate threat to U.S. forces, they constitute a long-term threat to

540        the law and order within the state. They should be tracked by intelligence in the same manner as an insur-

541        gent group. The intent of this analysis is to understand what role militias play in the insurgency, what role

542        they play in politics, and how they can be disarmed.

543 Opportunities

544        3-80. As the threat is evaluated and the interests and attitudes of the people are learned, it becomes possi-

545        ble to identify divisions between the insurgents and the people as well as between the HN government and

546        the people. For instance, if the insurgent ideology is not popular, the insurgents may rely on intimidation to

547        generate support. Another example would be discovering that the insurgents gain support by providing so-

548        cial services that the HN government neglects. Determining such divisions provides opportunities for craft-

549        ing friendly operations that expand splits between the insurgents and the people or lessen the divides be-

550        tween the HN government and the people.

551 DETERMINE THREAT COURSES OF ACTION

552        3-81. The purpose of this IPB step is to understand insurgent strategies and tactics so they may be effec-

553        tively countered. The initial determination of threat courses of action focuses on two levels of analysis. The

554        first is determining the overall strategy, or combination of strategies, selected by an insurgency to achieve

555        its goals. The second is determining tactical courses of action used in support of the strategy.

     3-12                                      FM 3-24/FMFM 3-24  16 June 2006 (FINAL DRAFT)


---

                    Intelligence in Counterinsurgency (FINAL DRAFT—NOT FOR IMPLEMENTATION)

556 INSURGENT STRATEGIES

557  3-82. As indicated in chapter 1, there are five general strategies insurgents may follow:

558        z Conspiratorial.

559        z Protracted popular war.

560        z Traditional.

561        z Military-focus.

562        z Urban warfare.

563  3-83. These approaches may be combined with one another or occur in parallel in the same AO as differ-

564  ent insurgent groups follow different paths. In addition, insurgents may change strategies over time. The

565  type of strategy pursued affects the insurgents’ organization, the types of activities they conduct, and the

566  emphasis they place on different activities. It should be noted that insurgents may be inept at the use of a

567  given strategy, or they may misread the operational environment and use an inappropriate strategy. Knowl-

568  edge of misapplication of strategy or the use of different strategies by different insurgent groups may pro-

569  vide opportunities that can be exploited by counterinsurgents.

570 Conspiratorial

571  3-84. A conspiratorial strategy relies on the secret creation of a political body that exploits popular revolu-

572  tion, the actions of other insurgents, or weakness in the government to seize control. Insurgent organiza-

573  tions following this strategy generally conduct very few overt violent or informational actions until they

574  believe the conditions are set for them to seize power. Their cadre is large relative to the number of the

575  combatants in the organization and they may have a small mass base or no mass base at all. The organiza-

576  tion will usually be small, secretive, disciplined, and tightly controlled by leadership. Insurgencies follow-

577  ing a conspiratorial strategy may be countered effectively by targeting leaders and cadre through direct ac-

578  tion. However, information operations and political or economic programs should be used to prevent the

579  government from weakening to the point that conspiratorial insurgents can seize power.

580 Military-focus

581  3-85. The military-focus strategy relies almost completely on violence. Followers of this strategy believe

582  the populace shares a general resentment for the government and that violence will create the conditions

583  for overthrowing the government. They further believe that, as the populace sees insurgents fighting the

584  government on their behalf, it will be motivated to support the insurgency. Insurgent organizations follow-

585  ing this strategy tend to have leaders and combatants, but little, if any, cadre or mass base. This strategy has

586  rarely been effective historically, although the Cuban revolution is a well-known success. Targeting leaders

587  and combatants through direct action is often the best way to counter an insurgent group using a military-

588  focus strategy. However, political, economic, and social programs can be used to bolster support for the

589  HN government and make it impossible for the insurgents to gain support through violent actions.

590 Urban

591  3-86. An urban strategy relies on terrorism in urban areas. The intent of the terrorist activities is to incite

592  repression by COIN forces that bolsters support to the insurgency. In addition, practitioners of the urban

593  strategy may seek to tie-down large numbers of COIN forces in cities so they may gain freedom of move-

594  ment in rural areas. They may also infiltrate and subvert government and security forces in urban areas. In-

595  surgent organizations using this strategy tend to be composed of small, compartmentalized cells. Like the

596  military-focus strategy, the cadre and mass base in the organization will generally be small relative to the

597  number of combatants. Countering the urban strategy relies on effectively securing cities without being re-

598  pressive. As in countering other strategies, information operations, political programs, economic programs,

599  and social programs may be used to isolate the insurgents and ensure support to the HN government.

     16 June 2006 (FINAL DRAFT)       FM3-24/FMFM 3-24                                                   3-13


---

     Chapter 3 (FINAL DRAFT—NOT FOR IMPLEMENTATION)

600 Protracted Popular War

601        3-87. Protracted popular war is the best-known, most successful insurgent strategy and is most closely as-

602        sociated with Mao Zedong. It relies on the slow development of popular support while simultaneously un-

603        dermining the will, legitimacy, and popular support of counterinsurgents. This strategy makes the best use

604        of asymmetries between insurgents and counterinsurgents.

605        3-88. Although Mao ascribed phases to this strategy, it need not follow those phases. The insurgents also

606        need not progress to conventional war. Information and political activities supported by terrorism and/or

607        guerrilla warfare may be sufficient to defeat counterinsurgents. In some cases, information and political ac-

608        tivities may be effective without any violent action.

609        3-89. There are many different ways of structuring an insurgent organization to follow this strategy, and

610        the structure will likely change with both time and location on the battlefield. However, they tend to rely

611        on a large mass base of supporters. Other indicators of this strategy are overt violence, heavy use of infor-

612        mation and political activities, and focus on building popular support for the insurgency. Protracted popu-

613        lar war is best countered by winning the “hearts and minds” of the populace and separating the leaders,

614        cadre, and combatants from the mass base through information operations, civil-military operations, eco-

615        nomic programs, social programs, and political action.

616 Traditional Strategy

617        3-90. A traditional strategy relies on building an insurgency along the lines of ethnic, tribal, or religious

618        groups. Such insurgencies draw heavily upon the identity of a group to differentiate themselves from the

619        government. When this strategy is used effectively, entire communities may join the insurgency together.

620        This strategy is strongly associated with liberation insurgencies as insurgent leaders create an “us-and-

621        them” gap between the government and the ethnic, tribal, or religious group and use this division to en-

622        courage others to join the insurgency. Insurgencies following this strategy tend to have a large mass base

623        of passive and active supporters built around pre-existing social networks. There also tend to be many aux-

624        iliaries. The number of cadre is generally small, as the insurgency relies on traditional authority structures

625        and is generally not intent on spreading a new political system. The number of combatants varies, but there

626        are often large numbers of part-time combatants. There may or may not be many full-time combatants.

627        Leaders usually come from traditional leadership positions, such as religious leaders, tribal chiefs, or tribal

628        councils. Insurgencies using a traditional strategy can be difficult to counter, particularly if they are fight-

629        ing to liberate their group from the government. Generally speaking, political or economic deals are neces-

630        sary to end the conflict.

631 TACTICAL COURSES OF ACTION

632        3-91. Insurgents base their tactical courses of action on their capabilities and intentions. The capabilities of

633        an organization are the support, information, political, and violent capabilities evaluated in the third step of

634        IPB. The intentions come from goals, motivations, strategy, culture, perceptions, and leadership personali-

635        ties. It is important to understand that many different courses of action can happen in any given region at

636        any given time and that tactical courses of action change with both time and location. People and their atti-

637        tudes, both within the nation and often without, are the ultimate targets of the insurgents in all operations.

638        Therefore, special attention must be paid to what effects insurgent courses of action have on the people and

639        how the insurgents achieve those effects. Finally, it is important to note that tactical actions can have stra-

640        tegic effects. This is because insurgent propaganda and media reporting can reach a global audience, mul-

641        tiplying the effects of insurgent tactical actions. (Tactical courses of action are covered in greater depth in

642        appendix B.)

643 SECTION III – INTELLIGENCE, SURVEILLANCE, AND RECONNAISSANCE
644 OPERATIONS

645        3-92. The purpose of intelligence, surveillance, and reconnaissance (ISR) operations is to develop the in-

646        telligence necessary for addressing the issues that drive the insurgency. Several factors are particularly im-

647        portant for ISR operations in COIN environments. These include:

     3-14                             FM 3-24/FMFM 3-24                     16 June 2006 (FINAL DRAFT)


---

                          Intelligence in Counterinsurgency (FINAL DRAFT—NOT FOR IMPLEMENTATION)

648  z A focus on the local populace.

649  z Collection occurring at all echelons.

650  z Localized nature of insurgencies.

651  z All Soldiers and Marines being collectors.

652  z Insurgent use of complex terrain.

653  3-93. Intelligence gaps and information requirements determined during IPB may range from the location

654  of insurgent leaders, to local perceptions of insurgents by the populace, to the status of the HN’s political

655  parties. In general, collection focuses on the populace, the insurgents, and the host nation.

656  3-94. The fact that all units collect and report information combined with the localized nature of insurgen-

657  cies means that the flow of intelligence in COIN is more bottom up than top-down. Pushing intelligence

658  collection assets down to the tactical level therefore benefits all echelons. Its advantages include improving

659  the collection capabilities of tactical units, ensuring reports go through appropriate channels to reach

660  higher-echelon audiences, and getting collectors to where the insurgents are.

661  3-95. While collection may occur in any unit and collectors may be pushed down to the lowest levels, the

662  overall intelligence synchronization plan (formerly the collection plan) must remain synchronized so that

663  all echelons receive the information they require. There are several means of accomplishing this. One is to

664  ensure priority intelligence requirements (PIRs) are “nested” at all echelons. They may be tailored to local

665  or regional circumstances, but local and theater collection should support one another. Headquarters moni-

666  tor requests for information from lower echelons and tasking from higher echelons so they can get informa-

667  tion to units that require it in a timely manner. It is important to ensure all Soldiers and Marines understand

668  the PIRs. This enables them to know when they should report something they have seen or heard and what

669  they should report.

670  3-96. Feedback from analysts and operators to collectors is important to synchronize the ISR effort. This

671  response lets collectors know a report is of interest and that there should be follow-up. Such feedback may

672  come from any unit at any echelon.

673  3-97. Also affecting intelligence synchronization is the requirement to work closely with U.S. governmen-

674  tal agencies, HN security and intelligence organizations, and third-country intelligence organizations. Syn-

675  chronization of the efforts of the various agencies and organizations is driven by theater-level ISR plan-

676  ning, but this coordination occurs at all echelons. Communication among collection managers and

677  collectors down to the battalion level is important to ensuring work is not duplicated unnecessarily and cir-

678  cular reporting does not occur. (See the Intelligence Collaboration and Fusion section, below).

679  3-98. Insurgents often seek to use complex terrain to their advantage. Collection managers do not ignore

680  areas of complex terrain. In addition, insurgents use “seams” between maneuver units to their advantage.

681  (Seams are boundaries between units not adequately covered by any unit.) Collection managers must have

682  a means of monitoring seams in order to ensure the enemy cannot establish undetected bases of operation.

683 THE INTELLIGENCE–OPERATIONS DYNAMIC

684  3-99. Intelligence and operations have a dynamic relationship in all operations. Intelligence drives opera-

685  tions and successful operations generate intelligence. For instance, an operation increasing the security and

686  general happiness of a town often increases the amount of information offered by its inhabitants. This re-

687  sults in more effective operations. The reverse of this is also true. Operations conducted without accurate

688  intelligence may upset the people and cause them to offer less information.

689  3-100. In many cases, tactical units new to an AO have little intelligence on the AO. Operations will be

690  conducted with the purpose of gathering intelligence. Even in permissive environments where a great deal

691  is known about the enemy, there is an intelligence aspect to all operations.

692  3-101. Because intelligence and operations are so closely related, it is important that collectors be linked

693  directly to the analysts and operators they support and remain responsive to their requirements. Further,

694  collectors should not passively wait for requirements, but closely monitor the operational environment and

695  recommend requirements on their own.

     16 June 2006 (FINAL DRAFT)            FM3-24/FMFM 3-24                                           3-15


---

     Chapter 3 (FINAL DRAFT—NOT FOR IMPLEMENTATION)

696 HUMAN INTELLIGENCE AND OPERATIONAL REPORTING

697        3-102. Human intelligence (HUMINT) is a category of intelligence derived from information collected

698        and provided by human sources (JP 1-02). During COIN operations, much intelligence is based on infor-

699        mation gathered from people, to include informants (HUMINT sources) and captured insurgents. HUMINT

700        is often the primary intelligence category in COIN. Because every Soldier and Marine is a potential collec-

701        tor, any interaction with the populace may result in human intelligence reporting. This means that

702        HUMINT will often be based on information from operational reporting as well as dedicated HUMINT

703        collectors.

704        3-103. The lives of people offering HUMINT are in danger. Insurgents continuously try to defeat ISR

705        collection operations by intimidating or murdering sources. Soldiers and Marines must protect the identity

706        of HUMINT sources.

707        3-104. U.S. forces should not expect people to willingly provide information if insurgents have the ability

708        to violently intimidate them. HUMINT reporting increases if COIN forces protect the populace from the

709        insurgents and people begin to believe the insurgents will lose the conflict. To maintain increased coopera-

710        tion from the people, it is very important that insurgents not be allowed to return and murder or intimidate

711        them. If insurgents do return to an area, COIN forces lose credibility with its inhabitants.

712        3-105. Residents may approach patrols and offer them information. Soldiers and Marines should take the

713        information offered and, if appropriate, establish a means of contacting the individual. Patrol members re-

714        ceiving the information should then report it as part of their debriefing. If possible, trained HUMINT per-

715        sonnel should be involved in any contacting of the source. This is to ensure the safety of both U.S. person-

716        nel and local residents.

717        3-106. People will often provide inaccurate information to Soldiers and Marines. They may simply be

718        spreading rumors or providing inaccurate information purposefully to use U.S. forces to settle tribal, eth-

719        nic, or business disputes. In some cases, insurgents provide bad information to Soldiers and Marines to

720        lead them into ambushes, to get them to undertake an operation that will upset the population, to learn

721        about U.S. planning time and tactical procedures, or to stretch U.S. forces thin by causing them to react to

722        false reports. For these reasons, the accuracy of HUMINT should be verified before being used to support

723        direct action.

724 HUMAN INTELLIGENCE NETWORKS

725        3-107. An effective means of HUMINT collection is to build a network of HUMINT sources. A

726        HUMINT network provides the COIN equivalent of the reconnaissance and surveillance conducted by

727        scouts in conventional operations. These networks serve as “eyes and ears” on the street and provide an

728        early warning system for tracking insurgent activity. Although Soldiers and Marines will regularly get

729        “walk-in” or “walk-up” intelligence reports from citizens, the only counterintelligence personnel are

730        trained and authorized personnel to operate a HUMINT network. Due to legal considerations, the potential

731        danger informants face if identified, and the potential danger to troops involved, only counterintelligence

732        personnel operate a HUMINT networks and develop HUMINT sources. All Soldiers and Marines may re-

733        cord information given to them by walk-up informants or contacts with whom they have a regular liaison

734        relationship; however, they may not develop HUMINT sources or networks. (When published, FM 2-22.3

735        will provide complete coverage of HUMINT operations.)

736 NONTRADITIONAL HUMAN INTELLIGENCE SOURCES

737        3-108. As previously stated, the interactions of any Soldier and Marine with the populace can generate

738        HUMINT reporting if troops know PIRs and conduct after-action debriefings. It is therefore imperative

739        that Soldiers and Marines know what information is reportable and that every patrol is debriefed. These

740        debriefings should be as detailed as possible in order to ensure PIRs are accurately answered. In addition,

741        because of their continuous contact with the populace, Soldiers and Marines regularly generate new poten-

742        tial sources for HUMINT personnel to develop. Analysts and HUMINT collectors should work closely

     3-16                            FM 3-24/FMFM 3-24            16 June 2006 (FINAL DRAFT)


---

     Intelligence in Counterinsurgency (FINAL DRAFT—NOT FOR IMPLEMENTATION)

743  with operations staffs and other personnel to ensure new sources are properly developed. Some valuable

744  sources of HUMINT include the following:

745  z Patrol debriefings and after-action reviews. Patrols regularly encounter individuals offering

746  information and observe new enemy tactics and techniques. Patrol debriefings are especially

747  important to units at brigade level and below, though the information collected can be of higher-

748  echelon significance.

749  z Civil affairs action reports. These are especially useful for gathering information about poli-

750  tics, economy, and infrastructure. Civil affairs personnel also regularly come into contact with

751  individuals offering information.

752  z Psychological operations (PSYOP) action reports. PSYOP personnel conduct opinion polls

753  and gather information on community attitudes, perceptions, and interests/grievances. PSYOP

754  personnel also regularly encounter individuals offering information.

755  z Special forces reporting. Special forces often work closely with local nationals and produce

756  valuable HUMINT reports.

757  z Leadership liaison. Commanders and leaders regularly meet with their counterparts in the HN

758  security forces and with community leaders. These meetings often result in the passing of in-

759  formation or tips.

760  z Contracting. Contracting officers work with theater contractors, both HN and external, per-

761  forming support functions or building national infrastructure. Contractors may offer information

762  to contracting officers.

763  z Multinational operations centers. These provide a venue for sharing information between the

764  host nation and U.S. personnel.

765  z Tips hotlines. Telephone or e-mail hotlines provide a means for local citizens to provide infor-

766  mation without undue exposure to insurgent retribution. They are especially useful for time-

767  sensitive intelligence, such as warning of an attack, or the current location of an insurgent.

768  z U.S. persons. There will be times when U.S. civilians, such as contractors or journalists, offer

769  information to Soldiers and Marines. For legal reasons, it is important to understand regulations

770  regarding intelligence-related information collected from U.S. persons. In addition, military in-

771  telligence personnel cannot collect information concerning U.S. persons. (See FM 2-22.3, when

772  published.)

773 CAPTURED INSURGENTS AND DEFECTORS

774  3-109. Captured insurgents and defectors from the insurgency are a very important source of HUMINT.

775  The information they provide about the internal workings of an insurgency are usually better than any other

776  HUMINT source can provide. In addition, they have valuable insight into the perceptions, motivations,

777  goals, morale, organization, and tactics of an insurgent organization. Both captured insurgents and defec-

778  tors should be thoroughly questioned on all aspects of an insurgency discussed in the IPB discussion,

779  above. The answers provided should be compared with captured equipment, pocket litter, and documents

780  to build a better understanding of the insurgency. While properly trained Soldiers and Marines can conduct

781  immediate tactical questioning of captured insurgents or defectors, legally only trained HUMINT personnel

782  can thoroughly interrogate such subjects. (Refer to Chapter 7 and appendix D of this manual, FM 34-52,

783  and FM 2-22.3 [when published] for more information on interrogation of captured personnel.)

784 SURVEILLANCE AND RECONNAISSANCE CONSIDERATIONS

785  3-110. Because all Soldiers and Marines are potential collectors, all day-to-day tactical operations are ad-

786  dressed in the ISR plan. This means every patrol or mission should be provided intelligence collection re-

787  quirements in addition to operations requirements.

788  3-111. Overt area and zone reconnaissances are excellent means for tactical units to learn more about

789  their AO, to include the terrain, infrastructure, people, government, local leaders, and insurgents. Overt re-

790  connaissance by patrols allows commanders to fill intelligence gaps and develop relationships with local

791  leaders, while simultaneously providing security to the populace.

     16 June 2006 (FINAL DRAFT)  FM3-24/FMFM 3-24                                                    3-17


---

     Chapter 3 (FINAL DRAFT—NOT FOR IMPLEMENTATION)

792        3-112. Covert reconnaissance and surveillance operations employing scouts or concealed observation

793        posts are often ineffective in urban areas, suburban areas, close-knit communities, and other places where

794        the populace is alert and suspicious of outsiders. Under these circumstances, it is very difficult for scouts to

795        conduct reconnaissance or surveillance without being observed by insurgents or people who may tip off in-

796        surgents. It is important that commanders and collection managers understand that reconnaissance of a tar-

797        get may be noticed and cause insurgents to leave the area. Likewise, small groups of scouts may be attrac-

798        tive targets for insurgent attacks if the scouts’ location is known. For these reasons, the use of a HUMINT

799        network or aerial imagery intelligence platforms is often preferable to ground-based reconnaissance and

800        surveillance. Successful ground reconnaissance in populated areas requires leaders to be creative in how

801        they put personnel in place. For instance, dismounted movements at night, leaving a small “stay behind”

802        observation post while the rest of a patrol moves on, or secretly photographing a place of interest while

803        driving by may all be effective. However, commanders weigh the benefits of these operations with the po-

804        tential cost of insurgents having early warning of counterinsurgent intentions.

805 CONSIDERATIONS FOR OTHER INTELLIGENCE DISCIPLINES

806        3-113. An intelligence discipline is a well-defined area of intelligence collection, processing, exploitation,

807        and reporting using a specific category of technical or human resources (JP 1-02). The following discus-

808        sion addresses COIN-specific considerations for selected intelligence disciplines and information types.

809 SIGNALS INTELLIGENCE

810        3-114. In conventional environments, signals intelligence (SIGINT) collection is a good source for enemy

811        locations, intentions, capabilities, and morale. The same applies in COIN operations. SIGINT is often help-

812        ful for confirming or denying HUMINT reporting and may be the primary source of intelligence in areas

813        under insurgent control. Pushing SIGINT collection platforms down to tactical units can therefore have

814        positive impacts on intelligence collection.

815 OPEN-SOURCE INTELLIGENCE

816        3-115. Open-source intelligence (OSINT) is a valuable tool for understanding the environment. It is often

817        more useful than any other discipline for understanding public attitudes and public support for insurgents

818        and counterinsurgents. OSINT is also an important means of determining the effectiveness of information

819        operations. Monitoring a wide variety of media in multiple languages benefits the COIN effort. If possible,

820        this should occur at every echelon with collection requirements, varying by the kinds of media that are im-

821        portant at each level. For instance, reporting by major news networks often matters a great deal at the thea-

822        ter level, while local newspapers or radio stations may be of importance to a tactical unit.

823 IMAGERY INTELLIGENCE

824        3-116. Imagery intelligence (IMINT): In COIN operations, imagery platforms may be used to conduct

825        surveillance of likely insurgent safe houses and other facilities. Further, aerial IMINT platforms are also ef-

826        fective at detecting unusual movements of personnel and supplies, which can help commanders determine

827        where best to interdict.

828        3-117. Static imagery, such as aerial photos of facilities, is useful in operational planning and for detect-

829        ing long-term changes in structures or activities.

830        3-118. Real-time video, often from aerial surveillance platforms, is critical to assessing whether particular

831        locations are likely sites of insurgent activity and may be used to track insurgents during operations. If

832        flown high enough so insurgents cannot hear the platform, real-time video provides surveillance in areas

833        where it is difficult or impossible to use an observation post.

834 TARGET EXPLOITATION AND DOCUMENT EXPLOITATION

835        3-119. Documents and pocket litter, as well as information found in computers and cell phones, can pro-

836        vide critical information analysts need to map insurgent organization, capabilities and intent. Target Ex-

     3-18                            FM 3-24/FMFM 3-24                      16 June 2006 (FINAL DRAFT)


---

     Intelligence in Counterinsurgency (FINAL DRAFT—NOT FOR IMPLEMENTATION)

837  ploitation (TAREX) and document exploitation (DOCEX) are also of great benefit to interrogators in sub-

838  stantiating what detainees know and whether they are telling the truth.

839  3-120. TAREX in a COIN environment is like evidence collection in a law enforcement environment.

840  Units must have procedures in place to ensure captured equipment and documents are tracked accurately

841  and attached to the correct insurgents. While this evidence may not need to be sufficient to convict in a

842  court of law (though in some cases it does), it needs to be sufficient to justify the use of operational re-

843  sources to apprehend the individuals in question. Pushing HUMINT or law enforcement personnel to the

844  battalion level and below can improve TAREX/DOCEX by tactical units. Procedures for ensuring that tac-

845  tical units get the results of higher-level TAREX/DOCEX are also important. Units must be able to receive

846  intelligence collected from the documents, equipment, and personnel they capture in a timely manner to

847  fully benefit from the operation and adapt to their local situation.

848 PROPERTY OWNERSHIP RECORDS

849  3-121. Property ownership records include census records, deeds, and other means of determining owner-

850  ship of land and buildings. They help counterinsurgents to determine who should or should not be living in

851  a specific area and aid in securing the populace. In some cases, it may be necessary for Soldiers and Ma-

852  rines to go door to door and collect census data themselves.

853 TECHNICAL INTELLIGENCE

854  3-122. Insurgents often adapt their tactics, techniques, and procedures rapidly. This may include the use

855  of improvised explosive devices, homemade mortars, and other pieces of customized military equipment.

856  Technical intelligence on insurgent equipment is of benefit for understanding their capabilities.

857 MEASUREMENT AND SIGNATURES INTELLIGENCE

858  3-123. Measurement and signatures intelligence sensors provide remote monitoring of avenues of ap-

859  proach or border regions for smugglers or insurgents. They can also be used to locate insurgent safe havens

860  and cache sites, aid in targeting, and determining insurgent activities and capabilities.

861 SECTION IV – COUNTERINTELLIGENCE AND COUNTERRECONNAISSANCE

862  3-124. Insurgents place heavy emphasis on gathering intelligence. They do so using informants, double

863  agents, reconnaissance, surveillance, open-source media, and open-source imagery. Insurgents can poten-

864  tially use any person interacting with U.S. or multinational personnel as an informant. These include all of

865  the same people that U.S. forces use as potential HUMINT sources. This makes operations security very

866  important. It also means U.S. personnel must carefully screen the contractors, informants, translators, and

867  other personnel working with them.

868  3-125. Background screenings should include the collection of personal and biometric data and a search

869  through available reporting databases to determine that the person is not an insurgent. Identification badges

870  may be useful for local nationals working on U.S. and HN government facilities. However, these badges

871  may be forged or stolen, and insurgents can use them to identify people working with the government.

872  Therefore, biometrics is preferable, when available.

873  3-126. Insurgents, criminals, political organizations, and disaffected individuals may also provide U.S.

874  personnel with false information. They do so to create animosity between troops and the populace, to get

875  Soldiers and Marines to act against local enemies, to learn about the reaction time and patterns of U.S. op-

876  erations, and to confuse the COP. This means HUMINT collectors should screen informants and get feed-

877  back from analysts and operations personnel on the accuracy of intelligence provided.

878  3-127. Insurgents have their own reconnaissance and surveillance networks. Because they usually blend

879  well with the populace, they can execute reconnaissance of targets without easily being identified. They

880  also have an early warning system composed of citizens who inform them of U.S. movements. Identifying

881  the techniques and weaknesses of enemy reconnaissance and surveillance enables commanders to detect

882  signs of insurgent preparations and to surprise insurgents by neutralizing their early warning system.

     16 June 2006 (FINAL DRAFT)          FM3-24/FMFM 3-24                                                    3-19


---

     Chapter 3 (FINAL DRAFT—NOT FOR IMPLEMENTATION)

883        3-128. Insurgents may also have a SIGINT capability based on commercially available scanners and ra-

884        dios, wire taps, or captured U.S. equipment. If Soldiers and Marines use unencrypted radios or phones, it is

885        possible for insurgents to collect information from them. From an operations security standpoint, it is there-

886        fore preferable for troops to not use commercial radios or phones. If Soldiers and Marines have to use

887        commercial equipment or unencrypted communications, they should employ brevity codes to reduce insur-

888        gents’ ability to collect on them.

889 SECTION V – ANALYSIS

890        3-129. The purpose of analysis is to fuse raw intelligence into products that support COIN operations. In-

891        telligence analysis in COIN is very challenging. This is due to the need to understand perceptions and cul-

892        ture, the need to track hundreds or thousands of personalities, the local nature of insurgencies, and the ten-

893        dency of insurgencies to change over time.

894        3-130. Databases are very important for analyzing insurgent activities and personalities. At a minimum,

895        there should be a common theater database of insurgent actions and another containing all intelligence re-

896        porting. These should be accessible by all analysts in theater and include all reporting from all units and

897        organizations in theater. This is to ensure a common operational picture can be developed.

898        3-131. Because all echelons collect and use intelligence, all staffs are heavily involved in analysis. Units

899        are simultaneously intelligence producers and consumers. While this is normal at brigade and above, bat-

900        talion staffs often do not have the personnel to collect patrol debriefs, analyze incoming intelligence from

901        multiple sources, produce finished intelligence products, and disseminate products to the appropriate con-

902        sumers. In many cases brigade intelligence sections may also not be adequate for a COIN environment.

903        3-132. Pushing additional analysts down to battalion and brigade staffs is important to ensure tactical

904        units have the analytical support they require. There are instances when an analyst can be beneficial at the

905        company level. This is the case when a maneuver company must collect large amounts of information on

906        the local populace and insurgents. An analyst can aid the company commander and leaders in the collection

907        and processing of this information and develop an operational picture of the AO. Pushing analysts down to

908        the brigade level and below places analysts closer to collectors, improves the COP, and helps ensure higher

909        echelon staffs receive answers to their PIRs. If no additional analysts are available, commanders may as-

910        sign nonintelligence personnel to work in the intelligence section.

911        3-133. Tactical analysis at the brigade and below is the basis for operational-level intelligence developed

912        at higher echelons. This is due to the bottom-up flow of intelligence in COIN. Battalions and brigades de-

913        velop intelligence for their AOs, while higher echelons fuse it into theaterwide intelligence of the insur-

914        gency. Operational-level intelligence adds national and international politics and their effects on the opera-

915        tional environment.

916        3-134. There are two basic kinds of analysis done at all echelons: analysis of enemy actions and network

917        analysis. Analysis of enemy actions is commonly known as current operations. It focuses on what the en-

918        emy is doing now. Network analysis focuses on the people in an AO and develops an understanding of in-

919        terrelationships between them and the ideas and beliefs driving their actions. Generally speaking, current-

920        operations information provides threat warning and metrics of enemy capabilities, while network analysis

921        provides intelligence for targeting and planning.

922 CURRENT OPERATIONS

923        3-135. Current operations intelligence supports a commander’s understanding of what the enemy is cur-

924        rently doing. The basic tasks of analysts working in current operations are—

925        z Conduct analysis on past and current enemy actions (event-pattern analysis) and look for

926        changes in insurgent tactics or strategy.

927        z Track the effects of friendly operations on the populace and enemy.

928        z Provide intelligence support to ongoing operations.

929        z Disseminate immediate threat warnings to appropriate consumers.

     3-20                                      FM 3-24/FMFM 3-24                         16 June 2006 (FINAL DRAFT)


---

                Intelligence in Counterinsurgency (FINAL DRAFT—NOT FOR IMPLEMENTATION)

930  3-136. Intelligence for current operations comes from a variety of sources, but operations reports are par-

931  ticularly important. This is because current enemy activities are more often reported by patrols, units con-

932  ducting raids, or observation posts than they are by dedicated intelligence collectors. OSINT will also be

933  important for tracking the effects of information operations. Current operations analysis depends on the in-

934  surgent actions database for determining changes in insurgent tactics and techniques.

935 NETWORK ANALYSIS

936  3-137. The purpose of network analysis is to provide commanders with an understanding of the insur-

937  gency and what is driving it. Network analysis requires a large investment of time. Analysts may have to

938  spend weeks or months going through large amounts of multiple-source intelligence reporting in order to

939  provide an accurate picture of insurgent groups. The investment is worth it, however. The more accurate

940  and thorough the intelligence on insurgent organizations and the operational environment, the more effec-

941  tive friendly operations become. It is easy for intelligence personnel to be drawn into current operations

942  due to the time-sensitive and high-profile demands of such activity. However, commanders must ensure

943  that network analysis still occurs. The tasks of network analysts are to—

944  z Identify insurgent goals and motivations.

945  z Identify the grievances exploited by insurgents.

946  z Determine how culture, interests, and history inform insurgent and HN decision making.

947  z Understand the links between political, religious, tribal, criminal, and other social networks.

948  z Determine how various networks and groups interact with insurgent networks.

949  z Determine the structure and function of insurgent organizations.

950  z Identify key insurgent activities and leaders.

951  z Understand popular and insurgent perceptions of the host nation, the insurgency, and U.S. mili-

952  tary forces and how these affect the insurgency.

953  3-138. There are many techniques and tools used in network analysis. What makes them all similar is that

954  they examine interactions among individuals, groups, and beliefs within the historic and cultural context of

955  the operational environment. One of the more important products of network analysis is an understanding

956  of how the people in an AO think. This knowledge allows predictive analysis of enemy actions along with

957  the development of effective information operations and civil-military operations. Such cultural analysis is

958  difficult because it requires a Soldier or Marine to understand the mindset of an insurgent or average citi-

959  zen. However, this knowledge is critical to ensuring operations are effective. (Network analysis is covered

960  in more depth in appendix E.)

961 INTELLIGENCE REACH

962  3-139. Intelligence reach (sometimes called reachback) refers to the ability to exploit resources, capabili-

963  ties, and expertise not physically located in the theater of operations. Deployed or deploying units often use

964  reach capabilities to “outsource” time-intensive aspects of network analysis or socio-cultural factors analy-

965  sis. This is particularly useful when deployments occur with little warning and when organizations used for

966  reach have a great deal of expertise available on a given subject. Analysts may receive reach assistance

967  from the commands above them or from external sources. Many of the resource organizations exist in the

968  continental U.S. and are connected to DOD, while others are not. Most organizations affiliated with DOD

969  regard assisting field commanders as one of their primary missions. Lists of reach organizations and their

970  expertise are available from the Center for Army Lessons Learned at Fort Leavenworth, Kansas <

971  http://call.army.mil/>.

972 CONTINUITY

973  3-140. The complexity of analyzing an insurgency means it often takes analysts months to fully under-

974  stand the operational environment and the insurgency. For this reason, analysts should maintain situational

975  awareness of the insurgency for as long as possible. This can be accomplished by having intelligence and

     16 June 2006 (FINAL DRAFT)     FM3-24/FMFM 3-24                                                    3-21


---

      Chapter 3 (FINAL DRAFT—NOT FOR IMPLEMENTATION)

 976       other staff sections track the fight from their home station. This flattens the learning curve of units rotating
 977       in to a theater and increases their effectiveness during the critical first months of deployment.

 978    SECTION VI – INTELLIGENCE COLLABORATION AND FUSION

 979       3-141. The purpose of intelligence collaboration and fusion is to organize the collection and analytical ef-
 980       forts of various units and organizations into a coherent and mutually supportive intelligence effort. The
 981       common operational picture for an insurgency is complex. Insurgencies don’t normally lend themselves to
 982       generalizations like “if this leader is removed the insurgency is over” or “this group drives the insurgency.”
 983       It is important not to oversimplify an insurgency. However, analysts and commanders still require a means
 984       of defining and describing the enemy that can be commonly understood. One means of aiding this process
 985       is to track and report the insurgency by region, insurgent organization, key personalities, or by insurgent
 986       goals and motivations.

 987       3-142. As noted earlier, insurgencies are often localized but have national or international aspects to
 988       them. This complicates intelligence fusion between adjacent units and various echelons. For instance, if
 989       numerous insurgent groups operate in one country, adjacent battalions may face very different threats. The
 990       higher-echelon analysts must then understand multiple insurgent organizations and determine what links
 991       there are, if any, between them. Usually, battalions focus on the population and insurgents in their AO.
 992       Higher-echelon analysts determine links and interactions among the people and insurgents across unit
 993       boundaries. Theater-level analysts determine the major linkages within the theater and internationally.
 994       Based on these requirements, a common database based on intelligence reporting is a prerequisite for effec-
 995       tive intelligence fusion.

 996       3-143. Also complicating fusion is the fact that many government agencies and foreign security forces are
 997       involved in COIN operations. Analysts must establish good working relationships with various agencies in
 998       order to ensure they are able to fuse intelligence.

 999  INTELLIGENCE CELL AND WORKING GROUPS

1000       3-144. Intelligence community assets operating in an AO work in or coordinate with the intelligence cell
1001       in one of the unit’s command posts under the staff supervision of the unit intelligence officer. Examples of
1002       intelligence community assets operating in a division AO include the following:
1003
1004               z Defense Intelligence Agency.
1005                     Defense HUMINT case officers.
1006                     Reports Officers.
1007                     Document exploitation teams.
1008
1009               z Central Intelligence Agency.
1010                     Chief of base.
1011                     Case officers.
1012                     Reports officers.
1013
1014               z Air Force Office of Special Investigations special agents.
1015               z U.S. Special Operations Command.
1016
1017                     Special forces operational detachment–alpha teams.
1018                     Special mission units.
1019                     Civil Affairs teams.
1020                     PSYOPS teams.
1021               z Federal Bureau of Investigation (FBI) Agents.
                   z Immigration and Customs Enforcement/Department of Homeland Security agents.
                   z State Department political advisor.
                   z National intelligence support team.
                   z National Security Agency.

      3-22  FM 3-24/FMFM 3-24                         16 June 2006 (FINAL DRAFT)


---

      Intelligence in Counterinsurgency (FINAL DRAFT—NOT FOR IMPLEMENTATION)

1022               z National Geospatial-Intelligence Agency.
1023               z Treasury Department.

1024       3-145. As necessary, intelligence officers form working groups/boards to synchronize collection, analy-
1025       sis, and targeting efforts. (See FMI 5-0.1, paragraphs 2-30, 2-38, and 2-43.) Cells and working groups con-
1026       duct regular meetings to establish and maintain joint situational awareness, share collection priorities, de-
1027       conflict activities and operations, discuss target development, and share results of operations. These
1028       meetings build mutual trust and understanding of each member’s mission, capabilities, and limitations.
1029       They are integrated with meetings of other staff cells and working groups/boards (for example, the target-
1030       ing board) as part of the command post’s battle rhythm established by the chief of staff. (See FMI 5-0.1,
1031       paragraphs 2-89 through 2-91.)

1032       3-146. An effective intelligence cell enhances the commander’s knowledge of enemy activity, local pecu-
1033       liarities, and friendly forces operating in the unit’s AO. Incorporating HN representatives (for example, in-
1034       telligence services, military forces, and local government officials) and multinational partners into the in-
1035       telligence cell should also be considered in a COIN environment to foster teamwork, gain insight into local
1036       customs and activities, and prepare the host nation to assume the mission when multinational forces depart
1037       the area.

1038  PROTECTING SOURCES

1039       3-147. Protection of sources is another important consideration when sharing intelligence. Acting on in-
1040       telligence can compromise its sources. This is why organizations may sometimes choose to not share intel-
1041       ligence. However, use of the targeting process to synchronize targeting decisions is usually a better means
1042       of protecting sources. This is because the targeting board draws on information from the COP rather than
1043       specific sources. (See the targeting discussion in chapter 5.)

1044  HOST-NATION INTEGRATION

1045       3-148. COIN operations require U.S. personnel to work closely with the host nation. Sharing of intelli-
1046       gence with HN security forces and government personnel is an important and effective means of support-
1047       ing their COIN efforts. However, it should be noted that HN intelligence services may not be well devel-
1048       oped and their personnel may not be well trained. For this reason, HN intelligence should be seen as
1049       another useful source of intelligence, but definitely not as the only one, and usually not as the most impor-
1050       tant one. It is essential for U.S. personnel to evaluate the HN’s intelligence capabilities and offer training,
1051       as required.

1052       3-149. In addition, infiltration of the HN security forces by insurgents or foreign intelligence services can
1053       create drawbacks to intelligence sharing. Insurgents may learn what is known about them, gain insight into
1054       intelligence sources and capabilities, and get early warning of targeting efforts.

1055       3-150. When sharing intelligence with the host nation, it is important to understand the level of infiltra-
1056       tion by insurgents or foreign intelligence services. Insofar as possible, intelligence should be tailored so re-
1057       quired intelligence still gets to the HN consumers, but it does not give away information about sources and
1058       capabilities. In addition, care should be taken when providing targeting information and it should be done
1059       in such a way that insurgents do not receive early warning of an upcoming operation. As trust develops be-
1060       tween the HN and U.S. personnel, the amount of intelligence shared should grow. This will make the
1061       COIN effort more effective.

1062    SECTION VII – SUMMARY

1063       3-151. What makes intelligence analysis for COIN so distinct and so challenging is the amount of cultural
1064       information that must be gathered and understood. However, to truly grasp the environment of operations,
1065       commanders and their staffs must expend at least as much effort understanding the people they are support-
1066       ing as the enemy. All this information is essential to get at the root causes of the insurgency and to deter-
1067       mine the best ways to combat it. Identifying the real problem and developing solutions is the essence of the
1068       operational design process that is discussed in the next chapter.
