     Chapter 5 (FINAL DRAFT—NOT FOR IMPLEMENTATION)

114       Figure 5-1. Common counterinsurgency operational-level logical lines of operations

     5-4  FM 3-24/FMFM 3-24                          16 June 2006 (FINAL DRAFT)


---

                Executing Counterinsurgency Operations (FINAL DRAFT—NOT FOR IMPLEMENTATION)

115  5-12. Commanders determine which LLOs are applicable to their operational environment and how they

116  connect with and support one another. For example, establishing continuous security for the public as part

117  of stability operations behind a shield of offensive and defensive operations creates a safe environment in

118  which activities for life support and reconstruction operations can take place. People may perceive that the

119  environment is safe enough to leave their family at home while they seek employment or conduct public

120  economic activity. This perception facilitates the further provision of essential services and development of

121  greater economic activity, and can facilitate the attraction of outside capital for further development. Ne-

122  glecting one LLO will inevitably create vulnerable conditions in another LLO that the insurgency can ex-

123  ploit. Achieving the desired end state requires linked successes on all LLOs. At the operational level,

124  proper execution along the LLOs should lead to increased popular support for a legitimate government.

125  (See figure 5-2.)

126

127  Figure 5-2. The effect of proper application of LLOs in counterinsurgency

128  5-13. The LLOs are analogous to a rope. Each LLO is a separate string, which by itself may be relatively

129  weak and incapable of addressing the weight of issues in a COIN operation. However, a strong rope is cre-

130  ated when strands are woven together. The overall COIN effort is further strengthened through information

131  operations, which support and enhance the operation by highlighting the successes in each LLO. (See Fig-

132  ure 5-3.)

     16 June 2006 (FINAL DRAFT)  FM3-24/FMFM 3-24                               5-5


---

     Chapter 5 (FINAL DRAFT—NOT FOR IMPLEMENTATION)

133       Figure 5-3. The strengthening effect of interrelated LLOs

134       5-14. Using LLOs, tactical commanders develop missions and tasks, allocate resources, and assess the ef-

135       fectiveness of operations. The commander ought to specify the LLO that is the decisive operation and

136       those that have a shaping role. This prioritization usually changes as insurgency vulnerabilities are created,

137       the insurgency reacts or adjusts its activities, or the environment evolves. In this sense, commanders adapt

138       their operations not only to the state of the insurgency, but also to the condition of the overall environment,

139       which ideally should be moving toward greater stability.

140       5-15. LLOs are mutually supportive between echelons as well as adjacent organizations. For example,

141       similar LLOs among brigade combat teams produce complementary effects, while the collective set of bri-

142       gade accomplishments reinforce the goals at the division level. LLOs are normally used at brigade and

143       higher levels, where the leadership, staff, and unit resources are sufficiently robust to plan and employ

144       them; however, battalions can use the LLOs in a similar fashion to that of their higher headquarters. Com-

145       manders at various levels may expect their subordinate commanders to converse with them in these terms.

146       5-16. LLOs are formulated and coordinated during the course of action development step of the military

147       decision-making process. Beginning with the broadly defined end state, conditions for success, and other

148       aspects of the commander’s intent, the commander and staff perform a mission analysis of the situation and

149       environment to determine the objectives necessary to set those conditions and achieve the intent. Related

150       objectives are grouped based on their purpose to ensure unity of effort. If decisive actions can be identified,

151       these are expressed within the context of an LLO, and a logical connection is made between these actions

152       deemed decisive and the commander’s intent. The operational design, including the LLOs, is translated

153       into missions, objectives, and tasks, and then assigned to subordinate commanders.

154       The Importance of Multiple Lines of operations in COIN

155

156       In China during 1945, Chiang Kai-Sheik adopted a strategy to secure and defend the

157       coastal financial and industrial centers from the communist insurgency led by Mao

158       Zedong. These areas had been the Chinese government’s prewar core support ar-

159       eas. Although a logical plan, this strategy suffered in implementation. Republic of

160       China administration and military forces were often corrupt and did not provide good

161       governance and security or facilitate the provision of essential services to the people.

162       Furthermore, the government, because of an insufficient number of soldiers, was

163       forced to rely on warlord forces, which lacked quality and discipline. Their actions

164       undermined the legitimacy of and popular support for the government within the very

165       core areas vital to government rejuvenation. Likewise, when government forces at-

166       tempted to re-establish their presence in the Chinese countryside, their undisciplined

167       conduct towards the rural citizenry further delegitimized the Chinese government.

168       Because of these actions, Chiang’s forces were unable to secure or expand their

169       support base.

     5-6                 FM 3-24/FMFM 3-24                          16 June 2006 (FINAL DRAFT)


---

             Executing Counterinsurgency Operations (FINAL DRAFT—NOT FOR IMPLEMENTATION)

170  As a result there was increasing lack of material and political support for the govern-

171  ment, whose legitimacy was undercut. The government’s inability to enforce ethical

172  adherence to the rule of law by its officials and forces, combined with widespread

173  corruption and economic collapse, served to move millions from being supporters

174  into the undecided middle. When economic chaos eliminated any ability of the gov-

175  ernment to fund even those efforts that were proper and justified, an insurgent victory

176  led by the Chinese Communist Party became inevitable.

177  As government defeat followed defeat, a collapse of morale magnified the impact of

178  material shortages. Chiang’s defeat in Manchuria, in particular, created a psychologi-

179  cal loss of support within China, caused economic dislocation due to substantial price

180  inflation of foodstuffs, sowed discord and dissension among government allies, and

181  generated increased though fragmented international support for the Chinese Com-

182  munist Party. As the regime lost moral authority, it also faced a decreasing ability to

183  govern. All these factors served to create a mythical yet very powerful psychological

184  impression that the success of the Chinese Communist Party was historically inevi-

185  table. The failure of the leaders of the Republic of China to address the requirements

186  of lines of operations like good governance, economic development, and essential

187  services magnified their military shortcomings and forced their abandonment of the

188  Chinese mainland.

189 COMMON LOGICAL LINES OF OPERATIONS IN
190 COUNTERINSURGENCY

191  5-17. Commanders at all levels should select the LLOs that relate best to what they desire to achieve in the

192  manner they envision achieving it. This following list is not all inclusive, but gives commanders a place to

193  start:

194          z Information.

195          z Combat operations.

196          z Development of HN security forces.

197          z Essential services.

198          z Governance.

199          z Economic development.

200  These lines can be customized, renamed, changed altogether, or simply not used. LLOs should be used to

201  isolate the insurgency from the population, address and correct the root causes of the insurgency, and cre-

202  ate or reinforce the societal systems required to sustain the legitimacy of the HN government. The sections

203  that follow discuss six common LLOs that appear in most operations. Though information operations (IO)

204  is a separate strand in the coil of LLOs, it is probably the most important, since it is interwoven throughout

205  all the others and surrounds them.

206 INFORMATION

207  5-18. The information LLO is decisive in that it assists in setting conditions for success of all other LLOs.

208  By publicizing government policies, the actual situation, and counterinsurgent accomplishments, IO can

209  neutralize insurgent propaganda and false claims. The major task categories follow:

210          z Identify all the audiences (local, regional, and international), the various news cycles, and how

211          to reach them with the HN government’s message.

212          z Manage local population expectations regarding what COIN forces can achieve.

213          z Develop common, multiechelon themes based on and consistent with HN government policies

214          and the operation’s objectives, and sustain unity of the message.

215          z Coordinate and provide a comprehensive information preparation of the operational environ-

216          ment using all communications means and incorporating the activities of all other LLOs.

     16 June 2006 (FINAL DRAFT)          FM3-24/FMFM 3-24                                             5-7


---

     Chapter 5 (FINAL DRAFT—NOT FOR IMPLEMENTATION)

217       z Remember actions always speak louder than words—every Soldier and Marine is an integral

218       part of IO communications. IO are executed every day through the actions of firm, fair, profes-

219       sional, and alert Soldiers and Marines on the streets with the people.

220 Information Operations

221       5-19. Commanders and staffs orchestrate IO in harmony and cooperation with the activities in the other

222       LLOs. The information LLO should address and manage the public’s expectations by explaining what the

223       HN government and COIN forces are doing and why. The information LLO can effectively address the

224       subject of root causes the insurgency uses to gain support.

225       5-20. Information should be localized to address the concerns of the inhabitants of each AO. IO should

226       educate the public regarding successfully completed projects and improvements within all LLOs, including

227       accomplishments in security, infrastructure, essential services, and economic development. This informa-

228       tion facilitates popular acceptance of the legitimacy of the HN government.

229       5-21. The information LLO should use consistent themes based on policy, facts, and deeds—not claims or

230       future plans, because these can be thwarted. Themes must be reinforced by actions along all LLOs. Making

231       unsubstantiated claims can undermine the credibility and legitimacy of the HN government in the long

232       term. COIN forces should never knowingly make a promise or obligate themselves to an action that cannot

233       be completed. However, when a promise cannot be fulfilled, COIN forces should take the initiative and

234       publicly address the reasons why this occurred in order to reduce the negative impact.

235       5-22. Information themes are based on policy and should be distributed simultaneously or as soon as pos-

236       sible using all available means, such as radio, television, newspapers, flyers, billboards, and the Internet.

237       Polling and analysis should be conducted to determine which media allow the widest dissemination of the

238       themes to the desired audiences at the local, regional, national, and international levels.

239       5-23. Insurgencies are not constrained by truth and create propaganda that serves their purpose. Insurgent

240       propaganda may include lying, deception, and creating false causes to gain support for the insurgency. His-

241       torically, insurgencies change their message to address whatever issue gains them support in the changing

242       environment. The information LLO should publicly identify the insurgency’s propaganda and lies to the

243       local populace. Doing so creates doubt in the mind of the uncommitted public and supporters of the insur-

244       gency regarding the viability of the insurgents’ short- and long-term intentions.

245       5-24. Impartiality is a common theme for information activities when there are political, social, and sectar-

246       ian divisions in the host nation. COIN forces should avoid taking sides, if possible, as perceived favoritism

247       can exacerbate civil strife and make the COIN force a more desirable target for sectarian violence.

248 Media and the Battle for Perceptions

249       5-25. Effective commanders directly engage in a dialogue with the media and communicate IO themes.

250       With the proliferation of sophisticated communication technologies throughout the global information en-

251       vironment, the nature of media coverage has a significant impact on COIN operations at all echelons. Ci-

252       vilian and military media coverage influences the perceptions of the political leaders and public in the host

253       nation, the United States, and throughout the international community. The media directly influence the

254       support of key audiences for COIN forces, the execution of their operations, and the opposing insurgency.

255       Recognition of this influence creates a war of perceptions between insurgents and COIN forces that is con-

256       ducted continuously through the communications media.

257       5-26. The media are a permanent part of the information environment and effective media/public affairs

258       operations are critical to successful military operations. Every aspect of a military operation is subject to

259       immediate scrutiny. Well planned, properly coordinated, and clearly expressed IO significantly clear the

260       fog of war and improve the effectiveness and morale of COIN forces, the will of the U.S. public, and the

261       support of the HN people for their government. The right messages can reduce misinformation, distrac-

262       tions, confusion, uncertainty, and other factors that cause public distress and undermine the COIN effort.

263       Constructive and transparent information enhances understanding and support for continuing operations

264       against the insurgency.

     5-8                                  FM 3-24/FMFM 3-24                            16 June 2006 (FINAL DRAFT)


---

     Executing Counterinsurgency Operations (FINAL DRAFT—NOT FOR IMPLEMENTATION)

265  5-27. There are numerous methods available to commanders for working with the media to facilitate accu-

266  rate and timely information flow. These include the following:

267  z Embedded media.

268  z Press conferences.

269  z Applying resources.

270  z Network with media outlets.

271  5-28. Embedded media representatives get to know the Soldiers’ and Marines’ perspectives in the context

272  of the COIN environment. Embedding for days rather than weeks runs the risk of media representatives not

273  gaining any real understanding of the context of operations and may lead to unintended misinformation.

274  Media representatives embedding for weeks become better prepared to present an informed report. The

275  media should be given access to the young men and women in the field. These young people nearly always

276  do a fantastic job articulating the important issues for a broad audience and, given a chance, can share their

277  courage and sense of purpose with the American people and the world.

278  5-29. Weekly press conferences might be held, particularly with HN media, to explain operations and pro-

279  vide transparency to the people most affected by COIN efforts. Such venues also provide an opportunity to

280  highlight the accomplishments of COIN forces and the HN government.

281  5-30. Commanders should apply time, effort, and money to establish the proper combination of media out-

282  lets and communications to transmit the repetitive themes of HN government accomplishments and insur-

283  gent violence against the populace. This might require COIN forces to be proactive, alerting media to news

284  opportunities and perhaps providing transportation or other services to ensure proper coverage. Assisting in

285  the establishment of effective HN media is another important COIN requirement. A word of caution here:

286  there can be no perception by the populace or the HN media that the media is being manipulated by COIN

287  or HN forces. Even the slightest appearance of impropriety here can undermine the credibility of the COIN

288  force and the host nation.

289  5-31. Establishing relationships between COIN leaders and responsible members of the U.S. media is in

290  the interests of the Nation. Without a clear understanding of the COIN efforts, U.S. media representatives

291  relay a message to the American public as best they can. Through these professional relationships, the mili-

292  tary can ensure U.S. citizens better understand what their military is doing in support of the Nation’s inter-

293  ests. Operations security must always be maintained, but that should not be used as an excuse to create a

294  media blackout that can lead to misinformation. Similar relationships can be established with international

295  media sources.

296  5-32. The media are ever present and influence perceptions of the COIN environment. Therefore, success-

297  ful leaders engage the media, create positive relationships, and help the media tell the story. Otherwise the

298  media develops a story of its own that may not be as accurate and may not include the COIN force perspec-

299  tive. (See JP 3-61, FM 46-1, FM 3-61.1.)

300  5-33. In developing the information LLO consider the following:

301  z Consider word choices carefully. Words are important—they have specific meanings and de-

302  scribe policy. For example, is the COIN force a liberator or an occupier? Occupiers generate a

303  “resistance,” whereas liberators may be welcomed for a time. Soldiers and Marines can be influ-

304  enced likewise. In a conflict among the people, terms like “battlefield” influence perceptions

305  and confuse the critical nature of a synchronized approach. Refrain from referring to and con-

306  sidering the area of operations (AO) as a “battlefield” or it may continue to be one.

307  z Publicize insurgent violence and use of terror to discredit the insurgency.

308  z Admit mistakes (or actions perceived as mistakes) quickly, and explain these mistakes and ac-

309  tions—including mistakes committed by COIN forces. However, do not attempt to explain ac-

310  tions by the HN government. Instead encourage HN officials to handle such information them-

311  selves. They know better the cultural implications of their actions, and honesty should help to

312  build legitimacy.

313  z Highlight HN government and COIN force successes promptly. Positive results speak loudly

314  and resonate with people. Do not delay communications waiting for all results. Initiate commu-

     16 June 2006 (FINAL DRAFT)                FM3-24/FMFM 3-24                             5-9


---

     Chapter 5 (FINAL DRAFT—NOT FOR IMPLEMENTATION)

315        nications immediately to let people know what the COIN force is doing and why. Delaying an-

316        nouncements creates “old news” and misses news cycles.

317        z Respond quickly to insurgent propaganda. As stated above, delaying a response can let the in-

318        surgent story dominate many news cycles, allowing their version of events to become wide-

319        spread and accepted. This consideration may require giving increased information assets and re-

320        sponsibilities to lower-level leaders, since they are also at the “point of the spear” for IO.

321        z Shape expectations of the populace. Generally people expect too much too soon, and when the

322        government or COIN force is slow to deliver, people become easily and perhaps unfairly dis-

323        gruntled.

324        z Give the people some means to voice their opinions and grievances, even if that activity appears

325        at first to cause short-term friction with ongoing efforts. This applies not just to the formal po-

326        litical process, but even more to the informal, local issues (where government actually touches

327        the people). Develop a feedback loop from populace to local government to ensure needs are

328        identified and perceptions aligned.

329        z Keep troops engaged with the people. Presence patrols facilitate Soldiers and Marines mingling

330        with the populace. The communication flow works both ways, as the people and COIN forces

331        learn to know each other better.

332        z Conduct ongoing audience analysis and seek to identify key personnel that influence the people

333        at the local, regional, and national levels. Seek to determine with great specificity the relevant

334        lines of loyalty of a population.

335        z Take a census as soon as is practicable. Help the local government do this. This information can

336        be helpful for learning about the people and meeting their needs.

337        z Assist the government in the production and distribution of identification cards. This is an effort

338        to register all citizens—or, at least those nearing a predetermined, adult age. Identification cards

339        may help to track people’s movements, which are useful in identifying illicit activity.

340        z Treat detainees professionally and publicize their treatment. Arrange for local HN leaders to

341        visit and tour your detention facility. Consider allowing them to speak to detainees. If news me-

342        dia visit your detention facility, allow them as much access as is prudent. (Provide a guided tour

343        and explain your procedures.)

344        z Consider initiating a dialogue with the opposition. This does not equate to “negotiating with ter-

345        rorists,” and is an attempt to open the door to mutual understanding. There may be no common

346        ground and the enmity may be such that nothing specifically or directly comes of the dialogue.

347        However, if COIN forces are talking, they are using a positive approach—and may learn some-

348        thing. Do not rely solely on the host nation to do this. Consider adopting a “We understand why

349        you fight” mentality—and maybe stating this to the insurgents.

350        z Work to convince insurgent leadership that the time for resistance has ended and that there are

351        other ways to accomplish what they desire.

352        z Take the insurgents’ demands and turn them on the insurgents. Examine the disputed issues ob-

353        jectively, and then work with the host nation to resolve them where possible. Then communicate

354        any success as a sign of responsiveness and improvement.

355        z Communicate the message that the COIN force is robust and persistent, and will assist the popu-

356        lation through their present difficulty.

357        z Learn the insurgent’s messages or narratives and develop countermessages and counternarra-

358        tives to attack the insurgents’ ideology. An understanding of indigenous culture is required for

359        this activity, and HN personnel can play a key role.

360 COMBAT OPERATIONS

361        5-34. This line of operations is the most familiar to military forces. Care must be taken not to apply too

362        many resources to this LLO at the expense of other LLOs that facilitate the development or reinforcement

363        of the legitimacy of the HN government.

     5-10                                           FM 3-24/FMFM 3-24         16 June 2006 (FINAL DRAFT)


---

     Executing Counterinsurgency Operations (FINAL DRAFT—NOT FOR IMPLEMENTATION)

364  5-35. An inherent trait of an insurgency is the use of unlawful violence to weaken a HN government, in-

365  timidate people into passive or active support, and murder those who oppose the insurgency. There is al-

366  ways some element of measured combat operations required to address insurgents who cannot be co-opted

367  into operating inside the rule of law. However, COIN is “war amongst the people.” Combat operations

368  must therefore be executed with an appropriate level of restraint on the use of force to minimize or avoid

369  killing or injuring innocent people who are not involved with the conflict. Not only is there a moral basis

370  for the use of restraint or measured force; there are practical reasons as well. The COIN force does not

371  want to turn the will of the people against the COIN effort by harming innocents. Discriminating use of

372  fires and calculated, disciplined response should characterize performance in COIN, where kindness and

373  compassion can be as important as killing and capturing.

374  5-36. In general, battalion-sized and smaller unit operations are often the most effective for countering in-

375  surgent activities. The COIN force needs to get as close as possible to the people to secure them, glean the

376  maximum amount of quality information, and thereby gain a fluidity of action equal or superior to that of

377  the enemy. This does not mean larger unit operations are not required. The brigade is usually a synchroniz-

378  ing headquarters while the division shapes the environment to set conditions and facilitate brigade and bat-

379  talion success. The sooner the COIN force can execute small unit operations effectively, the better.

380  5-37. In developing this LLO, consider the following:

381  z Develop cultural intelligence, which assumes a prominent role. Make every effort to learn as

382  much about the environment as possible. Human dynamics tend to matter the most.

383  z Ensure that rules of engagement adequately guide Soldiers and Marines engaged in combat

384  while encouraging the prudent use of force commensurate with mission accomplishment and

385  self-defense.

386  z Consider how the populace might react when planning tactical situations, even for something as

387  simple as a traffic control point. Understand how people will respond to the operation.

388  z Identify and focus the COIN force on legitimate tasks for the supporting COIN force. These are

389  tasks the HN government and population generally perceive to be productive and appropriate for

390  an outside force.

391  z Win over, exhaust, divide, capture, or eliminate the senior- and mid-level insurgent leaders as

392  well as network links.

393  z Frustrate insurgency recruitment.

394  z Disrupt base areas and sanctuaries.

395  z Organize local security and support forces.

396  z Deny outside patronage (external support). Make every effort to stop the insurgency from im-

397  porting material support across international and territorial borders.

398  z When executing any form of unit sweep operation, clear only what the unit intends to hold—

399  otherwise, reconsider the operation and its goals.

400  z When Soldiers and Marines interact with the population, encourage them to treat people with re-

401  spect to avoid alienating anyone and assisting the insurgency.

402 DEVELOPMENT OF HOST-NATION SECURITY FORCES

403  5-38. Security is the key facilitating mechanism for most societal and government functions. Although

404  U.S. and multinational forces can provide direct assistance to establish and maintain security, this situation

405  is at best a provisional solution. Ultimately, the host nation must secure its own people.

406  5-39. The U.S. military, working with other government agencies and multinational partners, is able to

407  help the host nation develop the security forces required to establish and sustain stability within its borders.

408  This assistance can take many forms, to include the development, equipping, training, and employment of

409  HN security forces. The assistance may extend to operations in which multinational military units fight

410  alongside the newly formed, expanded, or reformed HN forces.

411  5-40. Though this LLO is discussed in detail in chapter 6, some ideas are worth highlighting here. In de-

412  veloping this LLO, consider the following:

     16 June 2006 (FINAL DRAFT)  FM3-24/FMFM 3-24                                                          5-11


---

     Chapter 5 (FINAL DRAFT—NOT FOR IMPLEMENTATION)

413        z Understand the security problem. The function, capabilities, and capacities required for HN se-

414        curity (military and police) forces should align with the theater strategy and the threat they face.

415        z Start at the earliest planning stage with a comprehensive approach to local needs in consultation

416        with local representatives. This process should be undertaken in partnership with the HN gov-

417        ernment and military authorities, and in consultation with multinational partners and those inter-

418        national organizations that may be involved. While the commitment of U.S. military power and

419        money may entail taking a leadership role, that role should always be partnered with local au-

420        thorities to achieve legitimacy in the eyes of the populace.

421        z Avoid mirror-imaging—trying to make HN forces look like the U.S. military. That solution fits

422        few cultures or situations.

423        z Establish separate training academies for military and police forces. Staff them with multina-

424        tional personnel. (Tap into the talents of as many nations as possible for this).

425        z Establish mobile training teams.

426        z Train the HN cadres first, and focus on identifying leaders. When trained, these key personnel

427        can establish new units, staff the training academies, and in some cases, staff mobile training

428        teams.

429        z Create general-purpose forces and special-purpose forces. These special-purpose forces should

430        be based on need and ideally should be limited. For police, this consideration could entail the

431        development of a special intelligence branch like the British have to prevent espionage and sub-

432        version as well as to provide security for ports and public figures For the HN military it could

433        mean riverine operations forces, explosive ordinance disposal, or other specialized units.

434        z Put HN personnel in charge of as much as possible as soon as possible.

435        z Conduct “joint” operations with HN forces, and show them that you respect their partnership.

436        All plans should be prepared in partnership with HN forces once those forces are ready to work

437        with the COIN force. Encourage their ownership of plans and operations as they move toward

438        self-sufficiency.

439        z In public, show appropriate respect to leaders of HN security forces. The people should know

440        that their security forces have earned the respect of the COIN force. But abuses cannot be toler-

441        ated, so that respect should depend upon generally upright comportment on the part of the HN

442        security forces.

443        z Put liaison officers with HN units. Additionally, provide unit advisors for HN units under devel-

444        opment (noting that liaison officers and military advisors serve distinctly different purposes).

445        z Establish competent military and police administrative structures early. Troops need to be provi-

446        sioned and paid in a timely manner. Pay should come from the HN organization—not through

447        the COIN force.

448        z Identify insurgents who might seek to join the security forces under false pretext. However, en-

449        courage insurgents to change sides—welcome them in with an “open-arms” policy. Vetting

450        “turncoats” is a task for the HN government in partnership with the country team.

451 ESSENTIAL SERVICES

452        5-41. The essential services LLO addresses the life support needs of HN population. The U.S. military’s

453        primary involvement is normally to provide a safe and secure environment that facilitates planning and ac-

454        tions to develop the supporting services or infrastructure needed to provide or restore essential services.

455        Due to security problems, the military may initially have the leading role. Other agencies may not be ini-

456        tially present to assume this role or might not have sufficient capability or capacity. Therefore, the U.S.

457        military must be prepared to conduct these tasks for an extended period.

458        5-42. The COIN force should work closely with the HN in establishing achievable goals. If lofty goals are

459        set and not achieved, both the COIN force and the HN government can lose the respect of the population.

460        The long-term objective for this LLO is to transition to complete HN responsibility and accountability. Es-

461        tablishing activities that the HN government will be unable to sustain on their own may be counterproduc-

462        tive. IO must be nested within this LLO to manage expectations and ensure that the public has a coherent

     5-12                               FM 3-24/FMFM 3-24                            16 June 2006 (FINAL DRAFT)


---

     Executing Counterinsurgency Operations (FINAL DRAFT—NOT FOR IMPLEMENTATION)

463  understanding of the problems requiring redress, to include infrastructure sabotage by insurgency forces.

464  The major categories follow:

465  z Transportation and public works.

466  z Public utilities.

467  z Telecommunications and public communications.

468  z Public health and sanitation.

469  z Life-support and humanitarian relief.

470  z Education.

471  5-43. Figure 5-4 (below) illustrates how a unit might apply the essential services LLO through the acro-

472  nym SWEAT-MS. Each category includes a set of missions that set the conditions for success. Those con-

473  ditions, in turn, contribute to achieving the higher commander’s desired end state.

474  Figure 5-4. Unit application of the essential services logical lines of operations

475  5-44. In developing this LLO, consider the following:

476  z Make this effort a genuine partnership between the COIN force and HN authorities. Use as

477  much local leadership, talent, and labor as soon as possible.

478  z Plan for a macro and a micro assessment effort. Acknowledge up front what is known and not

479  known about the environment—and begin an honest appraisal of what needs to be accom-

480  plished. The macro assessment concerns operational design functions and is long term in focus.

481  The micro assessment effort, by necessity, focuses on the local level and determines, with re-

482  gional sensitivity, what the actual and specific needs are in the immediate future.

483  z Appreciate local desires. A needs assessment must reflect a great deal of cultural sensitivity; oth-

484  erwise great attention (time and expense) could be wasted on something the people do not con-

485  sider to be of real value. Ask, How do I know this effort is important from a local perspective?

486  If there is no answer to that question, it may not be. HN authorities are a good place to start with

487  this question, and local polling of the populace may be useful.

488  z Make a point of establishing realistic, measurable goals, and put in place methods of assessment

489  towards the achievement of those goals.

490  z Form interagency planning teams to discuss design, assessment, and redesign. Learn early to

491  understand and use an interagency language and recognize and understand other agencies’ insti-

     16 June 2006 (FINAL DRAFT)       FM3-24/FMFM 3-24                                    5-13


---

     Chapter 5 (FINAL DRAFT—NOT FOR IMPLEMENTATION)

492                 tutional cultures. COIN activities are interagency activities—whether agencies beyond the mili-

493                 tary are initially present or not.

494        z Meet with representatives from organizations beyond the government team. Nongovernmental

495                 organizations seldom want to give the appearance of being too closely aligned with the COIN

496                 effort. Encourage their participation in planning, even if it means holding meetings in neutral ar-

497                 eas. In your meetings with these organizations, help them understand that there are mutual inter-

498                 ests in achieving the COIN force objectives of local security, stability, and relief.

499        z Be as transparent as possible with the local people. Do your best to help people understand what

500                 the COIN force is doing and the basis for decisions that affect them.

501        z Consider the role women play in the society and how this cultural factor may influence activities

502                 in this LLO.

503        z Consider that, in some situations, the local people may form an impression that the COIN force

504                 (especially the military side of it) has arrived to “save the day,” or in contrast, that their arrival

505                 will only cause greater problems. Such reactions are especially likely concerning essential ser-

506                 vices. Understanding these phenomena and working to keep expectations manageable avoids the

507                 frustrations that inevitably result from unrealized local expectations.

508 GOVERNANCE

509        5-45. This LLO relates to the capability and capacity of the HN government to secure its people, establish

510        and maintain order, and perform all necessary government activities that pertain to a legitimate, sovereign

511        nation.

512        5-46. In more rudimentary cases, where no government exists at all, this LLO may involve creating and

513        organizing a HN capability and capacity to govern. In the long run, the activities in this LLO will probably

514        affect the lives of the people the most and may lead to the elimination of the root causes for the insurgency.

515        Activities in this LLO are among the most important of all in establishing lasting stability for a region or

516        nation.

517        5-47. Good governance is normally a key requirement to achieve legitimacy for the HN government. Nec-

518        essary activities include the following:

519        z Security activities (military and police isolating the insurgency as part of public safety).

520        z Establishing and enforcing the rule of law.

521        z Public administration.

522        z Justice (judiciary system, prosecutor/defense representation, and corrections).

523        z Property records and control.

524        z Public finance.

525        z Civil information.

526        z Historical, cultural, and recreational services.

527        z Electoral process for representative government.

528        z Disaster preparedness and response.

529        5-48. In developing this LLO, consider the following:

530        z Encourage local leaders to step forward and participate. If no local council exists, encourage the

531                 populace to create such a body. Teachers, businessmen, and others who enjoy the respect of the

532                 community should be strongly encouraged to come together and form a temporary council to

533                 serve in such capacity until a more permanent organization can be elected.

534        z Assist (or encourage) the HN government to remove or reduce genuine grievances, expose

535                 imaginary ones, and resolve contradictions where possible immediately. Note that this consid-

536                 eration may be very difficult to achieve because—

537                  Genuine grievances may be hard to ascertain.

538                  Solutions might involve the HN giving up power or control in a fashion they are unable or

539                 unwilling to accommodate.

     5-14                                            FM 3-24/FMFM 3-24  16 June 2006 (FINAL DRAFT)


---

     Executing Counterinsurgency Operations (FINAL DRAFT—NOT FOR IMPLEMENTATION)

540  z Make only such promises as can be fulfilled in the foreseeable future.

541  z Help the HN develop and empower competent and responsive leaders and strengthen their civil

542  service and security forces. This is traditionally difficult to do, and backing an incompetent (or

543  worse) HN leader can backfire. Do not be afraid to step in and make a bold change where neces-

544  sary. A corrupt official, such as a chief of police who is working at the behest of both sides, can

545  be doing more harm than good. You may be forced to replace him. If so, move decisively. Ar-

546  range the removal of all officials necessary such that, although the pain of the affair may be

547  acute, it is brief and final. Wherever possible, have other HN authorities conduct the actual re-

548  moval.

549  z Provide accessibility to ensure two-way communications with the populace. Establish rapport

550  with the local public for HN government and COIN forces.

551  z Encourage the host nation to grant local demands and meet acceptable aspirations. Some of

552  these might be driving the insurgency.

553  z Emphasize the national perspective in all HN government activities, downplaying sectarian di-

554  vides.

555  z Provide liaison officers to HN government ministries or agencies. Even better, do this in an in-

556  teragency fashion, using a team approach. Obviously these proposed teams differ in composi-

557  tion, depending on function.

558  z Once the legal system is established or reestablished, send someone to observe firsthand a per-

559  son or persons moving through the legal system (arrest by police, trial, and punishment by con-

560  finement to a correctional facility). Ask to see the docket of the judges at the provincial court-

561  house. If there is no one on the docket or if it is full and there are no proceedings, there may be a

562  problem.

563  z Create a system for citizens to pursue legal redress for perceived wrongs by authorities. Rule of

564  law must include a citizen’s right and ability to petition the government for redress of wrongs

565  committed by that government—or to petition the COIN force for redress of wrongs perpetrated

566  by that force (intentionally or otherwise).

567  z Provide adequate security for the populace to resume their lives and livelihoods. This is an es-

568  sential requirement for effective governance.

569  z Whenever and wherever possible, build on extant capabilities. The host nation often has some

570  capability and supporting COIN forces may just need to help it develop greater capacity.

571 ECONOMIC DEVELOPMENT

572  5-49. The economic development LLO includes both a short-term and a long-term aspect. The short-term

573  aspect deals with immediate problems, such as large-scale unemployment and means to jump-start an econ-

574  omy from the local level through the national level. The long-term aspects of the LLOs should work to

575  stimulate an indigenous capability and capacity that produces robust and broad economic activity for the

576  HN population. Any stability a nation enjoys may be directly related to its population’s economic situation

577  and adherence to the rule of law. The basic economic health of a nation is also dependent on a govern-

578  ment’s ability to continuously secure its population.

579  5-50. Formulating a plan for economic development requires planners to understand the society, culture,

580  and the operational environment. For example, in a rural society, land ownership and the availability of ag-

581  ricultural equipment, seed, and fertilizer may be the chief components of any economic development plan.

582  In a more urban and diversified society, the availability of jobs and the infrastructure to support commer-

583  cial activities may be more important for constructive economic development. With the exception of com-

584  pletely socialist economies, governments do not create jobs except in the public bureaucracy. However, the

585  micro-economy can be positively stimulated through the advancement of small businesses. To jump-start

586  small businesses, there is a requirement for microfinance in the form of some sort of banking activities. So

587  then, work in this LLO requires attention to both the macroeconomy and the microeconomy.

588  5-51. Without a viable economy and employment opportunities, the public is likely to pursue false prom-

589  ises offered by an insurgency, which may be fostering the very conditions keeping the economy stagnant.

590  Insurgencies attempt to exploit a lack of employment/job opportunities to gain active and passive support

     16 June 2006 (FINAL DRAFT)    FM3-24/FMFM 3-24                                            5-15


---

     Chapter 5 (FINAL DRAFT—NOT FOR IMPLEMENTATION)

591        for their cause and ultimately delegitimize a government. The major categories of economic activity in-

592        clude the following:

593        z Fossil fuels, mining, and related refining infrastructure.

594        z Generation and transmission of power and energy.

595        z Transportation and movement networks.

596        z Stock and commodities exchange, and the banking industry.

597        z Manufacturing and warehousing.

598        z Building trades and services.

599        z Agriculture, food processing, fisheries, and stockyard processing.

600        z Labor relations.

601        z Education and training.

602        5-52. In developing this LLO, consider the following:

603        z To draw the most out of the local population, work with the host nation to strengthen the econ-

604        omy and quality of life. In the long run, success in COIN is about supporting the livelihoods of

605        the populace.

606        z Create an environment where business can thrive. In every state (except perhaps a completely

607        socialist one), business drives the economy. To strengthen the economy, find ways of encourag-

608        ing and supporting legitimate business activities. Even the provision of security is part of this

609        positive business environment.

610        z Work with the HN government to reduce unemployment to a manageable level.

611        z Seek to understand the impact of military operations on business activities and vice versa in the

612        AO. Understand the impact of outsourcing and military support on the local economy and the

613        level of employment. Focus on HN capability and capacity.

614        z Use economic leverage for entry into new areas and to reach new people. Remember that in

615        many societies, monies are distributed though the tribal or clan networks. For instance, making

616        sure an important clan leader gets a large contracting job may ensure that many local men are

617        employed—and therefore not as available to the insurgency. It may be necessary to pay more

618        than seems fair for a job, but this form of economic leverage is cheap if it keeps people from

619        supporting the insurgency.

620        z Ensure that noncompliance with government policies has an economic price. Likewise, show

621        early that compliance with those policies is profitable. In the broadest sense, the operational de-

622        sign should reflect that “peace pays.”

623        z Program funds for commanders to use from the beginning of any intervention for economic pro-

624        jects in their AO. No one has a better appreciation of the specific situation than those “on the

625        ground.” Creating these funds may require congressional action. (A more detailed description of

626        relevant funding sources is in appendix D.)

627 COUNTERINSURGENCY APPROACHES

628        5-53. There are many approaches to using LLOs to achieve success in a COIN effort. Each approach con-

629        sists of components that are not mutually exclusive to any particular strategy and are often shared among

630        multiple strategies. The strategies described below are not the only choices available, are neither discrete

631        nor exclusive, and may be combined depending on the environment and resources available. But the fol-

632        lowing methods and their components are offered as examples that have proven effective in the past. Mak-

633        ing any of these methods effective in the future requires adapting them to the demands of the local envi-

634        ronment. The three examples of approaches discussed are—

635        z Clear-hold-build.

636        z Combined action.

637        z Limited support.

     5-16                                   FM 3-24/FMFM 3-24                    16 June 2006 (FINAL DRAFT)


---

     Executing Counterinsurgency Operations (FINAL DRAFT—NOT FOR IMPLEMENTATION)

638 CLEAR-HOLD-BUILD

639  5-54. COIN efforts should begin by controlling key areas. Security and influence then spread out from

640  these secured areas like an oil spot. The pattern of this approach is to clear, hold, and build one village,

641  area, or city, and then to expand to other areas, reinforcing success. This approach aims to develop a long-

642  term, effective HN government framework and presence that secures the people and facilitates the meeting

643  of their basic needs, thereby reinforcing the legitimacy of the HN government. The primary tasks to ac-

644  complish clear-hold-build are—

645  z Provide continuous security for the local populace.

646  z Eliminate insurgent presence.

647  z Reinforce political primacy.

648  z Enforce the rule of law.

649  To create success that can spread, a clear-hold-build operation should not begin by assaulting the main in-

650  surgent stronghold or by focusing on areas where the population overtly supports the insurgents.

651  5-55. Clear-hold-build objectives require a considerable expenditure of resources and time. U.S. com-

652  manders and their HN counterparts should prepare for a long-term effort. All operations require a focused

653  unity of effort by civil authorities, intelligence agencies, and security forces. Coherent IO are also needed.

654  A clear-hold-build operation is executed in a specific, high-priority area experiencing overt insurgency and

655  has the following objectives:

656  z Creating a secure physical and psychological environment.

657  z Establishing firm government control of the population and the area.

658  z Gaining the support of the population as manifested by their participation in the government

659  programs to counter the insurgency.

660  5-56. Clear-hold-build operations should expand outward from a secure base, such as an urban industrial

661  complex whose population supports the government effort and where security forces are in firm control.

662  No area or its population that has been subjected to the intensive organizational efforts of a subversive in-

663  surgency organization can be won back until certain conditions are created:

664  z The commander responsible for the clear-hold-build operation has security forces clearly supe-

665  rior to the insurgent force known or suspected to be operating in the area or immediately avail-

666  able in an adjacent area.

667  z Sufficient nonmilitary resources are allocated to effectively carry out all essential improvements

668  needed to provide basic services and control the population.

669  z The insurgency is cleared from the area.

670  z The insurgency organizational infrastructure and its support have been neutralized or eliminated.

671  z A government presence is established to replace that of the insurgency, and the local population

672  willingly supports this presence.

673  5-57. The following discussion describes some examples of activities involved in a clear-hold-build ap-

674  proach. It execution involves activities across all LLOs. There can be overlap between required steps, es-

675  pecially between hold and build, where relevant activities are often conducted simultaneously.

676 Clearing the Area

677  5-58. Clear is a tactical mission task that requires the commander to remove all enemy forces and elimi-

678  nate organized resistance in an assigned area (FM 3-90). Clearing operations lead to the destruction or re-

679  treat of insurgent combatants. This task is most effectively initiated by a clear-in-zone or cordon-and-

680  search operation to disperse insurgent forces or force reaction by major insurgent elements in the area.

681  Once this action has been initiated, units employ a combination of offensive small unit operations, such as

682  area saturation patrolling and interdiction ambushes.

683  5-59. These combat operations are only the beginning, not the end state. Removal of the visible insurgent

684  forces does not remove the entrenched insurgent infrastructure. While their infrastructure exists, insurgents

685  continue to recruit among the population, attempt to undermine the HN government, and try to coerce the

     16 June 2006 (FINAL DRAFT)       FM3-24/FMFM 3-24                                                 5-17


---

     Chapter 5 (FINAL DRAFT—NOT FOR IMPLEMENTATION)

686        populace through intimidation and violence. After insurgent combatant forces have been eliminated, re-

687        moving the infrastructure begins. This should be done in a way to minimize the impact on the local popula-

688        tion and is essentially a police action that relies heavily on HN courts and legal processes.

689        5-60. If insurgent combat forces are not eliminated but instead are expelled or have broken into smaller

690        groups, they must be prevented from re-entering the area in force or re-establishing an organizational struc-

691        ture inside the area. Once COIN forces have established their support bases, security elements cannot re-

692        main static. They should be mobile and establish a constant presence throughout the area. Use of special

693        funds should be readily available for all units to pay compensation for damages that occur while clearing

694        the area of insurgent forces. Combat actions continue to maintain gains and set the stage for future activi-

695        ties by—

696        z Isolating the area to cut off external support and to catch insurgents trying to escape.

697        z Conducting periodic sweeps of the area to identify, disrupt, eliminate, and/or expel insurgents.

698        z Employing security forces and government representatives throughout the area to secure the

699                  people and facilitate follow-on stages of development.

700        5-61. Clearing operations are supplemented by IO focused particularly on two key audiences: the local

701        population and the insurgents. The purpose of the message to the population is to gain and maintain their

702        overt support for HN security forces with promises to protect the people from insurgent forces and activi-

703        ties. Conversely, the population should understand that if they actively support the insurgency, they are

704        prolonging combat operations and creating a risk to themselves and their neighbors. The purpose of the IO

705        message to the insurgents is to convince them that they cannot win against the HN efforts, and that the

706        most constructive course of action is to surrender or cease their activities.

707 Holding with Security Forces

708        5-62. Ideally this part of the clear-hold-build approach is handled by HN elements. Establishment of HN

709        security forces in bases among the population facilitates the continued disruption, identification, and elimi-

710        nation of the local insurgent leadership and infrastructure. The success or failure of the effort depends on

711        effectively and continuously securing the populace, and then on the effectiveness of re-establishing a HN

712        government presence (and operational systems) at the local level. Measured combat operations will con-

713        tinue against insurgent combatants as opportunities arise, but the main effort is focused on the population.

714        5-63. Key infrastructure must be secured. Since resources are always limited, those elements of the infra-

715        structure that are both vital for stability and vulnerable to attack should be protected. Ask in planning,

716        What elements of infrastructure are in jeopardy? For instance, a glass-making factory may be important for

717        economic recovery, but it may not be at risk of insurgent attack and therefore may not require security.

718        5-64. There are three key IO audiences during the hold stage:

719        z Populace.

720        z Insurgents.

721        z COIN force.

722        5-65. The IO message to the population should affirm that security forces supporting the HN government

723        are in the area to accomplish the following:

724        z Protect the population from so-called “revolutionary justice.”

725        z Eliminate insurgent leaders and infrastructure.

726        z Improve basic life support where possible.

727        z Reinstate HN government presence.

728        IO should also state that the security forces will remain for several years and will not leave. This message

729        of a persistent presence can be reinforced by making long-term contracts with the local populace for vari-

730        ous supply or construction requirements.

731        5-66. The IO message to the insurgents calls on them to surrender or leave the area, emphasizing the per-

732        manent nature of the government victory and presence. The HN government might try to exploit success by

     5-18                                            FM 3-24/FMFM 3-24                    16 June 2006 (FINAL DRAFT)


---

          Executing Counterinsurgency Operations (FINAL DRAFT—NOT FOR IMPLEMENTATION)

733  offering a local amnesty. Insurgent forces will most likely not surrender, but may cease hostile actions

734  against the HN government in that area.

735  5-67. The commander’s message to the COIN force should explain the changes in missions and responsi-

736  bilities that are associated with creating or reinforcing the legitimacy of the HN government. The impor-

737  tance of securing the people to protect them, gaining the people’s support by assisting them, and using

738  measured force when fighting the insurgents should be reinforced and understood.

739  5-68. Operations during this stage are designed to—

740       z Continuously secure the population to separate them from the insurgency.

741       z Establish a firm government presence and control over the area and population.

742       z Recruit, organize, equip, and train local people to provide area security.

743       z Establish a government political apparatus to replace the insurgency apparatus.

744       z Develop a dependable network of informants.

745  5-69. Major actions occurring during this stage include—

746       z Designating and allocating area-oriented security forces to continue offensive operations in the

747       area. Other security forces that participated in clearing actions are released or are assigned to

748       other tasks.

749       z Thorough population screening to identify and eliminate remaining insurgents and to identify

750       any lingering insurgent support structures.

751       z Conducting area surveys to determine resources and specific needs of the people and area. Local

752       leaders should be involved.

753       z Constructively influencing the people’s motivation through environmental improvements de-

754       signed to convince the population to support and participate in the active security of their area

755       and in the area reconstruction effort.

756       z Training of local paramilitary security forces, including arming and integrating them in success-

757       ful operations against the insurgents.

758       z Establishing a communications system integrating the area into the larger secure communica-

759       tions grid and system.

760 Building Support and Protecting the Population

761  5-70. The population in the AO must be protected from the insurgents in order to make any progress in

762  building support for the HN government. If the people are not secure or they perceive they are not, then

763  COIN efforts are greatly hindered. The HN security forces must conduct patrols and use measured force

764  against insurgent targets of opportunity on a continuous basis to ensure the protection of the people in the

765  AO.

766  5-71. This contact with the population is critical to the success of the local COIN effort. Actions designed

767  to completely eliminate the remaining covert insurgent political infrastructure must be continued, as its

768  presence will continue to threaten and influence people.

769  5-72. Special funds (or other available resources) should be available as wages to reimburse the population

770  for accomplishing applicable required tasks. Tasks that provide an overt and direct benefit for the popula-

771  tion and for which reimbursement is appropriate are key, initial priorities. These tasks can begin the proc-

772  ess of securing and reconciling the population to the HN government. Some sample tasks are—

773       z Collecting and clearing trash from the streets.

774       z Removing or painting-over insurgency symbols or colors.

775       z Building and improving roads.

776       z Digging wells.

777       z Preparing and building indigenous local security force infrastructure.

778       z Securing, moving, and distributing supplies.

779       z Providing guides, sentries, and translators.

     Chapter 5 (FINAL DRAFT—NOT FOR IMPLEMENTATION)

780 Monitoring the Population

781        5-73. Population control measures include determining who lives in the area and what they do. This proc-

782        ess requires research into family, clan, tribe, interpersonal, and professional relationships. Establishing

783        control normally begins with a census and the issuing of identification cards. A census must be advertised

784        and executed systematically. Census tasks include establishing who resides in which building and the re-

785        sponsible family head for each household. Those responsible for the household are held accountable to re-

786        port any future changes to the appropriate agencies. The collected census records provide intelligence re-

787        garding real property ownership, relationships, and business associations.

788        5-74. Insurgents may try to force the people to destroy their identification cards. The incentive for the peo-

789        ple to retain their identification cards must be sufficient. Insurgents will participate in the census to gain

790        valid identification cards themselves. Requiring applicants to bring two men from outside their family to

791        swear to their identity is a method to reduce this probability. Those who affirm the status of the identifica-

792        tion card applicant are accountable for their official statements made on behalf of the applicant. Identifica-

793        tion cards should have a code that indicates where the holders live.

794        5-75. Some other control measures are—

795        z Establishing and enforcing a curfew.

796        z Setting up a pass system administered by security forces or civil authorities and establishing lim-

797        its on the length of time people can travel.

798        z Establishing limits on the number of visitors from outside the area.

799        5-76. Once control measures are in place, the HN government should have an established system of pun-

800        ishments for various offenses. These should be announced to the population and enforced. The establish-

801        ment of this system should be initiated by the host nation to ensure the same system is enforced every-

802        where, and there is consistency with the rule of law throughout its territory. The HN government must have

803        the means to impose fines and other punishments for civil infractions of this nature.

804 Increasing Popular Support

805        5-77. COIN forces should use all available resources and capabilities to capitalize on every opportunity to

806        help the population and meet its needs and expectations. Projects to improve their economic, social, cul-

807        tural, and medical needs can begin immediately. Actions speak louder than words. Once the insurgent po-

808        litical infrastructure has been destroyed and local leaders begin to establish themselves, any necessary po-

809        litical reforms can be implemented. Other important tasks include—

810        z Establishing agencies of the HN government to carry out routine administrative functions and to

811        begin improvement programs.

812        z Providing HN government support to those willing to participate in reconstruction. Selection for

813        participation should be based on determinations of need and ability to help. People should also

814        be willing to secure what they create.

815        z Begin progressive efforts—such as participation of the population in local elections of leaders,

816        community-sponsored environmental improvement, formation of youth clubs, and other pro-

817        jects—to develop regional and national consciousness, and rapport between the population and

818        its government.

819        5-78. There are key IO audiences and messages in this stage, as well.

820        5-79. The IO message to the population has three facets.

821        z First, it should aim to gain understanding or approval for the actions of security forces that are

822        affecting the population, such as the control methods or a census. This is relatively easy to

823        achieve by telling the people what is to be done and for what purpose.

824        z The second aim is to establish human intelligence sources that lead to identification and destruc-

825        tion of any remaining insurgent infrastructure in the area.

826        z The third aim should be to win over those people who are still passive or neutral by demonstrat-

827        ing how the HN government is going to make their life better.

     5-20                               FM 3-24/FMFM 3-24                              16 June 2006 (FINAL DRAFT)
