     Executing Counterinsurgency Operations (FINAL DRAFT—NOT FOR IMPLEMENTATION)

828  5-80. The IO message to the insurgents should aim to create divisions between the leadership and the mass

829  base by emphasizing failures of the insurgency and successes of the government. Success is indicated when

830  elements of the insurgency abandon it and return to work with the government.

831  5-81. The commander should emphasize to COIN forces that they must remain friendly towards the popu-

832  lace while staying vigilant against insurgent actions. Commanders must ensure Soldiers and Marines un-

833  derstand their rules of engagement, which will become even more restrictive as peace and stability return.

834  5-82. The most important activities during the build stage are conducted by nonmilitary agencies. HN gov-

835  ernment representatives reestablish political offices and normal administrative procedures. National and in-

836  ternational development agencies rebuild infrastructure and key facilities. Local leaders are developed and

837  given authority. Life for the area’s inhabitants begins to return to normal. Activities along the combat and

838  security force LLOs become secondary to those involved in essential services, good governance, and es-

839  sential services LLOs.

840                          Clear-Hold-Build in Tal Afar, 2005-2006

841

842  In early 2005, the northern Iraqi city of Tal Afar had become a focal point for Iraqi in-

843  surgent efforts. The insurgents tried to assert control over the population. They used

844  violence and intimidation to inflame ethnic and sectarian tensions. They took control

845  of all schools and mosques, while destroying police stations. There were frequent

846  abductions and executions. The insurgents achieved at least some success as the

847  population divided into communities defined by sectarian boundaries. Additionally,

848  Tal Afar became a support base and sanctuary that was used to launch attacks in

849  the major regional city of Mosul and throughout Nineveh province.

850  During the summer of 2005, the 3d Armored Calvary Regiment (3d ACR), assumed

851  the lead for military efforts in and around Tal Afar. In the months that followed, the 3d

852  ACR applied a clear-hold-build approach to reclaim Tal Afar from the insurgents.

853  Destruction or Expulsion of Insurgency Forces (Clear)

854  In August 2005, the 3d ACR and Iraqi forces began the process of destroying the in-

855  surgency in Tal Afar. Their first step was to conduct reconnaissance to understand

856  the enemy situation, understand the ethnic, tribal, and sectarian dynamics, and set

857  the conditions for effective operations. Iraqi security forces and U.S. Soldiers isolated

858  the insurgents from external support by controlling nearby border areas and creating

859  an eight-foot-high berm around the city. The berm’s purpose was to deny the enemy

860  freedom of movement or safe haven in outlying communities. The berm prevented

861  free movement of fighters and weapons and forced all traffic to go through security

862  checkpoints that were manned by U.S. and Iraqi forces. Multinational checkpoints

863  frequently included informants who could identify insurgents. Multinational forces su-

864  pervised the movement of civilians out of contentious areas. The forces conducted

865  house-to-house searches. When significant violent resistance led to battle, combat

866  included the use of precision fires from artillery and aviation. Targets were chosen

867  through area reconnaissance operations, interaction with the local population, and in-

868  formation from U.S. and Iraqi sources. Hundreds of insurgents were killed or cap-

869  tured during the encirclement and clearing of the city. Carefully controlled application

870  of violence limited the cost to residents.

871  Deployment of Security Forces (Hold)

872  Following the defeat of enemy fighters, U.S. and Iraqi forces established security in-

873  side Tal Afar. The security forces immediately enhanced personnel screening at

874  checkpoints based on information from the local population. To enhance police le-

875  gitimacy in the eyes of the populace, the multinational forces began recruiting a more

     16 June 2006 (FINAL DRAFT)  FM3-24/FMFM 3-24                                               5-21


---

     Chapter 5 (FINAL DRAFT—NOT FOR IMPLEMENTATION)

876        diverse and representative mix of city residents and residents of surrounding com-

877        munities for the Iraqi police. The police recruits received extensive training in a police

878        academy program. U.S. forces and the Iraqi Army also trained Iraqi police in military

879        skills. Concurrent with this effort, the local and provincial government fired or prose-

880        cuted Iraqi police who were involved in offenses against the population and assigned

881        new police leaders to the city from Mosul and other locations. U.S. forces assisted to

882        ensure Iraqi Army, police, and their own forces shared common boundaries and were

883        positioned to provide mutual support to one another. At the same time, U.S. forces

884        continued to equip and train a border defense brigade that increased the capability to

885        interdict external support. Among its successes, the multinational force defeated an

886        insurgent network that included a chain of safe houses between Syria and Tal Afar.

887        Improving Living Conditions and Restoring Normalcy (Build)

888        The local population generally accepted guidance and projects to re-establish control

889        by the Iraqi government. The 3d ACR commander noted, “The people of Tal Afar un-

890        derstood that this was an operation for them—an operation to bring back security to

891        the city.”

892        With the assistance of the U.S. Agency for International Development’s Office of

893        Transition Initiatives and the State Department, efforts to re-establish municipal and

894        economic systems began in earnest. Among such initiatives were the provision of

895        essential services (water, electricity, sewage, and trash collection), education pro-

896        jects, police stations, parks, reconstruction efforts, as well as a legal claims process

897        and compensation program to address local grievances for damages.

898        As security and living conditions in Tal Afar improved, citizens began providing intel-

899        ligence that facilitated the elimination of the insurgent infrastructure. In addition to in-

900        formation received on the streets, multinational forces established joint coordination

901        centers in Tal Afar and nearby communities that became multinational command

902        posts and intelligence-sharing facilities with the Iraqi Army and the Iraqi police.

903        Unity of effort by local Iraqi leaders, Iraqi security forces, and U.S. forces was critical

904        to success. Many families who had fled the area returned to the secured city.

905 COMBINED ACTION

906        5-83. Combined action is a technique that involves joining United States and HN troops in a single organi-

907        zation, usually a platoon or company, to conduct counterinsurgency operations. This technique is appropri-

908        ate in environments where large insurgent forces do not exist or they lack resources and freedom of ma-

909        neuver. Combined action normally involves joining a U.S. rifle squad or platoon with a HN platoon or

910        company, respectively. The purpose of this approach is to hold and build while providing a persistent

911        COIN force presence among the populace. This approach attempts to first achieve security and stability in

912        a local area, followed by offensive operations nearby enemy forces now denied access or support. Not de-

913        signed for offensive operations themselves, combined action units rely on more robust combat units for this

914        task. Combined action units can also establish mutual support among villages to secure a wider area.

915                    Combined Action Program

916

917        Building on their COIN experiences in Haiti and Nicaragua, the Marine Corps imple-

918        mented an innovative program in South Vietnam in 1965 called the Combined Action

919        Program (CAP). This program paired small teams of about 15 Marines led by a non-

920        commissioned officer with approximately 20 indigenous security personnel. These

     5-22              FM 3-24/FMFM 3-24                               16 June 2006 (FINAL DRAFT)


---

     Executing Counterinsurgency Operations (FINAL DRAFT—NOT FOR IMPLEMENTATION)

921  combined action platoons operated in the hamlets and villages in the northern two

922  provinces of South Vietnam adjacent to the demilitarized zone. These CAP Marines

923  earned the trust of the villagers by living among them while helping them defend

924  themselves. Marines trained and led the local people’s defense forces, learned the

925  customs and language of the villagers, and were very successful in denying access

926  to areas under their control to the Viet Cong insurgency. CAP became a model for

927  local success in countering insurgencies. Many of the lessons learned from it were

928  copied and used in various peace enforcement and humanitarian assistance opera-

929  tions Marines conducted during the 1990s, such as Operations Provide Comfort in

930  Northern Iraq (1991) and Restore Hope in Somalia (1992-93).

931  5-84. Combined action programs can only be instituted in areas with limited insurgent activity and should

932  not be used to isolate or expel a well established and supported insurgent force. Combined action is most

933  effective after an area has been cleared of armed insurgents.

934  5-85. The following geographic and demographic factors can also influence the probability of success:

935  z Towns relatively isolated from other population centers are simpler to continuously secure.

936  z Towns and villages with a limited number of roads passing through are easier to secure than

937  towns with many routes in and out, because all these approaches must be guarded.

938  z Existing avenues of approach into a town should be observable from the town to facilitate the

939  interdiction of an insurgent force and the control of population movements.

940  z The town’s population should be small and constant, where people know one another and can

941  easily identify outsiders. In towns or small cities where a large number of inhabitants are not ac-

942  quainted with each other, conducting a census is the most effective tool to establish initial ac-

943  countability for everyone.

944  z The town and combined action/local defense force must establish mutual support with other ad-

945  jacent or nearby towns where combined action forces are operating. Larger reaction or reserve

946  forces as well as close air support, attack aviation, and air assault support should be quickly

947  available to assist each combined action unit.

948  5-86. Combined action unit members must develop and build positive relationships with their associated

949  HN security forces and with the town leadership. By living among the people, combined action units serve

950  an important purpose. They demonstrate the commitment and competence of COIN forces while sharing

951  experiences and relationships with the local people. These working relationships build trust and enhance

952  the legitimacy of the HN government. To further build trust, U.S. members should request that the HN se-

953  curity forces provide training on local customs, key terrain, possible insurgent hideouts, and relevant cul-

954  tural dynamics, as well as provide a description of recent local events.

955  5-87. While combined action units are integrated into a regional scheme of mutually supporting security

956  and influence, they should remain an organic asset of the parent unit from which they are drawn. Position-

957  ing reinforced squad-sized units (13 to 15 Soldiers or Marines) among HN citizens creates a dispersal risk

958  the parent unit can mitigate with on-call reserve and reaction forces along with mutual support from adja-

959  cent villages and towns.

960  5-88. The thorough integration of U.S. and HN combined action personnel supports the effective team-

961  work that is critical to the success of each team and the overall program. U.S. members should be drawn

962  from some of the parent unit’s best personnel. Commanders should designate potential members prior to

963  deployment to facilitate specific training and team-building to enable the success of the combined action

964  unit once it arrives in theater. Preferably, team members should have had prior experience in the host na-

965  tion. Other desirable characteristics for combined action members include—

966  z Ability to operate effectively as part of a team.

967  z Strong leadership qualities, among them—

968   Communicates clearly.

969   Shows maturity.

970   Leads by example.

 971                     Makes good decisions.
 972               z Ability to apply the commander’s intent in the absence of orders.
 973               z Possession of cultural awareness and understanding of the HN environment.
 974               z Absence of any obvious prejudices.
 975               z Mutual respect when operating with HN personnel.
 976               z Experience with the HN language or the ability to learn languages.
 977               z Patience and tolerance when dealing with language and translation barriers.

 978       5-89. Combined action units should be used in cleared and semicleared areas to work with the local peo-
 979       ple, help provide continuous security in towns, and train and conduct operations with HN security forces.
 980       Secondary missions include conducting civic action along established LLOs in support of their parent unit,
 981       gathering information for COIN forces, and providing other appropriate assistance to the HN local gov-
 982       ernment to further develop its legitimacy.

 983       5-90. Appropriate tasks include but are not limited to the following:
 984               z Assisting HN security forces in maintaining entry control points and providing reaction force
 985                    capabilities through the parent unit.
 986               z Conducting multinational, coordinated day and night patrols to secure the town and area.
 987               z Facilitating local contacts to gather information in conjunction with local HN security force rep-
 988                    resentatives. (Ensure information gathered is made available promptly and on a regular basis to
 989                    the parent unit for timely fusion and action.)
 990               z Conducting training in leadership and general military subjects so HN security forces can secure

 992               z Conducting operations with other multinational forces and HN units, if required.
 993               z Operating as a team with HN security forces to instill pride, leadership, and patriotism.
 994               z Assisting HN government representatives with civic action programs to establish an environ-
 995                    ment where the people have a stake in the future of their town and nation.
 996               z Protecting and assisting HN judicial and government representatives in establishing the rule of
 997                    law.

 998  LIMITED SUPPORT

 999       5-91. Not all COIN efforts require deployments of large combat formations. In many cases, U.S. support is
1000       limited, focused on missions like security forces advisory duty, fire support, or logistic sustainment. The
1001       purpose of the limited support approach is to focus on building HN capacity and capability. HN security
1002       forces are expected to conduct combat operations, including any clearing and holding that has to be ac-
1003       complished.

1004  ASSESSMENT OF COUNTERINSURGENCY OPERATIONS

1005                  The two best guides, which can not be readily reduced to statistics or processed through
1006                  a computer, are an improvement in intelligence voluntarily given by the population and a
1007                  decrease in the insurgents’ recruiting rate. Much can be learnt merely from the faces of
1008                  the population in villages that are subject to clear-and-hold operations, if these are vis-
1009                  ited at regular intervals. Faces which are at first resigned and apathetic, or even sullen,
1010                  six months later are full of cheerful welcoming smiles. The people know who is winning.
1011
1012                                                                                                                Sir Robert Thompson
                                                 Defeating Communist Insurgency: The Lessons of Malaya and Vietnam
1013
1014       5-92. Assessment is the continuous monitoring and evaluation of the current situation and progress of an
1015       operation (FMI 5-0.1). Commanders, assisted by the staff, continuously assess the environment, situation,
1016       and the progress of the operation, and compare it with the commander’s visualization and intent. Assess-
           ment precedes and is integrated into every operations-process activity. (See FM 3-0, chapter 6, and FMI 5-

      5-24  FM 3-24/FMFM 3-24                         16 June 2006 (FINAL DRAFT)


---

      Executing Counterinsurgency Operations (FINAL DRAFT—NOT FOR IMPLEMENTATION)

1017       0.1, chapter 3.) Assessment involves comparing projected outcomes to actual events, using conceptual
1018       tools to determine progress toward success. It entails two distinct tasks:
1019
1020               z Continuously monitoring the current situation (including the environment) and progress of the
1021                    operation.
1022
1023               z Evaluating the operation against established criteria using measurement tools and criteria.
1024       Based on their assessments, commanders adjust the operation and associated activities to better achieve the
           desired end state. (See FMI 5-0.1, paragraphs 4-12 through 4-20, and FM 6-0, paragraphs 6-90 through 6-
1025       92 and 6-110 through 6-121.)

1026  DEVELOPING MEASUREMENT CRITERIA
1027
1028       5-93. Commanders and staffs want to know why and when they are achieving success within the LLOs.
1029       Traditionally, they look for discrete quantitative and qualitative measurements to indicate success. How-
1030       ever, the complex nature of COIN makes progress difficult to measure. Subjective assessment at all levels
1031       is essential to understand the diverse and complex nature of COIN problems and to measure local success
1032       or failure against the overall operation’s purpose. Additionally, since commanders want to know how ef-
           fectively the LLO are working together, planners must evaluate not only progress along each LLO, but also
1033       how they are affecting activities along other LLOs.

1034  ASSESSMENT TOOLS
1035
1036       5-94. Assessment tools help commanders and staffs determine—
1037               z Completion of tasks and their impact.
1038               z Level of achievement of objectives.
1039               z Whether a condition of success has been developed or established.
1040               z Whether the operation’s end state has been attained.
1041               z Whether the commander’s intent was achieved.
1042
1043       For example, planning for transition of responsibility to the host nation is an integral part of COIN opera-
1044       tional design and planning because of the fundamental requirement to support development of the HN gov-
1045       ernment’s legitimacy. Assessment tools may be used to assess the geographic and administrative transfer of
           control and responsibility to the HN government as it develops its capabilities. Assessments differ for
1046       every mission, task, and LLO, and for different phases of an operation. Leaders adjust assessment methods
1047       as the insurgents adapt to COIN force tactics and the environment changes.

1048       5-95. The two most common assessment measures are measures of effectiveness (MOEs) and measures of
1049       performance (MOPs).
1050
1051       5-96. A measure of effectiveness is a criterion used to assess changes in system behavior, capability, or op-
1052       erational environment that is tied to measuring the attainment of an end state, achievement of an objective,
           or creation of an effect (FMI 5-0.1). MOEs focus on the results or consequences of actions. MOEs answer
1053       the question, Are we achieving results that move us towards our desired end state, or are additional or al-
1054       ternative actions required?
1055
           5-97. A measure of performance is a criterion to assess friendly actions that is tied to measuring task ac-
1056       complishment (FMI 5-0.1). An MOP answers the question, Was the task or action performed as the com-
1057       mander intended?
1058
           5-98. Leaders may use observable, quantifiable, objective data as well as subjective indicators to assess
1059       progress measured against expectations. A combination of both types of indicators is recommended to re-
1060       duce the chance of misconstruing trends.
1061
1062       5-99. All MOEs and MOPs for assessing COIN operations should be designed with the same characteris-
1063       tics. These four characteristics are—

                   z Measurable. MOEs and MOPs must have quantitative or qualitative standards against which
                        they can be measured. The most effective measurement would be a combination of quantitative
                        and qualitative measures to guard against an inaccurate view of results.

      16 June 2006 (FINAL DRAFT)  FM3-24/FMFM 3-24  5-25


---

      Chapter 5 (FINAL DRAFT—NOT FOR IMPLEMENTATION)

1064               z Discrete. Each MOE and MOP must measure a separate and distinct aspect of the task, purpose,
1065                    or condition.

1066               z Relevant. MOEs and MOPs must be relevant to the measured task, outcome, and condition. HN
1067                    local, regional, and national leaders and nongovernmental organization personnel may provide
1068                    practical, astute, and professional ideas and feedback to craft relevant MOPs and MOEs.

1069               z Responsive. Assessment tools must detect environmental/situational changes quickly and accu-
1070                    rately enough to facilitate the commander’s development of an effective response or counter.

1071  BROAD INDICATORS OF PROGRESS

1072       5-100. Numerical and statistical indicators have limits when measuring social environments. For example,
1073       in South Vietnam U.S. forces used the body count as an indicator of success or failure within what this
1074       chapter describes as the combat LLO. Yet, the body count indicator only communicated a small part of the
1075       information commanders needed to assess their operations and was therefore misleading. Body count can
1076       only be a partial and effective indicator when adversaries and their identities can be verified through a uni-
1077       form or possession of an insurgent political identification card. Additionally, COIN forces require the exact
1078       number of insurgent armed fighters initially present to gain an accurate appreciation of what the number of
1079       insurgent casualties might indicate in respect to enemy strength or capability. This discrete indicator fails to
1080       inform as to which side the local population blames for collateral damage, whether this fighting and resul-
1081       tant casualties damaged the insurgent infrastructure and affected the insurgency strategy in that area, and
1082       where the families of the dead insurgents reside and how they might react. For another example, within the
1083       essential services LLO the number of schools built or renovated does not equate to the effective operation
1084       of an educational system.

1085       5-101. Planners should start with broad measures of social and economic health or weakness when as-
1086       sessing environmental conditions. Here are some possible examples of useful indicators in COIN:

1087               z Refugees. Refugees are a product of the deliberate violence associated with insurgencies and
1088                    their counteraction. People and families exiled from or fleeing their homes and property and
1089                    people returning to them are measurable and revealing. The number, population, and demo-
1090                    graphics of refugee camps or the lack thereof are a resultant indicator.

1091               z Human movement and religious attendance. In societies where the culture is dominated by
1092                    religion, activities related to the predominant faith may indicate the ease of movement and con-
1093                    fidence in security, people’s use of free will and volition, and the presence of freedom of relig-
1094                    ion. Possible indicators include the following:
1095                     Flow of religious pilgrims or lack thereof.
1096                     Development and active use of places of worship.
1097                     Number of temples and churches closed by a government.

1098               z Value of the regional and/or national currency. If the currency is traded on international mar-
1099                    kets, the international perception of its stability, and by inference that of the economy as a
1100                    whole, can be evaluated by whether the currency is rising, falling, or stationary against other
1101                    currencies.

1102               z Presence and activity of small- and medium-sized businesses. When danger or insecure con-
1103                    ditions exist, these businesses close.

1104               z Level of agricultural activity. Is a region/nation self-sustaining, or must life-support-type food-
1105                    stuffs be imported? Has the annual need increased or decreased?

1106               z Presence or absence of associations. Formation and presence of multiple political parties, in-
1107                    dependent, professional associations, and trade unions.

1108               z Participation in elections, especially when an insurgency publicly threatens violence against
1109                    those who participate.

1110               z Government services available, such as—
1111                     Police stations operational and police officers present throughout the area.
1112                     Clinics and hospitals in full operation, and new whether facilities sponsored by the private
1113                    sector are open and operational.

      5-26  FM 3-24/FMFM 3-24                         16 June 2006 (FINAL DRAFT)


---

      Executing Counterinsurgency Operations (FINAL DRAFT—NOT FOR IMPLEMENTATION)

1114                     Virtually all schools and universities are open and functioning.
1115               z Industry exports.
1116               z Employment/unemployment rate.

1117  TARGETING

1118       5-102. The targeting process focuses operations and the use of limited assets and time. Commanders and
1119       staffs use the targeting process to achieve effects that support the lines of operations in a COIN campaign
1120       plan. It is important to understand that targeting is done for all operations, not just attacks against insur-
1121       gents. The targeting process can support IO, civil-military operations, and even meetings between com-
1122       manders and HN leaders, based on the commander’s desires. The targeting process occurs in the targeting
1123       cell of the appropriate command post.

1124       5-103. Targeting in a COIN environment requires the creation of a targeting board or working group at
1125       all echelons. (See FMI 5-0.1, paragraphs 2-31, 2-38, and 3-58.) The intelligence cell provides representa-
1126       tives to the targeting board/working group to synchronize targeting with intelligence sharing and ISR op-
1127       erations. The goal is to prioritize targets and determine the best means of engaging them in order to have
1128       effects on the people, host nation, and insurgency that support the commander’s intent and operation plan.

1129       5-104. The focus for targeting is on people, both insurgents and noncombatants. There are two major
1130       types of targets: key personalities and areas. Key personalities include insurgent personnel, community or
1131       political elites, and other individuals with power over the populace. Areas include segments of the popula-
1132       tion and regions under insurgent control.

1133       5-105. Personality and area targets can be further broken down into lethal targets and nonlethal targets.
1134       Lethal targets are best addressed via operations to capture or kill, while nonlethal targets are best dealt with
1135       via civil-military operations, IO, negotiation, political programs, economic programs, social programs and
1136       other noncombat methods. Nonlethal targets are usually more important than lethal targets and will never
1137       be less important.

1138       5-106. Personality targets include the following:
1139               z Lethal. Insurgent leaders to be captured or killed.
1140               z Nonlethal.
1141                     Personnel such as community leaders and insurgents, who should be engaged through out-
1142                    reach, negotiation, meetings, and other interaction.
1143                     Corrupt HN leaders who may have be replaced.

1144       5-107. Area targets include the following:
1145               z Lethal.
1146                     Insurgent bases and insurgent logistic depots or caches.
1147                     Smuggling routes that can be interdicted.
1148               z Lethal and nonlethal mix.
1149                     Populated areas where insurgents commonly operate.
1150                     Populated areas controlled by insurgents where the presence of U.S. or HN personnel pro-
1151                    viding security could undermine support to insurgents.
1152               z Nonlethal. Populations potentially receptive to civil-military operations or IO.

1153       5-108. The basic targeting process activities are similar to those described in FM 3-09.31:
1154               z Decide what targets must be addressed.
1155               z Detect the targets.
1156               z Deliver (conduct the operation).
1157               z Assess the effects of the operation.

1158       5-109. The first two activities are often switched in sequence, however. Rather than decide-detect-deliver,
1159       assess, the process may become detect-decide-deliver-assess. This is because the targets are individuals or

      16 June 2006 (FINAL DRAFT)  FM3-24/FMFM 3-24  5-27


---

      Chapter 5 (FINAL DRAFT—NOT FOR IMPLEMENTATION)

1160       groups of people rather than a fixed enemy order of battle. It is impossible to decide who/what to target
1161       without first knowing who/what the targets are.

1162  DETECT

1163       5-110. The detect activity is performed on a continuous basis. It requires a great deal of analytical work
1164       by intelligence personnel. They analyze large quantities of all-source intelligence reporting to determine
1165       the validity of threats, how important different potential targets actually are, what means should be used to
1166       engage the targets, and the expected effects of engaging the targets. This requires a detailed understanding
1167       of social networks, insurgent networks, insurgent actions, and community atmospherics. (See appendix E.)

1168       5-111. As noted earlier, attention must be paid to intelligence regarding the perceptions and interests of
1169       the populace. This intelligence is crucial to IO and civil-military operations targeting. It is also important
1170       for developing political, social, and economic programs. (See FM 3-13, appendix E, for IO targeting tech-
1171       niques.)

1172  DECIDE

1173       5-112. The decide activity is the culmination of previous intelligence work. Intelligence personnel, with
1174       the commander and other staff members, must decide when analysis is sufficiently developed to warrant
1175       engagement. Continuous staff integration and regular meetings of the intelligence cell and targeting board
1176       smooth and enable this process. Staff personnel take finished intelligence products, fuse them with their
1177       own understanding of the AO, and help the commander and operations officer make decisions about
1178       who/what to target and how. Intelligence personnel provide information on the relative importance of dif-
1179       ferent target personalities and areas, and the projected effects of lethal or nonlethal engagement.

1180       5-113. At the end of the decide activity, the targeting board has produced a prioritized list of targets and a
1181       recommended course of action associated with each. Fragmentary orders by operations personnel often
1182       come about based on the decide activity. Each of these orders is an operation that should be nested with the
1183       higher headquarters’ plan and the commander’s intent. The intelligence synchronization plan may have to
1184       change, based on targeting decisions.

1185  DELIVER

1186       5-114. The deliver activity is simply executing the missions decided upon by the commander.

1187  ASSESS

1188       5-115. The assess activity occurs continuously throughout an operation. During assessment, it is critical
1189       that collectors and analysts review the results of the operation and change intelligence synchronization
1190       plans and analyses appropriately. In addition to assessing changes to their own operations, intelligence per-
1191       sonnel look for reporting that indicates the effects on all aspects of the operational environment, to include
1192       insurgents and civilians. Relevant reporting can come from any of the intelligence disciplines, open
1193       sources, or operational reporting. Depending on the effects, commanders may choose to expand the opera-
1194       tion, maintain it as is, halt it, initiate a branch or sequel, or take steps to correct the damage of a mistake.
1195       Therefore, an accurate after-action assessment is very important. Metrics often include the following:
1196
1197               z Changes in local attitudes (friendliness towards U.S. and HN personnel).
1198               z Changes in public perceptions.
1199               z Changes in the quality or quantity of intelligence provided by individuals or groups.
1200               z Changes in the economic or political situation of an area.
1201               z Changes in insurgent patterns.
1202               z Captured/killed insurgent personnel.
                   z Captured equipment and documents.
1203
1204       5-116. As indicated in chapter 3, detainees, captured documents, and captured equipment may provide a
           great deal of additional information. Its exploitation and processing into intelligence often adds to the over-

      5-28  FM 3-24/FMFM 3-24                         16 June 2006 (FINAL DRAFT)


---

      Executing Counterinsurgency Operations (FINAL DRAFT—NOT FOR IMPLEMENTATION)

1205       all understanding of the enemy and precipitates additional targeting decisions. In addition, the assessment
1206       of the operation should be fed back to collectors. This allows them to see if their intelligence sources are
1207       credible. In addition, effective operations often cause locals to provide more intelligence, which drives fu-
1208       ture operations.

1209  LEARNING AND ADAPTATION

1210       5-117. Once the operation plan has been initiated, a commander may conduct operations to develop the
1211       situation to gain a more thorough understanding of specific elements of the environment. This increased
1212       environmental understanding represents a form of operational learning and applies across all LLOs. The
1213       commander and staff should expect to adjust the operation’s design and plan based on what they learn. The
1214       result is an ongoing design-learn-redesign cycle.

1215       5-118. COIN operations involve complex, changing relations among all the direct and peripheral partici-
1216       pants, and these participants adapt, respond, and react to each other throughout an operation. A cycle of
1217       adaptation usually develops between insurgents and the counterinsurgents in which both sides continually
1218       adapt to neutralize existing adversary advantages and develop a new (albeit usually short-lived) advantage
1219       of their own. Victory is gained through a tempo or rhythm of adaptation that is beyond the other side’s
1220       ability to achieve or sustain. Therefore, the COIN force should seek to gain and sustain advantages over in-
1221       surgents through emphasis on the learning and adaptation that is emphasized throughout this manual.

1222       5-119. This process of learning and adapting in COIN is made even more difficult because of the com-
1223       plexity of the problems to be solved. Generally, there is not a monolithic adversary that can be singularly
1224       classified as the enemy. Many insurgencies consist of multiple competing insurgency groups (more than 40
1225       groups in some historical cases such as Sri Lanka). The HN government and COIN force must adapt based
1226       on understanding this very intricate environment. But the key to effective COIN operational design and
1227       execution remains the ability to adjust better and faster than the insurgents.

1228  SUMMARY

1229       5-120. The challenge of COIN is complex, demanding, and tedious in execution. There are no simple or
1230       quick solutions. Success may often seem elusive. However, contributing to the complexity of the problem
1231       is the manner in which the environment is viewed by the COIN force and how success is defined. The spe-
1232       cific architecture of the COIN operation and the manner in which it is executed must be based on a holistic
1233       treatment of the environment and remain focused on the commander’s vision of resolution. Success will be
1234       gained from unity of effort across all LLOs to achieve goals that relate directly to the purpose for the COIN
1235       operation—to establish legitimacy for the HN government and gain popular support. Operational design
1236       and execution cannot really be separated. They are both part of the same whole.
