     Chapter 6 (FINAL DRAFT—NOT FOR IMPLEMENTATION)

770                  Multinational Security Transition Command–Iraq

771

772        The experience of Multinational Security Transition Command–Iraq (MNSTC-I) dem-

773        onstrates challenges of developing and assisting HN security forces engaged in an

774        intensive insurgency. MNSTC-I programs were built upon three pillars:

775        • Training and equipping the Iraqi Security Forces to standard.

776        • Use of transition teams to guide the development of leaders and staffs.

777        • Partnerships between U.S./multinational forces on the ground and the developing

778        Iraqi forces.

779        Initial plans called for only a small army (to deal with external threats) supplemented

780        by conventional police forces (to maintain internal law and order). As the insurgency

781        matured, the decision was made to develop a larger Iraqi Army and to focus it on the

782        internal threat. A more robust police force was also developed. Training regimens

783        also matured, becoming generally longer and more focused on counterinsurgency

784        tasks. Decisions by the Iraqi government also necessitated training and organiza-

785        tional changes. Training programs were adjusted, based on the experience of re-

786        cruits, and eventually lengthened and changed in response to the increasing lethality

787        of the insurgency and lessons learned. Advisers assigned to Iraqi units were termed

788        “transition teams” and instructed to focus on the development of leaders and staffs at

789        battalion level and above.

790        Once it became clear that the civilian infrastructure also required considerable reform

791        and resources under the auspices of the overall security development program,

792        MNSTC-I assumed the additional mission of creating ministerial-level organizations,

793        finding resources, and changing culture. Another significant challenge was the selec-

794        tion and training of competent indigenous leadership, NCOs as well as commis-

795        sioned officers. This became even more challenging as the host nation regained

796        sovereignty and asserted its independent authority, which led to a greater focus on

797        developing staffs by transition teams.

798        The MNSTC-I experience shows the advantage of doing a prompt initial assessment

799        and then adjusting as conditions change and lessons are learned. Development of

800        indigenous security forces from scratch in an active insurgency environment is often

801        more about overcoming fog and friction than about perfect planning. MNSTC-I also

802        demanded robust interagency and multinational participation in the training effort,

803        and fought for the funding necessary to make it effective. MNSTC-I also found that

804        measures of effectiveness that are easily measured, such as soldiers equipped or

805        battalions fielded, were not as useful as more complicated and more subjective met-

806        rics, such as the training level of fielded units and their loyalty to the national gov-

807        ernment.

808 POLICE IN COUNTERINSURGENCY

809        6-91. The primary front line COIN force is often the police—not the military. Few military units can

810        match a good police unit in developing an accurate human intelligence picture of their area of operations.

811        Because of their frequent contact with populace, police are often the most appropriate force to counter

812        small insurgent bands that receive support from the civilian population. In COIN operations, special police

813        strike units may be moved to different areas of operations, while patrol police remain in the local area on a

814        daily basis and build a detailed intelligence picture of the insurgent strength, organization, and support.

     6-18                              FM 3-24/FMFM 3-24                    16 June 2006 (FINAL DRAFT)


---

                Developing Host-nation Security Forces (FINAL DRAFT—NOT FOR IMPLEMENTATION)

815 ORGANIZING THE POLICE

816  6-92. The police are normally organized into several independent but mutually supporting forces. These

817  may include the following:

818  z Criminal and traffic police.

819  z Border police.

820  z Transport police for security of rail lines and public transport.

821  z Specialized paramilitary strike forces.

822  In addition, a country may establish a variety of reserve police units or home guards to provide local secu-

823  rity. The force may include paramilitary or gendarmerie-like units. Police might be organized on a national

824  or local basis. Whatever police organization is established, it is important for U.S. personnel to understand

825  the system and help the host nation organize and employ its police forces effectively. This often means

826  dealing with a variety of police organizations and developing appropriate plans for training and advising

827  each one.

828  6-93. While military forces might have to perform police duties at the start of an insurgency, in the long

829  term it is best to establish police forces to take over these duties as soon as possible. As quickly as possible,

830  the U.S., multinational partners, and the host nation should institute a comprehensive program of police

831  training. Moreover, plans for police training need to envision a several-year program to systematically

832  build institutions and leadership.

833  6-94. Although the roles of the police and military in COIN may be blurred, there are important distinc-

834  tions between the two forces. If insurgents are considered criminals, the police may retain the primary re-

835  sponsibility for their arrest, detention and prosecution

836  6-95. Combating insurgency requires a police force that is visible day and night. The host nation will not

837  gain legitimacy if the populace believes that insurgents and criminal bands control the city and village

838  streets. Well-sited and protected police stations are one means to establish a presence in communities as

839  long as the police do not hide in those stations. Police presence provides security to communities and

840  builds support for the legitimacy of the government. The police are also in daily contact with the civilian

841  community, thus serving as a key information collectors for COIN forces.

842  6-96. Good pay and attractive benefits must be combined with a strict code of conduct that follows the rule

843  of law and allows for the immediate dismissal of police personnel for corruption. Planning must ensure that

844  police pay, housing, benefits, and work conditions are good enough to attract a high quality of police re-

845  cruit, and to serve as a shield against the temptation of the petty corruption that undermines the public’s

846  confidence in the police and government. One of the most important steps in organizing a police force is to

847  set up an independent review board, with the authority to investigate charges of police abuse and corrup-

848  tion, oversee the complaints process, and dismiss and fine police found guilty of misconduct. This board

849  should be composed of experts, government officials or nongovernmental organization members. It should

850  not be under the direct command of the police force or responsible government ministry.

851 TRAINING THE POLICE IN COUNTERINSURGENCY

852  6-97. Police training is best conducted as an interagency and multinational operation. In a multinational ef-

853  fort, a separate multinational police training and advisory command could work in tandem with the military

854  training command. Ideally the leaders for police training are civilian police personnel from the Depart-

855  ments of Justice and State along with senior police officers from multinational partners. Civilian police

856  have personnel with extensive experience in large city operations. Justice Department and multinational

857  police organizations also have extensive experience operating against organized crime groups. Experience

858  countering organized crime is especially relevant to COIN, as most insurgent groups are more similar to

859  organized crime in their organizational structure and relations with the population than they are to military

860  units. U.S. military police units serve best when operating as a support force for the professional civilian

861  police trainers. However, there may be times when military forces are given the primary responsibility for

862  police training, and they must be prepared to assume that role if required. (More details on police training

863  responsibilities are discussed in appendix D.)

     16 June 2006 (FINAL DRAFT)          FM3-24/FMFM 3-24                                     6-19


---

     Chapter 6 (FINAL DRAFT—NOT FOR IMPLEMENTATION)

864        6-98. If mandated to do so, military police are capable of providing much of the initial police training and

865        are especially suited to teach the HN police forces the following skills:

866        z Weapons-handling.

867        z Small unit tactics.

868        z Special weapons employment.

869        z Convoy escort.

870        z Riot control.

871        z Traffic control.

872        z Prisoner/detainee handling and processing.

873        Higher-level police—skills such as civilian criminal investigation procedures, anti-organized crime opera-

874        tions, and police intelligence operations—are best taught by civilian experts.

875        6-99. Military police or corrections personnel can also provide training for detention and corrections op-

876        erations. HN personnel should be trained to handle and interrogate detainees and prisoners in accordance

877        with internationally recognized human rights norms. Prisoner and detainee management procedures should

878        provide for the security and the fair and efficient processing of those detained.

879        6-100. Police forces, just like military forces, need effective support personnel to be effective. This re-

880        quires training teams to ensure that training in support functions is established. Specially trained personnel

881        required by police forces include the following:

882        z Armorers.

883        z Supply specialists.

884        z Communications personnel.

885        z Administrative personnel.

886        z Vehicle mechanics.

887        6-101. Effective policing also requires an effective justice system with trained judges, prosecutors, de-

888        fense counsel, prison officials, and court personnel who can process arrests, detentions, warrants, and other

889        judicial records. These elements are important components for establishing the rule of law.

890        6-102. Advisers should assist the host nation in establishing and enforcing the roles and authority of the

891        police. The authority to detain and interrogate, procedures for detention facilities, and human rights stan-

892        dards are important considerations during this process.

893                         Developing a Police Force in Malaya

894

895        In 1948, the Malayan Communist Party began an insurgency against the British co-

896        lonial government. The first British response was to dramatically expand the security

897        forces. Between 1948 and 1950, the Malaya Police was expanded fivefold to 50,000

898        men, while the British army garrison was expanded to 40,000 men. However, there

899        was not enough time to provide anything more than a few weeks of rudimentary

900        training to the new police officers before throwing them into operations.

901        Police with little training and little competent leadership were not only ineffective in

902        conducting operations but also routinely abused the civilian population and fell into

903        corrupt practices. The population largely regarded the police as a hostile force and

904        was reluctant to provide information on the insurgents. In response, the British began

905        reforming and retraining the entire Malayan Police force. The officers who had

906        proven to be most competent in operations were pulled from the field and made in-

907        structors in new police schools. Police NCOs and officers were sent to advanced

908        courses of three-to-four months duration. A police intelligence school was estab-

909        lished, where police officers were taught the latest methods of criminal investigation

910        as well as intelligence collection and analysis. As part of the process of winning the

     6-20                                 FM 3-24/FMFM 3-24                                16 June 2006 (FINAL DRAFT)


---

     Developing Host-nation Security Forces (FINAL DRAFT—NOT FOR IMPLEMENTATION)

911  ethnic Chinese away from the insurgents, the British worked closely with ethnic Chi-

912  nese organizations to recruit Chinese for the Malaya Police.

913  Efforts to bring Chinese into the army and police were fairly successful, and both

914  forces began to be more representative of the Malayan population as a whole. With

915  thorough police and soldier training and with fully trained officers, the discipline of the

916  Malayan security forces improved dramatically. With better discipline in the police,

917  the Malayan civilian population provided more information about the insurgent or-

918  ganization and forces. Thanks to the intelligence training program, intelligence analy-

919  sis quickly improved. By 1953, the government had gained the initiative and the in-

920  surgent military forces and support structure were in a state of rapid decline. By late

921  1953, the British were able to begin withdrawing their troops and turning the war over

922  to the Malayans, who by then were fully prepared to conduct the COIN mission. The

923  British were extremely successful in developing and utilizing HN security forces to

924  defeat the Malayan insurgency.

                                FINAL DRAFT—NOT FOR IMPLEMENTATION

1                                                   Chapter 7

2   Leadership and Ethics for Counterinsurgency

3   Leaders must have a strong sense of the great responsibility of their office; the resources

4   they expend on war are human lives.

5                                                   Marine Corps Doctrinal Publication 1, 1997

6   There are leadership and ethical imperatives that are prominent and in some cases

7   unique to counterinsurgency. The dynamic and ambiguous environment of modern

8   counterinsurgency places a premium on leadership at every level, from sergeant to

9   general. Combat in counterinsurgency is frequently a small unit leader’s fight; how-

10  ever, the actions of commanders at the brigade and division levels are often more sig-

11  nificant because the senior leader sets the conditions and the tone for all actions by

12  subordinates. Today’s Soldiers and Marines are required to be competent in a broad

13  array of tasks. They must also rapidly adapt cognitively and emotionally to the per-

14  plexing challenges of counterinsurgency, and master new competencies as well as

15  new contexts. Those in leadership positions must provide the moral compass for their

16  subordinates as they navigate this complex environment. Underscoring these impera-

17  tives is the fact that exercising leadership in the midst of ambiguity requires intense,

18  discriminating professional judgment.

19 LEADERSHIP IN COUNTERINSURGENCY

20  7-1. Military leaders, as members of American society, are expected to act ethically in accordance with

21  shared national values and Constitutional principles, which are reflected in law and the military oaths of

22  service. Military leaders have the unique professional responsibility of exercising military judgment on be-

23  half of the American people they serve. They continually reconcile mission effectiveness, ethical standards,

24  and thoughtful stewardship of the nation’s precious resources—human and material—in the pursuit of na-

25  tional aims.

26  7-2. Military leaders work proactively to establish and maintain the proper ethical climate of their organi-

27  zations. They serve as visible examples for every subordinate, demonstrating cherished values and military

28  virtues in their decisions and actions. Army and Marine Corps leaders are responsible for ensuring the try-

29  ing environment of counterinsurgency does not undermine the values of their Soldiers and Marines. Under

30  all conditions, they must remain faithful to basic American, Marine Corps, and Army standards of proper

31  behavior and respect for the sanctity of life.

32  7-3. Leaders educate and train their subordinates. They create standing operating procedures and other in-

33  ternal systems to prevent violations of legal and ethical rules. They check routinely on what Soldiers and

34  Marines are doing. Effective leaders respond quickly and aggressively to indications of illegal or unethical

35  behavior. The Nation’s and profession’s values are not negotiable. Violations of them are not just mistakes;

36  they are failures in meeting the fundamental standards of the profession of arms.

37 LARGE AND SMALL UNIT LEADERSHIP TENETS

38  7-4. There are basic leadership tenets that are applicable for all levels of command and leadership in

39  counterinsurgency (COIN), though their application and importance may vary.

    16 June 2006 (FINAL DRAFT)  FM 3-24/FMFM 3-24                                                7-1


---

    Chapter 7 (FINAL DRAFT—NOT FOR IMPLEMENTATION)

40       7-5. Leaders are responsible for ensuring that Soldiers and Marines are properly trained and educated, in-

41       cluding cultural preparation for the environment in which they will operate. In a COIN environment, it is

42       often counterproductive to employ troops who are poorly trained or unfamiliar with operating in close

43       proximity to the local populace. COIN forces aim to mobilize the good will of the people against the insur-

44       gents. Therefore, the populace must feel protected, not threatened, by COIN forces’ actions and operations.

45       7-6. Proper training addresses many of the possible scenarios of the COIN environment. Education

46       should prepare Soldiers and Marines to deal with the unexpected and unknown. Senior commanders

47       should, at a minimum, ensure that their small unit leaders are inculcated with tactical cunning and mature

48       judgment. Tactical cunning is the art of employing the fundamental skills of the profession in shrewd and

49       crafty ways to out-think and out-adapt the adversaries. Developing mature judgment and cunning requires

50       a rigorous regimen of preparation that begins before deployment and continues throughout. These skills are

51       particularly important for junior leaders in COIN because of the decentralized nature of operations.

52       7-7. Senior leaders are responsible for determining the purpose of their operations. This entails, as dis-

53       cussed in chapter 4, a design process that focuses on learning about the nature of unfamiliar problems. Ef-

54       fective commanders know the people, topography, economy, history, and culture of their area of operations

55       (AO). They know every village, road, field, population group, tribal leader, and ancient grievance within it.

56       The COIN environment changes continually; good leaders appreciate that state of flux and constantly as-

57       sess their situation.

58       7-8. Another part of analyzing a COIN mission involves assuming responsibility for everyone in the AO.

59       This means that leaders feel the pulse of the local population, understand their motivations, and care about

60       what the people want and need. Genuine compassion and empathy for the populace provides an effective

61       weapon against insurgents.

62       7-9. Senior leaders exercise a leadership role for everyone within their AO. They exert a direct influence

63       on those in the chain of command, but equally important is the indirect leadership provided to everyone

64       within their AO. All elements engaged in COIN efforts look to the military for leadership. Therefore mili-

65       tary actions and words must be beyond reproach. The greatest challenge for leaders may be in setting an

66       example for the local population. Effective senior and junior leaders embrace this role and understand its

67       significance. It involves more than just killing insurgents; it includes the responsibility to serve as a moral

68       compass that extends beyond the COIN force and into the community. It is that moral compass that distin-

69       guishes Marines and Soldiers from the insurgents.

70       7-10. Senior commanders are responsible for maintaining the “moral high ground” in all deeds and words

71       of their units. Information operations complement and reinforce actions, and actions reinforce the opera-

72       tional narrative. All COIN force activity is wrapped in a blanket of truth. Maintaining credibility requires

73       immediately investigating all allegations of immoral or unethical behavior, and providing a prudent degree

74       of transparency.

75       7-11. Military leaders emphasize to subordinates that, on the battlefield, the principles of honor and moral-

76       ity are inextricably linked. They do not allow subordinates to fall victim to the enormous pressures associ-

77       ated with the strain of prolonged combat against an elusive, unethical, and indiscriminate foe. While the

78       environment that fosters insurgency is characterized by violence, immorality, distrust, and deceit, Army

79       and Marine Corps leaders continue to demand and embrace honor, courage, and commitment to the highest

80       standards. They know when to inspire and embolden their Soldiers and Marines, and when to enforce re-

81       straint and discipline. Effective leaders at all levels get out and around their units and among the people.

82       Such leaders see what they are actually doing, engage in discourse, and most importantly, listen, in order to

83       get a true sense of the complex situation in their AO.

84       7-12. Leaders at every level establish an ethical tone and climate that guards against the moral compla-

85       cency and the frustrations that build up in protracted counterinsurgency. Leaders remain aware of the emo-

86       tional toll that constant combat takes on their subordinates and the potential resulting psychoneurotic in-

87       jury. Such injuries can result from cumulative stress over a prolonged period, witnessing the death of a

88       comrade, or killing other human beings. Caring leaders recognize these pressures and provide emotional

89       “shock absorbers” for their subordinates. It is critical that Soldiers and Marines have outlets to share their

90       feelings and reach closure on traumatic experiences. These psychological burdens may be carried around

    7-2                              FM 3-24/FMFM 3-24           16 June 2006 (FINAL DRAFT)


---

     Leadership and Ethics for Counterinsurgency (FINAL DRAFT—NOT FOR IMPLEMENTATION)

91   for a long time. Leaders watch for the signs of possible psychoneurotic injury within individuals and units.

92   These include—

93   z Physical and mental fatigue.

94   z Lack of respect for human life.

95   z Loss of appetite; trouble with sleep; no interest in physical hygiene.

96   z Lack of unit cohesion and discipline.

97   z Depression and fatalism.

98   7-13. Combat requires commanders to be prepared to take some risk, especially at the tactical level.

99   Though this tenet is true for the entire spectrum of conflict, it is particularly important during COIN opera-

100  tions, where the insurgent seek to hide among the local populace.

101                              Defusing a Confrontation

102

103  A small unit of American Soldiers was walking along a street in Najaf when hundreds

104  of Iraqis poured out of the buildings on either side. Fists waving, voiced raised, they

105  pressed in on the Americans, who glanced at one another in terror. The Iraqis were

106  shrieking, frantic with rage. The source of this rage was unfathomable to the sur-

107  prised soldiers. A journalist present with the soldiers expected that a shot would

108  come from somewhere, the Americans would open fire, and the world would see the

109  “My Lai massacre” of the Iraq war. At that moment, an American officer stepped

110  through the crowd holding his rifle high over his head with the barrel pointed to the

111  ground. Against the backdrop of the seething crowd, it was a striking gesture. “Take

112  a knee,” the officer commanded. The soldiers looked at him as if he were crazy.

113  Then, one after another, swaying in their bulky body armor and gear, they knelt be-

114  fore the boiling crowd and pointed their guns at the ground. The Iraqis fell silent, and

115  their anger visibly subsided. The officer then casually ordered his Soldiers to with-

116  draw and continue on their patrol. Sometimes de-escalation, rather than generating

117  overwhelming force, is the proper action to take.

118  7-14. Leaders prepare to indirectly inflict suffering on their Soldiers and Marines by sending them into

119  harm’s way to accomplish the mission while attempting to avoid, at great length, injury and death to inno-

120  cents. This requirement gets to the very essence of what some describe as “the burden of command.” The

121  steadfast ability to regularly lose Soldiers and Marines, yet continue day-in and day-out to close with the

122  enemy, requires a resoluteness and mental toughness in commanders and units that must be developed in

123  peacetime through study and hard training, and then maintained in combat.

124  7-15. Success in COIN operations requires small unit leaders agile enough to transition among many types

125  of missions and able to adapt to change. They must be able to shift through the spectrum of conflict from

126  unstable peace to combat and back again in the course of days or hours. Alert junior leaders recognize the

127  dynamic context of a tactical situation and can apply informed judgment to achieve the commander’s intent

128  in a stressful and ambiguous environment. COIN operations are characterized by rapid changes in tactical

129  and operational environments and a high degree of ambiguity due to the presence of a civilian population

130  within which insurgents may disappear. Adaptable leaders scan the rapidly changing situation, identify its

131  key characteristics, ascertain what has to be done, and determine the most appropriate manner to accom-

132  plish the mission.

133  7-16. Cultural awareness has become an increasingly important competency for small unit leaders. Percep-

134  tive junior leaders learn how cultures affect military operations. They develop their knowledge of major

135  world cultures and learn the specifics of the new operational environment as a matter of priority when de-

136  ployed. Different solutions are required in different cultural contexts. Effective small unit leaders remain

137  aware of how their words and actions might be interpreted in different cultural settings and adapt to new

138  situations. Like all other competencies, cultural awareness requires self-awareness, self-directed learning,

139  and adaptability.

     16 June 2006 (FINAL DRAFT)      FM3-24/FMFM 3-24                                          7-3


---

     Chapter 7 (FINAL DRAFT—NOT FOR IMPLEMENTATION)

140       7-17. Self-aware leaders understand the need to continually assess their capabilities and limitations. They

141       are humble, self-confident, and brave enough to admit their faults and shortcomings. More important, self-

142       aware leaders work at improving and growing. After-action reviews, dialogues, and open discussion

143       throughout a COIN force are essential to achieve understanding and improvement. Soldiers and Marines

144       can become better, stronger leaders through a similar habit of self-examination, awareness, and focused

145       corrective effort.

146       7-18. Commanders exercise initiative as leaders and fighters. Learning and adapting with appropriate deci-

147       sion making authority are critical to gaining an advantage over insurgents. Effective senior leaders estab-

148       lish a leadership climate that promotes decentralized modes of command and control, what the Army calls

149       mission command. Under mission command, commanders create the conditions for subordinates’ success

150       by providing general guidance and the commander’s intent, assigning authority commensurate with re-

151       sponsibility to small unit leaders, and establishing control measures to monitor their actions and keep them

152       within bounds without micromanaging everything they do. At the same time, it is important that the com-

153       mander’s presence be felt throughout the AO, especially at decisive points. The operation’s purpose and

154       commander’s vision of resolution must be clearly understood throughout the force.

155       7-19. Sharing hardship and danger with subordinates builds confidence and esprit. Marines and Soldiers

156       who know their leaders are committing them to schemes of maneuver based on firsthand knowledge of the

157       point of contact are more confident in their chances of success. However, this fighter/leader concept does

158       not absolve leaders from remembering their position and avoiding needless risk.

159       7-20. COIN operations require leaders to exhibit patience, persistence, and presence. Successful com-

160       manders lead Soldiers and Marines, cooperate with the entire COIN force, leverage the capabilities of mul-

161       tinational partners and nongovernmental organizations, and gain the confidence of the local population,

162       while at the same time defeating and discrediting their adversary.

163                           Patience, Presence, and Courage

164

165       The Marine platoon walked the streets in Iraq on foot, passing out candy, chocolates.

166       and the occasional soccer ball to waving children. Their patrols weaved fearlessly

167       around lines of cars and through packed markets. For the most part, their house calls

168       began with knocks, not kicks. It was their strategy to win the respect of the city’s

169       Sunni population.

170       Until Monday. A night patrol had hit an improvised explosive device (IED) while pa-

171       trolling in an armored Humvee. Five Marines were wounded initially, and two died

172       shortly thereafter. A third Marine, a popular noncommissioned officer (NCO), subse-

173       quently died of his wounds. The bomb had completely destroyed the vehicle.

174       The platoon was stunned. Some of the more veteran NCOs shrugged it off, but the

175       younger Marines were keyed up, and wanted to make the elusive enemy pay a price.

176       A squad leader, stood up in the squad bay and said, “When we go back out there

177       tomorrow, I can guarantee you one thing, there will be a pile of dead Hajis out there.

178       Take that to the bank.”

179       Just then, the company commander walked in. He was widely respected within the

180       command and generally short on words. He quickly sensed the unit’s mood and rec-

181       ognized the potential danger of their dark attitude. “You have to remember why we

182       are here,” he said, “a very small percentage of this country’s people are out to create

183       problems. They benefit from creating chaos, and they benefit from anything we do

184       that detracts from our honor or our purpose here. They would love to get an overre-

185       action to that IED last night. I know we lost some good people last night, and I know it

186       can cause emotions to run high. Don’t let that happen, don’t get caught up in the an-

187       ger of the moment and do something all of us will regret for a long time. When we go

188       back out tomorrow, we all have to focus on what we’re trying to accomplish here, and

189       keep our mind on the mission. I know we’ve taken some hits, but escalating the vio-

     7-4                           FM 3-24/FMFM 3-24                          16 June 2006 (FINAL DRAFT)


---

            Leadership and Ethics for Counterinsurgency (FINAL DRAFT—NOT FOR IMPLEMENTATION)

190  lence is not the way to win, it’s falling for their strategy, instead of our game plan. You

191  don’t want to do something that you will regret forever or that will keep us here

192  longer.”

193 ETHICS

194  7-21. Article VI of the U.S. Constitution and the Army Values, Soldier’s Creed, and Core Values of U.S.

195  Marines all require obedience to the law of armed conflict. They hold Soldiers and Marines to the highest

196  standards of moral and ethical conduct. Conflict brings to bear enormous moral challenges, as well as the

197  burden of life and death decisions with profound ethical considerations. Combat, including COIN and

198  other forms of irregular warfare, often obligates Soldiers and Marines to choose the riskier course of action

199  to minimize harm to noncombatants. This risk-taking is an essential part of the Warrior Ethos. In conven-

200  tional conflicts, balancing competing responsibilities of mission accomplishment at least friendly cost with

201  protection of noncombatants is difficult enough. Complex COIN operations place even tougher ethical de-

202  mands on Marines, Soldiers, and their leaders.

203  7-22. Even in conventional operations, Soldiers and Marines are not permitted to use force disproportion-

204  ately or indiscriminately. Typically, more force reduces risk. But the American military values obligate

205  Soldiers and Marines to accomplish their missions while taking measures to limit the destruction, particu-

206  larly in terms of harm to noncombatants, caused by military operations. This restriction is based on the be-

207  lief that it is wrong to harm innocents, regardless of their citizenship.

208  7-23. Limiting the misery caused by war requires combatants to consider certain rules, principles, and con-

209  sequences that restrain the amount of force they may apply. At the same time, combatants are not required

210  to take so much risk that they fail their mission or forfeit their lives. As long as their use of force is propor-

211  tional to the gain to be achieved and discriminate in distinguishing between combatants and noncombat-

212  ants, Soldiers and Marines may take actions where they knowingly risk, but do not intend, harm to non-

213  combatants.

214  7-24. Ethically speaking, COIN environments can be much more complex than conventional ones. Insur-

215  gency is more than combat between armed groups; it is a political struggle with a high level of violence in-

216  tended to destabilize and ultimately overthrow a government. COIN forces using excessive force to limit

217  short-term risk alienate local residents. In doing this, they deprive themselves of the support or tolerance of

218  the populace. This situation is what the insurgents want. It increases the threat they pose. Sometimes lethal

219  responses are counterproductive. At other times, they are essential. The art of command includes knowing

220  the difference and directing the appropriate action.

221  7-25. A key part of any insurgent’s strategy is to attack their domestic and international opposition’s po-

222  litical will. One of the insurgents’ most effective means to undermine and erode political will is to portray

223  their opposition as untrustworthy or illegitimate. These attacks are especially effective when insurgents can

224  portray the opposition as unethical by their own standards. To combat these efforts, Soldiers and Marines

225  treat noncombatants and detainees humanely and in accordance with America’s values and internationally

226  recognized human rights standards. In COIN, preserving noncombatant lives and dignity is central to mis-

227  sion accomplishment. This imperative creates a complex ethical environment that requires combatants to

228  treat prohibitions against harming noncombatants as absolute. Further, it can sometimes require combatants

229  to forego lethal solutions altogether. In practical terms, this consideration means that mission accomplish-

230  ment sometimes obligates combatants to act more like police than warriors. That requirement imposes a

231  very different calculus for the use of force.

232 WARFIGHTING VERSUS POLICING

233  7-26. In counterinsurgencies, warfighting and policing are dynamically linked. The moral purpose of com-

234  bat operations is to secure peace. The moral purpose of policing is to maintain peace. In COIN operations,

235  military forces defeat enemies to establish civil security; then, having done so, these same forces preserve it

236  until indigenous police forces can assume responsibility for maintaining the civil order. When combatants

237  conduct stability operations in a way that undermines civil security, they undermine the moral and practical

     16 June 2006 (FINAL DRAFT)  FM3-24/FMFM 3-24                                                 7-5


---

     Chapter 7 (FINAL DRAFT—NOT FOR IMPLEMENTATION)

238       purposes they serve. There is a clear difference between warfighting and policing. COIN operations require

239       that every unit be adept at both and capable of moving rapidly between one and the other, depending on

240       circumstances.

241       7-27. In COIN operations, the environment frequently and rapidly shifts from warfighting to policing and

242       back again. There are numerous examples from both Iraq and Afghanistan where U.S. forces drove insur-

243       gents out of urban areas but, because of the difficulty of maintaining civil security afterwards, the insur-

244       gents later returned and reestablished operations. The insurgents had to be dealt with as an organized com-

245       batant force all over again. To prevent this situation from occurring, forces that establish civil security need

246       to be prepared to maintain it. And maintaining civil security entails very different ethical obligations than

247       establishing it.

248       7-28. Civil security holds when institutions, civil law, courts, prisons, and effective police are in place and

249       can protect the recognized rights of individuals. Typically this requires that—

250       z The enemy is defeated or transformed into a threat not capable of challenging a government’s

251       sovereignty.

252       z Institutions necessary for law enforcement, including police, courts, and prisons, are function-

253       ing.

254       z These institutions are credible, and people trust them to resolve disputes.

255       7-29. Where a functioning civil authority does not exist, COIN forces are obligated to work to establish it.

256       Where U.S. forces are trying to build a host-nation (HN) government, the interim military government

257       should transition to HN authority as soon as possible. Accomplishing this requires COIN forces to work

258       within the framework of the institutions established to maintain order and security. In these conditions,

259       COIN operations more closely resemble police work than combat operations.

260       7-30. The most salient difference between warfighting and policing is the moral permissibility of noncom-

261       batant and bystander casualties. In warfighting, noncombatant casualties are permitted as long as combat-

262       ants observe the restrictions of proportionality and discrimination. In policing, bystanders may not be

263       harmed intentionally under any circumstances. Failure to observe this rule undermines the peace—often

264       tenuous in these circumstances—that military action has achieved. In maintaining the peace, police are per-

265       mitted to use only the least force possible to achieve the immediate goal.

266 PROPORTIONALITY AND DISCRIMINATION

267       7-31. Because of the importance of gaining popular support and establishing legitimacy for the HN gov-

268       ernment, practicing proportionality and discrimination during COIN is an operational necessity as well as a

269       legal and moral one. Proportionality and discrimination require combatants not only to minimize the harm

270       to noncombatants, but also to make positive commitments to—

271       z Preserve noncombatant lives by limiting the damage they do.

272       z Assume additional risk to minimize potential harm.

273       7-32. Proportionality requires that the advantage gained by a military operation not be exceeded by the

274       collateral harm. Combatants must take all feasible precautions in the choice of means and methods of at-

275       tack to avoid and minimize loss of civilian life, injury to civilians, and damage to civilian objects.

276       7-33. In conventional operations, proportionality is usually calculated in simple utilitarian terms: civilian

277       lives and property lost versus enemy destroyed and military advantage gained. But in COIN operations,

278       advantage is best calculated not in terms of how many insurgents are killed or detained, but rather which

279       enemies are killed or detained. If certain key insurgent leaders are essential to the insurgents’ ability to

280       conduct operations, then military leaders need to consider their relative importance when determining how

281       best to pursue them. In COIN environments, the number of civilian lives lost and property destroyed needs

282       to be measured against how much harm the targeted insurgent could do if allowed to escape. If the target in

283       question is relatively inconsequential, then proportionality requires combatants to forego severe action, or

284       seek noncombative means of engagement.

285       7-34. Further, when conditions of civil security exist, COIN forces are prohibited from taking any actions

286       in which noncombatants will knowingly be harmed. However, this does not mean COIN forces are prohib-

     7-6                    FM 3-24/FMFM 3-24                                         16 June 2006 (FINAL DRAFT)


---

     Leadership and Ethics for Counterinsurgency (FINAL DRAFT—NOT FOR IMPLEMENTATION)

287  ited from taking risks that might place civilian lives in danger. But those risks are subject to the same con-

288  siderations of proportionality. The benefit anticipated must outweigh the risk taken.

289  7-35. Discrimination requires combatants to differentiate between enemy combatants who represent a

290  threat and noncombatants who do not. In conventional operations, this restriction means that combatants

291  cannot intend to harm noncombatants, though proportionality permits them to act knowing some noncom-

292  batants may be harmed. In policing situations, combatants cannot act in any way in which they know by-

293  standers may be harmed.

294  7-36. In insurgencies, it is not only difficult to distinguish combatant from noncombatant; it is also diffi-

295  cult to determine whether the situation permits harm to noncombatants. Two levels of discrimination are

296  necessary:

297  z Deciding between targets.

298  z Determining an acceptable risk to noncombatants and bystanders.

299  7-37. Discrimination also applies to the means by which combatants engage the enemy. The law and mo-

300  rality of war prohibit the use of certain weapons, such as chemical munitions, soft lead bullets, and others

301  against combatants. (See FM 27-10.) In COIN environments, combatants must not only discriminate

302  among the kinds of weapons used but also whether lethal means are permitted in the first place. Where

303  civil security exists, even tenuously, COIN forces are obligated to pursue noncombat and nonlethal means

304  first, using lethal force only when necessary.

305 DETENTION AND INTERROGATION

306  7-38. Detentions and interrogations are critical components to any military operation. But the nature of

307  COIN operations produces the potential for complex detainee situations due to insurgents’ deliberate min-

308  gling with the civilian population and lack of distinctive uniforms. Interrogators are often under extreme

309  pressure to get information that can save the lives of noncombatants, Soldiers, and Marines. While enemy

310  prisoners in conventional war are considered moral and legal equals, the moral and legal status of insur-

311  gents is ambiguous and often contested. What is not ambiguous is the legal obligation of Marines and Sol-

312  diers to treat all prisoners and detainees according to the law. (Appendix D provides more guidance on the

313  legal issues concerning detention and interrogation.)

314 LIMITS ON DETENTION

315  7-39. Mistreatment of noncombatants, including prisoners and detainees is illegal and immoral. It will not

316  be condoned. The Detainee Treatment Act of 2005 makes the standard clear:

317  No person in the custody or under the effective control of the Department of Defense or

318  under detention in a Department of Defense facility shall be subject to any treatment or

319  technique of interrogation not authorized by and listed in the United States Army Field

320  Manual on Intelligence Interrogation.

321  No individual in the custody or under the physical control of the United States Govern-

322  ment, regardless of nationality or physical location, shall be subject to cruel, inhuman,

323  or degrading treatment or punishment.

324  §§ 1002(a); 1003 (a), Pub. L. No. 109-148, enacted at Title X of the Defense Appropriation

325                               Act, H.R. 2863, 109th Cong., 1st Sess. (Dec. 30, 2005).

326  7-40. In COIN environments, distinguishing an insurgent from a civilian is difficult and often impossible.

327  Treating the second like the first, however, is a sure recipe for failure. Individuals suspected of insurgent or

328  terrorist activity may be detained for two reasons:

329  z To prevent them from conducting further attacks.

330  z To gather information in order to prevent other insurgents and terrorists from conducting at-

331              tacks.

332  These reasons allow for two classes of persons to be detained and interrogated:

333  z Persons who have engaged in, or assisted those who engage in, terrorist or insurgent activities.

     16 June 2006 (FINAL DRAFT)   FM3-24/FMFM 3-24                                               7-7


---

     Chapter 7 (FINAL DRAFT—NOT FOR IMPLEMENTATION)

334              z Persons who have incidentally attained knowledge regarding insurgent and terrorist activity, but

335              who are not guilty of associating with such groups.

336       By engaging in such activities, persons in the first category may be detained as criminals or enemies, de-

337       pending on the context. Persons in the second category may be detained and questioned for specific infor-

338       mation. However, since they have not, by virtue of their activities, represented a threat, they may be de-

339       tained only long enough to obtain the relevant information. Since persons in the second category have not

340       engaged in criminal or insurgent activities, they must be released even if they refuse to provide informa-

341       tion.

342       7-41. At no time is it permissible to detain family members or close associates in order to compel sus-

343       pected insurgents to surrender. This kind of hostage taking is both unethical and illegal.

344 LIMITS ON INTERROGATION

345       7-42. Abuse of detained persons is immoral, illegal, and unprofessional. Those who engage in cruel or in-

346       human treatment of prisoners betray the standards of the profession of arms and the laws of the United

347       States. They are subject to punishment under the Uniform Code of Military Justice. The Geneva Conven-

348       tions as well as the Convention against Torture and Other Cruel, Inhuman or Degrading Treatment or Pun-

349       ishment agree on what is unacceptable for interrogation. Torture and cruel, inhumane, and degrading treat-

350       ment is never a morally permissible option, even in situations where lives depend on gaining information.

351       No exceptional circumstances permit the use of torture and other cruel, inhuman or degrading treatment.

352              Lose Moral Legitimacy, Lose the War

353

354       During Algerian war of independence between 1954 and 1962, French leaders de-

355       cided to permit torture against suspected insurgents. Though they were aware that it

356       was against the law and morality of war, they argued that (1) this was a new form of

357       war and these rules did not apply; (2) the threat the enemy represented, commu-

358       nism, was a great evil that justified extraordinary means; and (3) the application of

359       torture against insurgents was measured and nongratuitous.

360       This official condoning of torture on the part of French Army leadership empowered

361       the moral legitimacy of the opposition, undermined the French moral legitimacy, and

362       caused internal fragmentation among serving officers that led to an unsuccessful

363       coup attempt in 1962. In the end, failure to comply with moral and legal restrictions

364       against torture severely undermined French efforts and contributed to their loss de-

365       spite a number of significant military victories. Illegal and immoral activities made the

366       counterinsurgents extremely vulnerable to enemy propaganda inside Algeria among

367       the Muslim population, as well as in the United Nations and the French media. These

368       actions also degraded the ethical climate throughout the French Army. France even-

369       tually recognized Algerian independence in July, 1963.

370       7-43. The ethical challenges posed in COIN require commanders’ attention and action. Proactive com-

371       manders establish procedures and checks to ensure subordinate leaders do not allow apparent requirements

372       of the moment to result in violations of proper detainee-handling procedures. Prohibitions against mis-

373       treatment may sometimes clash with leaders’ moral imperative to accomplish their mission with minimum

374       losses. Such situations place leaders in the difficult situation where they must choose between obedience to

375       the law and the lives of their troops. However, American law and professional values compel commanders

376       to forbid mistreatment of noncombatants in their hands, including captured enemies. It is essential that sen-

377       ior commanders make the limits of acceptable behavior clear to their subordinates and take positive meas-

378       ures to ensure their standards are met.

379       7-44. To the extent that the work of interrogators is indispensable to fulfilling the state’s obligation to se-

380       cure its citizens’ the lives and liberties, conducting interrogations is a moral obligation. The methods used,

381       however, must reflect the Nation’s commitment to human dignity and international humanitarian law. A

     7-8                                           FM 3-24/FMFM 3-24  16 June 2006 (FINAL DRAFT)


---

     Leadership and Ethics for Counterinsurgency (FINAL DRAFT—NOT FOR IMPLEMENTATION)

382  commander’s need for information remains valid and can be met while observing relevant regulations and

383  ethical standards. Acting morally does not necessarily mean that leaders give up obtaining critical informa-

384  tion. Acting morally does require that leaders relinquish certain ways of obtaining information, even if that

385  means that members of the military and intelligence professions must take greater risk.

386 THE LEARNING IMPERATIVE

387  7-45. Today’s operational environment requires military organizations at all echelons to prepare for a

388  broader range of missions than ever before. The Services are preparing for preventative stability operations

389  or postconflict reconstruction tasks with the same degree of professionalism and study given to the conduct

390  of combat operations. Similarly, COIN operations are receiving the attention and study merited by their

391  frequency and potential impact. This broader mission set implies significant leadership development, edu-

392  cation, and training implications, especially for land forces. The next chapter highlights how COIN re-

393  quirements affect logistic operations.

394  7-46. Army and Marine leaders need to visualize the operational impact of many tactical actions and relate

395  their operations to larger strategic purposes. Effectively blending traditional military operations with other

396  forms of influence is necessary. Effective leaders are placing a stronger emphasis on organizational

397  change, developing subordinates, and empowering them to execute critical tasks in consonance with broad

398  guidance. Leaders are required to directly and indirectly influence the behavior of others outside their chain

399  of command. Finally, leaders are increasingly responsible for creating environments in which individuals

400  and organizations learn from their experiences and for establishing climates that tap the full ingenuity of

401  subordinates. Open channels of discussion and debate are needed to encourage growth of a learning envi-

402  ronment in which experience is rapidly shared and lessons adapted for new challenges. The velocity of or-

403  ganizational adaptation must outpace the enemy’s efforts to identify and exploit weaknesses or develop

404  countermeasures. (For more on the importance and application of learning in counterinsurgency, see Ap-

405  pendix G.)

406  7-47. Effective individual professional development programs develop and reward initiative and adaptabil-

407  ity in junior leaders. Self-development, life-long learning, and reflection on experience should be encour-

408  aged and rewarded. Cultural sensitivity, development of nonauthoritarian interpersonal skills, and foreign

409  language ability must be mastered. Institutional professional development programs must develop leaders’

410  judgment to help them recognize when situations change from combat to policing. Leaders must be as

411  skilled at limiting lethal force as they are in concentrating it. Indeed, they must learn that nonlethal solu-

412  tions may often be preferable to “breaking things and killing people.”

413 SUMMARY

414  7-48. Senior leaders must model and transmit to their subordinates appropriate respect for professional

415  standards of self-discipline and adherence to ethical values. Effective leaders create command climates that

416  reward professional conduct and penalize unethical behavior. They also are comfortable with delegating

417  authority. However, as always, accountability for the overall behavior and performance of a command can-

418  not be delegated. Commanders remain accountable for the attainment of objectives and the manner in

419  which they are attained.

420

421

1                                         Chapter 8

2   Building and Sustaining Capability and Capacity

3   …the object [is to]…restore government authority in an area and establish a firm secu-

4   rity framework.…“Winning” the population can tritely be summed up as good govern-

5   ment in all its aspects.…More desirable than outright gifts are schemes which are self-

6   perpetuating or encourage a chain reaction. For example, building plans should stimu-

7   late the production of local building material.…All this helps to give the impression not

8   only that the government is operating for the benefit of the people, but that it is carrying

9   out programs of a permanent nature…It gives people a stake in stability and hope for the

10  future….

11                                                                  Sir Robert Thompson

12            Defeating Communist Insurgency: The Lessons of Malaya and Vietnam, 1966

13  This chapter begins with a general discussion and analysis of how logistics in coun-

14  terinsurgency (COIN) operations are different from logistics in conventional opera-

15  tions. This is followed by a survey of COIN-specific factors that affect how com-

16  manders can leverage available logistic assets and assign logisticians to meet special

17  requirements needed to support different COIN logical lines of operations. The dis-

18  cussions that follow acknowledge that COIN operations may be entered into from a

19  variety of military conditions ranging from unstable peace to general war. Further-

20  more, due to the long-term commitment usually required for successful COIN, logis-

21  tic units need to become more adept at relief-in-place operations that maintain conti-

22  nuity of progress between rotations.

23 LOGISTIC CONSIDERATIONS IN Counterinsurgency OPERATIONS

24  8-1. In counterinsurgency (COIN) operations, logistic units are asked to not only support combat efforts

25  as they always have, but also to provide decisive or shaping support as well. Logistic providers are now no

26  longer the tail but the nose of a counterinsurgent force. Among the most valuable services that military lo-

27  gisticians can provide to COIN operations are the means and knowledge required for setting up or restart-

28  ing self-perpetuating sustainment schemes that give the people a stake in stability and hope for the future.

29  One of the paradoxes of counterinsurgency is that many of the logisticians’ best weapons for countering an

30  insurgency do not shoot. In this respect, logistic units can provide some of the most versatile and effective

31  nonlethal resources available to U.S. military forces. Logisticians supporting COIN operations prepare to

32  provide support across all logical lines of operations (LLOs) visualized and articulated by the commander.

33  In many cases, logisticians already supporting COIN combat operations may be the only available source

34  able to provide essential knowledge, capabilities, and materials in the most timely manner. While this chap-

35  ter focuses heavily on the capabilities and responsibilities of logistic units and logisticians, commanders of

36  all types of units at all levels should also be aware of the distinct characteristics of COIN support.

37 WHAT IS DIFFERENT ABOUT LOGISTICS IN COIN OPERATIONS

38  8-2. In COIN operations, logistic units and other logistic providers find themselves performing all the

39  functions they normally do in conventional operations, as well as some new ones. Conventional operations

40  usually involve two recognizable military-like organizations engaged in force-on-force operations in con-

41  tiguous areas of operations. What is different in COIN operations is that all the usual logistic functions, as

42  well as the COIN-specific new activities, must now all be performed in a frequently disorienting environ-

    16 June 2006 (FINAL DRAFT)  FM 3-24/FMFM 3-24                                                           8-1


---

    Chapter 8 (FINAL DRAFT—NOT FOR IMPLEMENTATION)

43       ment in which security conditions can rapidly change from moment to moment and every few hundred

44       yards over a wide variety of terrain conditions.
